The rules that govern those actions, such as "obtain the customer's explicit confirmation before changing anything", are usually stated in the prompt and followed only as reliably as the model chooses to follow them. We present Concordat, a Python library that treats an agent as an object whose interface is a finite-state protocol, in the spirit of typestate and session types, and enforces that protocol on the model at run time. On every turn the model is offered only the tools that are legal in the current protocol state, calls outside that set are rejected before they have side effects, decisions reserved for humans can only be requested, and token and tool-call budgets are charged and split linearly across handoffs. Protocols are checked when they are defined, and a subclass of an agent is rejected if it could bypass its parent's protocol, a behavioural form of Liskov substitution. We evaluate a Concordat agent against the standard agent of tau-bench on its 50 airline tasks, adding simulated customers who pressure the agent to skip confirmation. The standard agent made changes without the required "yes" in 13 of 50 tasks with gpt-4.1 and in 29 of 128 task-runs with Claude Haiku 5.5; the Concordat agent made none, with no significant difference in task success. tau-bench's own reward did not detect most of these violations. Concordat is open source.
  </details>

- **[MuSID-KD: Multigranularity Knowledge Distillation for Generative Recommendation with Semantic IDs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35218)**  `arXiv:2610.35218`  
  _Fengshi Zhang, Xiyan Ding, Shuxiao Xie, Wei Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative recommendation with semantic IDs (SIDs) represents each item as a sequence of discrete tokens and autoregressively predicts the next item's SID. Despite strong recommendation performance, repeated decoding over multiple SID positions incurs substantial inference cost. Knowledge distillation transfers knowledge from a powerful generative teacher to a lightweight student, yet conventional objectives do not fully account for multi-token SID generation. We identify two limitations: item-level distribution alignment may not adequately preserve the teacher's ranking of candidate groups sharing SID prefixes, while uniform alignment across SID positions may affect final item ranking differently. We propose MuSID-KD, which combines Hierarchical Candidate Distribution Alignment (HCDA) and Ranking Impact Guided Levelwise Distillation (RIGD). HCDA aggregates item probabilities according to shared SID prefixes and aligns candidate distributions at successive levels, from candidate groups to individual items. RIGD evaluates the reduction in pairwise ranking surrogate loss when one student score contribution is replaced with the teacher's contribution, and allocates supervision accordingly. Experiments on real-world recommendation datasets using three lightweight student architectures demonstrate improved recommendation accuracy and better alignment of prefix marginal distributions and pairwise item rankings, without modifying the student's online inference architecture. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Logic Hijacking of RAG: Single-Document Poisoning via Local Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34829)**  `arXiv:2610.34829`  
  _Hangyi Zhao, Bonan Zhang, Fusen Guo, Jiaheng Wei, Chao Chen, Tianqing Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-document corpus poisoning under black-box access poses a severe threat to Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). However, existing attacks predominantly rely on explicit factual refutations or imperative instruction overrides. Modern aligned LLMs often arbitrate such conflicts conservatively, rejecting overt contradictions in favor of parametric memory or clean retrieved context while triggering input guardrails. We propose MONOLOGUE, a single-document poisoning framework based on Logic Hijacking without explicit refutation. Rather than disputing the ground truth, our method concedes it as an authoritative historical baseline before introducing the update, weaving a grounded record-correction narrative (e.g., dated institutional reattributions) that presents the counterfactual target as the most recent update. To prevent suspicious live writes, payloads are refined entirely offline via local multi-dimensional gated optimization, coupling automated text quality audits with surrogate behavioral verification. Evaluations across three benchmarks, three retrieval backends, and four open-weight generator regimes show that our method achieves the strongest overall attack efficacy among the evaluated single-document poisoning attacks.
  </details>

- **[LegoSR: A Unified Framework for Self-improving Agent-Guided Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33465)**  `arXiv:2610.33465`  
  _Hao Mao, Xu T. Liu, Peng Zhao, Shuai Lu, Yuntian Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Equation discovery solvers often use different data interfaces, equation representations and evaluation criteria, making their results hard to compare. We present LegoSR, a unified framework for algebraic symbolic regression and differential equation discovery. LegoSR integrates seven solvers through a common equation representation and evaluation process, and supports searches constrained by partially known equations. Researchers can run solvers directly or use LegoSR Agent, which selects solvers and adapts searches using solver knowledge, intermediate results and lessons from past tasks. On noisy ordinary and partial differential equation benchmarks, LegoSR Agent recovers nearly as many equations as the union of seven expert-tuned solvers and outperforms a generic agent with the same model, tools and budget by up to 29 percentage points. Three examples of increasing difficulty show the agent's role growing from running a solver to choosing one and then to building a partially known equation from failed searches.
  </details>

- **[AgentOrch: How Close Can Global Orchestration Come to Step-by-Step Interaction?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33375)**  `arXiv:2610.33375`  
  _Zijie Liu, Qi Yang Huang, Jinhao Duan, Yueh Che Lee, Thomas A. Joseph, Zayne Turner, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprise workflow agents typically rely on step-by-step interaction (Step-by-Step): the agent executes an action, observes its result, and invokes a large language model (LLM) again to decide the next step. Global orchestration (Global) instead generates a complete executable workflow in advance and executes it without further LLM calls, substantially reducing inference overhead, but it remains less accurate than Step-by-Step. We study how close Global can come to Step-by-Step and what narrows the remaining gap. We introduce AgentOrch, a benchmark that directly compares global orchestration with step-by-step interaction on 650 enterprise SOPs and 3,220 runtime variants under matched tools, environments, and evaluation. Step-by-Step remains more accurate for every evaluated model, but the gap has shrunk by about 79% from early models to the strongest recent model. We identify three routes to further narrowing this gap. Incremental workflow construction improves Global accuracy by an average of 9.98 percentage points (pp) without runtime execution feedback. Revising complete workflows with execution feedback improves all evaluated models, including 6.13 pp on held-out runtime variants. Targeted training on complete workflows improves accuracy by an average of 12.18 pp across all five trained models. Although a gap remains, global orchestration offers a promising path toward enterprise agents that deliver higher accuracy with fewer LLM calls.
  </details>

- **[Reject, Remember, Retry: Why Multi-Turn Attackers Should Remember Their Mistakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31937)**  `arXiv:2610.31937`  
  _Jekaterina Novikova, Yuqing Huang, Jack Ji, Sagarika Suresh, Jithin Pradeep, Lovedeep Gondara_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing multi-turn jailbreak attackers condition on the same conversation history as the target. We introduce $R^3$ (Reject, Remember, Retry), a black-box multi-LLM framework in which the attacker conditions on a compact state rather than the raw transcript. Turns that fail to raise the attack risk score are removed from the target's context but remembered by the attacker, together with the reason for failure. We call this asymmetric memory. Across two benchmarks and six target LLMs, $R^3$ outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by up to 53% in ASR@5 under matched query budgets, and a low-capacity attacker with $R^3$'s state is able to outperform high-capacity attackers without it by up to 34% in ASR@5. Ablations attribute most gains to remembering rejected turns, and the risk score acts as a coarse threshold rather than a progress measure.
  </details>

- **[Model Organisms of Collusion: Corrective Transfer in Untrusted Monitors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30582)**  `arXiv:2610.30582`  
  _Juan-Pablo Rivera, Ari Spiesberger, Juan J Vazquez, Arun Jose, Axel Ahlqvist, Adeline Kassler, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI control protocols aim to get useful work from models that may be misaligned by monitoring their behaviour. One approach uses a second copy of the same model as the monitor. This avoids relying on a weaker reviewer the model could outsmart, but risks collusion: the model, now monitoring itself, may cover up its own misbehaviour. One remedy is to fine-tune the monitor on whatever examples of misbehaviour the control team has caught. We ask whether this training also removes collusion that the control team has not yet found. We fine-tune Llama-3.3-70B and Qwen2.5-72B to install five policy-monitor behaviour pairs, correct the monitor on 300 honest reviews of one caught collusion, and measure collusion on the other four in unseen prompt formats. Averaged over the three seeds of each family, correction reduces held-out collusion from 91% to 14-20% under three of the four methods we compare (individual seeds 10-32%), while a control fine-tuned on the same number of unrelated examples leaves 56-93%. Family-average accuracy on control items changes by at most five points and capability is preserved, apart from a drop in mathematical accuracy on one Llama seed. Under the tested training configuration, methods with similar caught-task accuracy differ in held-out transfer, from 58% to 83% average reduction, so the caught behaviour alone does not reveal how far a correction reaches. Training on a known failure removes it almost entirely (1-2% remaining), yet 14-20% of unknown failures persist, so near-perfect correction of the caught behaviour overstates how much collusion has been removed.
  </details>

- **[From Prediction to Action: Population Health World Model for Digital Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24662)**  `arXiv:2610.24662`  
  _Xike Xie, Yu Li, Weizhong Yang, Amalie Dyda, Shaohua Kevin Zhou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Digital medicine now captures data that traces individual health trajectories and reveals population health dynamics through electronic health records, claims, remote monitoring, wearable devices, mobile devices, social sensing data, medical images, etc. But much of its modeling still centers on task-specific, outcome-specific, and prediction-specific estimation. The next frontier is not simply better prediction, but action in an evolving health world shaped by multiple decision-makers, heterogeneous populations, resource constraints, and feedback in care delivery. This is why digital medicine now needs world models: not only to estimate what will happen, but to represent how actions change what can happen next -- whom to prioritize, how to allocate scarce resources, how to adapt care across subgroups, and how interventions reshape future health trajectories and population dynamics.We call such a model population health world model (PHWM): a more foundational representation of an evolving health world in which health states, individual trajectories, population health dynamics, interventions, constraints, and feedback are intrinsically coupled. Building a PHWM endows three unmet structural gaps in current digital medicine: dynamic health-state modeling, population-level intervention intelligence, and validation, equity, and governance for action-oriented use. We argue that addressing these gaps requires three minimal capabilities: representation learning under action-dependent observation, multi-scale intervention intelligence, and validation with explicit decision boundaries, while highlighting the importance of social, contextual, and public-health sensing data for action-oriented digital medicine.
  </details>

- **[Self-Building Labs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23826)**  `arXiv:2610.23826`  
  _Jiaru Bai, Yuchi Zhao, Han Hao, Elia Savino, Miroslav Bogdanovic, Derk H.W. ten Klooster, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) accelerate scientific discovery by automating experimental execution and optimization. AI agents can increasingly propose new experiments, but existing SDLs can only run the procedures they were built for. Adapting them to a new experiment still requires extensive manual engineering, which makes laboratory flexibility the bottleneck for scaling discovery. We introduce the self-building laboratory (SBL), which designs, configures, and operates its own laboratory setup given a high-level scientific goal. SBL starts from whatever already exists, from an empty room to a working SDL, and builds the missing capabilities: the lab layout, its digital twin, robot skills, and experimental workflows. We demonstrate the system working across different robots and setups, scaling from a single workstation to laboratories with several robots working together. We validate SBL's end-to-end capabilities through a real-world quantum-dot synthesis experiment. By shifting human researchers from manually configuring and programming device automation to specifying scientific objectives, SBL makes scientific automation more accessible, flexible, and scalable. Project website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Terminal-RSI: Recursive Self-Improving Terminal Agents via Recursive Self-Rewrite and Policy-Refine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22192)**  `arXiv:2610.22192`  
  _Junyao Yang, Zongxia Li, Yucheng Shi, Zhongzhi Li, Ruhan Wang, Ruiwen Zhou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Converting verifier outcomes into useful policy updates and partial progress into complete task resolution is crucial for terminal agents. We present Terminal-RSI, which alternates Recursive Self-Rewrite (RSR) of verified trajectories with critic-inheriting Recursive Policy-Refine (RPR). We formulate Turn-Boundary Skip-Observation GAE (TB-GAE) to retain token-specific baselines while applying decay across interaction turns. Family Dense Reward (FDR) calibrates semantic progress across task families; Success-Prioritized Reward (SPR) separates strict success from bounded partial credit. A Warm-up Critic Model, recursive critic handoff, and stage-specific learning rates coordinate the two refinement phases. The five-stage sequence from Qwen3.8-27B base model through RSR-1, RPR-1, RSR-2, and RPR-2 yields Terminal-RSI-27B: 19.84% on Terminal-Bench 4.0, 86.52% on Terminal-Bench 2.1, 16.95% on Terminal-Bench 3.0, and 79.00% on Terminal-Bench Hard. On LHTB, process reward rises from 0.21 to 0.37 and strict completion from 0/46 to 5/46; SWR100 success doubles from 3.00% to 6.00%. By recursively coupling verified trajectory rewriting with critic-inheriting policy refinement, each retained stage consolidates discovered behaviors and progressively advances the agent from partial local repairs to strict terminal verification.
  </details>

- **[Does a Governed Confidence Score Know When a Text-to-SQL Answer Is Right? An Exploratory Study on BIRD and a Pre-registered Confirmation on Spider](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17256)**  `arXiv:2610.17256`  
  _Raghu Venkat, Tricha Anjali_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational analytics lets people ask questions of records spread across many systems and get answers in plain language; it is useful only if each answer can be trusted and explained. A production system of this kind attaches a confidence score to every answer it writes over a database and uses it to show the answer, show it with a warning, or withhold it. The score's weights, caps and thresholds were set by design. This paper audits that design. On the 1,534 questions of BIRD dev, answered by GPT-5.1 with five samples per question, the designed score separates right from wrong answers weakly (AUROC 0.624) and withholds 35.7% of correct answers. Nearly all of its ranking signal comes from agreement among the samples, and most of it is lost where agreement is combined by a minimum with a checklist that drops to zero on any unsupported claim, including commentary. Before any Spider answer existed we froze and hashed a rule in which agreement with the shown answer is the score, with deterministic vetoes and deterministic and judge-based caps. On Spider dev AUROC rose from 0.566 to 0.725 (+0.159, 95% CI +0.097 to +0.221; database-level CI +0.098 to +0.234), with PASS accuracy 95.7% against a 92.6% base rate. The frozen rule was not the best agreement-based rule on Spider, and the harness and prompts were rebuilt after registration. Added after review: a GPT-5.1 judge asked for P(correct), averaged with agreement, ranked higher in every evaluation (0.837 on Spider), but it grades its own model's answers and its gain shrinks under execution-match labels; one answer per question from a second model family raised agreement's AUROC from 0.749 to 0.800 on Spider, a gain that held under both label definitions. Labels are model-judged; a ten-answer human pilot found no disagreement, and a full audit has not been done.
  </details>

- **[WeFair: Fidelity-Aware Alignment from Human Feedback for Personalized Query Rewriting in Short-Video Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16927)**  `arXiv:2610.16927`  
  _Yingtao Ren, Aolin Li, Cheng Cheng, Huiyun Hu, Huasheng Liang, Baoxing Xie, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Short-video search needs compact query rewriters that recover users' personalized intent from ambiguous queries under strict latency constraints. Existing LLM-based methods have improved rewriting by using synthetic reasoning and subsequent consumption-behavioral feedback. However, overemphasizing subsequent consumption can favor engaging content over the user's immediate need, causing intent drift even when rewritten queries are grounded in behavioral history. We propose WeFair, Fidelity-Aware Alignment from Human Feedback for Personalized Query Rewriting in WeChat short-video search. WeFair uses human feedback according to how directly it reflects personalized intent, assigning feedback fidelity to a progression from demonstration to preference to verification. We first mine actual queries and user reformulations from search sessions, and then screen them for contextual support. In the demonstration stage, a supervised fine-tuning curriculum progresses from large-scale context-to-query learning to reasoning and direct prediction on human reformulation and preservation. In the preference stage, direct preference optimization learns relative preferences from subsequent consumption feedback. In the verification stage, group relative policy optimization rewards exact agreement with reformulation or preservation targets to constrain intent drift. Further analysis shows that broad supervision benefits the full curriculum supervised training, while strict verification achieves better performance than auxiliary rewards. In offline experiments, WeFair-2B outperforms strong rewriting baselines in overall accuracy and intent preservation. An online A/B test shows relative increases of 2.593% in effective video-view rate and a 2.350% relative reduction in query reformulation rate. WeFair-2B has been deployed in the WeChat short-video search system, which serves over 100 million queries per day.
  </details>

- **[From Stages to States: Unified Iterative Reasoning for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15620)**  `arXiv:2610.15620`  
  _Chuang Yu, Xuefei Ma, Yuanhao Feng, Wuxiong Zeng, Bo Wang, Minghong Cai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Chain-of-Thought (MCoT) substantially enhances the complex reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) by explicitly modeling intermediate reasoning processes. However, most learning-based MCoT methods follow a stage-centric paradigm, where different reasoning behaviors are assigned to predefined stages and optimized separately. This results in fragmented reasoning and a training-inference reasoning-state gap. To address these limitations, we propose ReState, a unified iterative reasoning framework that reformulates multimodal reasoning from a stage-driven pipeline into a state-driven dynamic process. It comprises two complementary components: Unified Reasoning State Modeling (URSM) and Closed-Loop Reasoning-State Learning (CRSL). URSM unifies reasoning construction, correct reasoning preservation, and erroneous reasoning correction as reasoning-state transitions within a shared state space, enabling these heterogeneous reasoning behaviors to be learned jointly. CRSL further incorporates self-generated historical reasoning states during training and recursively updates the current state during inference, thereby mitigating the reasoning-state gap and enabling iterative self-refinement without requiring an additional reflection module, critic, or independent correction stage. Furthermore, we construct VersaCoT-10K, a high-quality CoT dataset spanning eight visual reasoning domains, which provides diverse supervision for multi-domain reasoning and reasoning-state refinement. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ReState exhibits strong adaptability across different MLLM backbones and achieves strong performance on multiple public reasoning benchmarks, supporting the shift from stage-centric reasoning toward state-centric reasoning. Our data and code are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Fine-Tuning VLM for Enhancing AI's Spatial Intelligence: Understanding 3D and 2D Rotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04206)**  `arXiv:2610.04206`  
  _Uttamasha Monjoree, Wei Yan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial intelligence is a fundamental skill in multiple domains, such as Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics (STEM), Medicine, Architecture, and Construction. Recent studies indicate that Vision-Language Models (VLMs) still face limitations in spatial reasoning, which inhibits artificial intelligence (AI) from performing practical spatial tasks. Using multiple object-rotation datasets developed for training and evaluation, our experiments demonstrated promising improvements in both 2D and 3D rotation detection. Fine-tuned Google DeepMind-built Gemma-4 mixture-of-experts (MoE) models significantly outperformed fine-tuned Gemma-4 generalist models in predicting rotations defined by both their axes and angles. Fine-tuning also substantially improved angle estimation for 2D representation without requiring an explicit coordinate system. Furthermore, identifiable objects did not improve angle-detection accuracy; instead, objects with prominent linear features showed improved performance.
  </details>

- **[LazyAgent: Demand-Driven Materialization and Physical Optimization of Agentic Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23058)**  `arXiv:2609.23058`  
  _Xin Heng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent runtimes that plan before acting run every step as soon as it is ready. Neither these runtimes nor their benchmarks distinguish a step that is ready from a step the request needs, and the difference is large: across 303 requests on 154 production workflows, an eager runtime that stops once the goal is met spends 62.2% of its CPU on work the request never needed. LazyAgent is, to our knowledge, the first complete formulation of demand-driven execution for agent runtimes. Its rule: run a ready step only if the current request needs it, and keep that answer exact as the plan changes. A demanded set derived from the program decides, in one traversal with no model calls, so LazyAgent spends nothing outside the request. Because demand is defined exactly, its behavior is proved: builders know before any run where demand must save and where it must tie. The bill is known before a plan runs, so LazyAgent can act as a query optimizer to choose among candidate workflows. Measured live against that eager runtime, LazyAgent saves 51.7% and 52.1% of container time on release gates where a frontier LLM writes the patches, 12.8% of tokens on DataSciBench plans at matched quality, and 24.0% of dollars inside a live LangGraph application, with no losses. When the LLM re-plans after real code failures, it solves every solvable goal with 11.7% fewer tokens per goal. On the production workflows it saves 42.0% of CPU, ties exactly where a request needs the whole graph, and adds at most 0.43% overhead. The same set steers every physical operation: projection saves 80-89% of a shared CI step, where the identical eager control saves 0.0%. Public agent benchmarks are designed the same way as eager runtimes, so they cannot show this difference. We compose our test programs from their tasks and call on industry to build benchmarks that measure how efficiently agent workflows run.
  </details>

- **[A Unified Per-Token Gating Family for On-Policy Distillation: FKL/RKL Mixing with Multi-Channel and Bias Coefficients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11768)**  `arXiv:2609.11768`  
  _Suwan Wu, Yumeng Lin, Pengcheng Yuan, Xiaolong Jiang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Per-token gating of forward/reverse KL losses has become a standard technique for on-policy knowledge distillation (OPD), but existing methods such as EOPD (Jin et al., 2026) and ToDi (Jung et al., 2025) each fix a single gating signal and a single gating direction, and the two have never been compared directly. We introduce a four-coefficient parameterization lambda_t = sigma(a * h_t + b * u(x) + c + d * gap_t) in which direction-aligned proxies of EOPD and ToDi appear as one-dimensional (1D) restrictions, and which adds multi-channel composition and an explicit bias as further degrees of freedom. On TweetEval (Barbieri et al., 2020) emotion and hate, with a Qwen3-32B teacher and a Qwen3-4B student, configurations in the full family reach higher accuracy than the matched-magnitude single-channel (entropy-only / gap-only) 1D restrictions in 33 of 36 comparable cells, and a 26-cell mean-match isolation experiment places dynamic gating ahead of effective-KL-matched static baselines in 19 of 26 cells. Because cells share training data, models, and parameter substructure, we report both counts as exploratory aggregate directional evidence rather than as independent hypothesis tests. Targeted three-seed paired replications of the nine headline comparisons singled out by that sweep -- including a third task, offensive -- are directionally consistent, but individually smaller than the single-seed estimates and not significant at n=3. We therefore present the parameterization primarily as a shared coordinate system for comparing per-token gating designs in short-output classification OPD.
  </details>

- **[SIRF: A Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model for Industrial Content Risk Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11752)**  `arXiv:2609.11752`  
  _Suwan Wu, Yumeng Lin, Pengcheng Yuan, Xiaolong Jiang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For industrial content risk control, the real deployment constraint is not average accuracy but how much risk can be auto-handled under high precision and second-level latency. We present SIRF (Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model), which internalizes a platform's complex policies, synthesized without additional human annotation via EntiGraph, MAGA rewriting and account-level chain-of-thought (CoT), into the weights via continued pretraining (CPT), so rules are applied at high precision under an ultra-low-latency, verdict-only deployment. A controlled same-source comparison (Qwen3-8B-SFT vs. SIRF-8B-SFT, identical policy injection and verdict-only output form, differing only in policy-grounded CPT) attributes the gain to internalization: SIRF-8B-SFT reaches 71.3% Black Recall@P95, +15.1pp over the baseline, using only ~70M CPT tokens without harming general ability, and among included, logprob-available models under this interface it matches or exceeds far larger systems. SIRF is deployed as a tree-model adjudication layer (20% more mis-penalized samples recovered) and transfers to a freezing scenario at low cost (~70% relative mis-penalization reduction).
  </details>

- **[From Association to Causation: Improving Retrieval Precision of Retrieval-Augmented Generation via Causal Relations and an Attention Mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21702)**  `arXiv:2608.21702`  
  _Jing Liu, Yongxing Qi, Muchen Jiang, Chengnan Hu, Qingqing Peng, Haoming Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds LLM generation on retrieved documents, but the standard terminal retrieval stage--dense-vector similarity, optionally followed by reranking--often returns documents that share keywords with the query without containing the needed information, a failure mode that grows with the knowledge base. We trace it to a conceptual gap: similarity captures only associational relations, whereas the documents that matter are linked to the query causally. We model the terminal retrieval stage with a causal graph grounded in Reichenbach's common cause principle: the keywords shared by the query and a retrieved document form a latent common cause A, and the document's residual keywords form a latent set B linking the document to the ideal output. Since a retrieved document is a collider (A -> d <- B), retrieval itself opens an associational path between the query and B, which licenses a training-free, attention-style re-scoring rule: the cosine similarity between the query embedding and the weighted centroid embedding of B. Unlike causality-enhanced RAG variants that model causal relations inside the knowledge content, our graph models the causal structure of the retrieval process itself. On a real 471-document enterprise knowledge base, the method promotes a relevant guideline from rank 6 to the top 3; on a controlled diagnostic corpus reproducing the keyword-stuffing regime, it improves the mean target rank from 2.88 to 1.25, while a trained cross-encoder reranker barely helps (2.63). Conversely, on three BEIR benchmarks the score underperforms the similarity baseline, delineating the applicability boundary: the method guards the keyword-stuffing regime of growing proprietary knowledge bases and complements neural rerankers; a corpus-level calibration gate selects the correct regime with >= 95% reliability. A fully local testbed demonstrates deployability.
  </details>

- **[FedPA-LoRA: Product-Aligned Framework for Mitigating Aggregation and Initialization Errors in Heterogeneous Federated LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15381)**  `arXiv:2608.15381`  
  _Juseok Jeon, Ramy E. Ali, Doyun Kwon, Myungbeom Her, Jinhwi Kim, Jinhyun So_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) enables efficient federated fine-tuning of large language models, but its factorization creates a tension between aggregation fidelity and local optimization continuity. Factor-wise aggregation incurs aggregation mismatch but better preserves factor continuity, whereas product-space reconstruction reduces this mismatch at the cost of greater factor-level initialization mismatch. We propose FedPA-LoRA, a product-aligned framework that addresses both mismatches and establishes convergence bounds for homogeneous and heterogeneous ranks. Clients preserve local factors across rounds to eliminate initialization mismatch and align their products with rank-specific global references to reduce client drift. The server aggregates heterogeneous-rank updates in product space to mitigate aggregation mismatch and efficiently reconstructs a rank-constrained global adapter without forming the dense aggregate. By decoupling local and reference ranks, FedPA-LoRA supports client-specific computation and communication budgets. Experiments on natural language understanding and generation tasks show consistent gains under data and resource heterogeneity, including a 6.82 percentage-point improvement in average GLUE accuracy under heterogeneous ranks. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Neuro-Symbolic Meta-Policies for Temporal Knowledge-Graph Memory under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18368)**  `arXiv:2607.18368`  
  _Taewoon Kim, Vincent François-Lavet, Michael Cochez_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Partially observable reinforcement learning requires deciding what to retain, retrieve, and forget over time. We introduce a neuro-symbolic meta-policy that learns which symbolic memory heuristic to apply at each decision point while keeping execution symbolic. Our setting uses temporal knowledge-graph memory in RoomKG, where hidden state and observations are represented as Resource Description Framework (RDF) graphs and memory is augmented with temporal RDF triple annotations. The model combines knowledge-graph encoding of memory contents with value heads for question answering, exploration, and forgetting, yielding a controller that is both adaptive and inspectable. This gives the work a direct Semantic Web grounding through RDF-based representation, annotation-compatible graph semantics, and graph-based symbolic operations over explicit memory state. On train/test room splits at long-term memory capacity of 512, the qualifier-aware StarE-GNN configuration achieves the best held-out performance among the compared symbolic, neural, and neuro-symbolic systems while preserving step-level traceability of memory-management decisions.
  </details>

- **[Physics-Audited Agentic Discovery in Scientific Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07379)**  `arXiv:2607.07379`  
  _Diab W. Abueidda, Bilal Ahmed, Panos Pantidis, Mostafa E. Mobasher_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic discovery can yield mechanics surrogates with low validation error that nonetheless violate physical requirements. We present Physics-Audited Agentic SciML (PA-SciML), a framework combining large language model agents with physics auditing. Physical requirements, encoded as contracts such as superposition, boundary conditions, and causality, are fixed before candidate search and tested on predicted fields without reference solutions, including through an adversarial search. Audit results guide later proposals and model selection but are reported separately from validation error. Single-edit tests document reusable design changes. Four solid-mechanics problems of increasing complexity, from parametric elasticity to heterogeneous stiffness fields and cyclic plasticity with combined hardening, reveal causality, zero-input, and stress-bound violations in surrogates selected by error alone. In elastodynamics, two surrogates reach nearly equal error, but only the audit-enabled one is causal by construction. Audit-enabled surrogates pass the required checks at sampled inputs while matching or exceeding audit-disabled accuracy in most comparisons.
  </details>

- **[Generative Recursive Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19376)**  `arXiv:2605.19376`  
  _Junyeob Baek, Mingyu Jo, Minsu Kim, Mengye Ren, Yoshua Bengio, Sungjin Ahn_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How should future neural reasoning systems implement extended computation? Recursive Reasoning Models (RRMs) offer a promising alternative to autoregressive sequence extension by performing iterative latent-state refinement with shared transition functions. Yet existing RRMs are largely deterministic, following a single latent trajectory and converging to a single prediction. We introduce Generative Recursive reAsoning Models (GRAM), a framework that turns recursive latent reasoning into probabilistic multi-trajectory computation. GRAM models reasoning as a stochastic latent trajectory, enabling multiple hypotheses, alternative solution strategies, and inference-time scaling through both recursive depth and parallel trajectory sampling. This yields a latent-variable generative model supporting conditional reasoning via $p_\theta(y \mid x)$ and, with fixed or absent inputs, unconditional generation via $p_\theta(x)$. Trained with amortized variational inference, GRAM improves over deterministic recurrent and recursive baselines on structured reasoning and multi-solution constraint satisfaction tasks, while demonstrating an unconditional generation capability.this https URL
  </details>

- **[Teaching Large Language Models When Not to Know: Learning Temporal Critique for Ex-Ante Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14636)**  `arXiv:2605.14636`  
  _Chenlu Ding, Jiancan Wu, Yanchen Luo, Zheyuan Liu, Yancheng Yuan, Xiang Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often fail to reason under temporal cutoffs: when prompted to answer from the standpoint of an earlier time, they exploit knowledge that became available only later. We study this failure through the lens of ex-ante reasoning, where a model must rely exclusively on information knowable before a cutoff. Through a systematic analysis of prompt-level interventions, we find that temporal leakage is highly sensitive to cutoff formulation and instruction placement: explicit cutoff statements outperform implicit historical framings, and prefix constraints reduce leakage more effectively than suffix constraints. These findings indicate that prompting can steer models into a temporal frame, but does not endow them with the ability to verify whether a response is temporally admissible. We further argue that supervised fine-tuning is insufficient, since ex-ante correctness is not an intrinsic property of an answer, but a relation between the answer and the cutoff. To address this gap, we propose TCFT, a Temporal Critique Fine-Tuning framework that trains models to acquire cutoff-aware temporal verification. Given a query, a cutoff, and a candidate response, TCFT teaches the model to identify post-cutoff leakage, explain temporal boundary violations, and judge temporal admissibility. Experiments with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct show that TCFT consistently outperforms prompting and SFT baselines, reducing average leakage by 41.89 and 37.79 percentage points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Controllable User Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11519)**  `arXiv:2605.11519`  
  _Guy Tennenholtz, Ofer Meshi, Amir Globerson, Uri Shalit, Jihwan Jeong, Craig Boutilier_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Using offline datasets to evaluate conversational agents often fails to cover rare scenarios or to support testing new policies. This has motivated the use of controllable user simulators for targeted, counterfactual evaluation, typically implemented by prompting or fine-tuning large language models. In this work, we formalize controllable simulation as a causal inference problem. By bridging natural language evaluation with off-policy evaluation methodology, we show that the standard practice of training simulators via supervised fine-tuning on post-hoc trajectory labels yields a structurally biased model. Specifically, these labels are inextricably coupled to the data-generating behavior policy, injecting a look-ahead bias that breaks causal consistency. Furthermore, we prove that under policy shift this failure causes the variance of evaluation metrics to explode geometrically, a phenomenon we term controllability collapse. To restore causal consistency, we establish theoretical conditions for accurate simulation and propose practical training mitigations: a priori controls, step-wise dynamic controls, and direct policy-conditioned learning. Empirical evaluation confirms that while standard global controls distort conversational distributions and collapse behavioral diversity, our causally grounded simulators eliminate look-ahead bias, preserve natural variance, and exhibit robust zero-shot generalization to unseen agent behaviors.
  </details>

- **[ThinkSafe: Self-Generated Safety Alignment for Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.23143)**  `arXiv:2601.23143`  
  _Seanie Lee, Sangwoo Park, Yumin Choi, Gyeongman Kim, Minki Kang, Jihun Yun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models achieve strong performance through reinforcement learning but can suffer safety degradation. External teacher distillation can restore safety yet may compromise reasoning performance. We formalize safety realignment as a KL projection: the student's native filter-accepted distribution is uniquely KL-optimal, while teacher targets incur excess KL when they differ. Guided by this analysis, we propose ThinkSafe, which generates safety-alignment data from the frozen student without external teachers. Refusal steering elicits safety reasoning, and a safety guard filters the responses before offline fine-tuning. Our analysis bounds steering-induced target distortion and gives a sufficient condition for lower average target KL than teacher distillation. Experiments on DeepSeek-R1-Distill and Qwen3 show substantial safety improvements with competitive reasoning performance. On Qwen3-0.6B, ThinkSafe achieves better safety and comparable reasoning to GRPO in about one-seventh of the wall-clock time, including data generation. Code, models, and datasets are available atthis https URLandthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Ax-Prover: A Deep Reasoning Agentic Framework for Theorem Proving in Mathematics and Quantum Physics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.12787)**  `arXiv:2510.12787`  
  _Benjamin Breen, Marco Del Tredici, Jacob McCarran, Javier Aspuru Mijares, Weichen Winston Yin, Kfir Sulimany, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Ax-Prover, a multi-agent system for automated theorem proving in Lean that can solve problems across diverse scientific domains and operate either autonomously or collaboratively with human experts. To achieve this, Ax-Prover approaches scientific problem solving through formal proof generation, a process that demands both creative reasoning and strict syntactic rigor. Ax-Prover meets this challenge by equipping Large Language Models (LLMs), which provide knowledge and reasoning, with Lean tools via the Model Context Protocol (MCP), which ensure formal correctness. To evaluate its performance as an autonomous prover, we benchmark our approach against frontier LLMs and specialized prover models on two public math benchmarks and on two Lean benchmarks we introduce in the fields of abstract algebra and quantum theory. On public datasets, Ax-Prover is competitive with state-of-the-art provers, while it largely outperforms them on the new benchmarks. This shows that, unlike specialized systems that struggle to generalize, our tool-based agentic theorem prover approach offers a generalizable methodology for formal verification across diverse scientific domains. Furthermore, we demonstrate Ax-Prover's assistant capabilities in a practical use case, showing how it enabled an expert mathematician to formalize the proof of a complex cryptography theorem.
  </details>

- **[Shared Modular Recurrence in Contextual MDPs for Universal Morphology Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08630)**  `arXiv:2506.08630`  
  _Laurens Engwegen, Max Weltevrede, Caroline Horsch, Daan Brinks, Wendelin Böhmer_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A universal controller for any robot morphology would greatly improve computational and data efficiency. Steps have been made towards such multi-robot control by utilizing contextual information about the properties of individual robots and exploiting their modular structure in the architecture of deep reinforcement learning agents. When the robots have highly dissimilar morphologies, however, this becomes a challenging problem, especially when the agent must generalize to new, unseen robots. In this paper, we consider the fact that contextual features are often only partially available, but that they can be recovered through modular interactions. This can allow for better multi-robot control and generalization to contexts that are not seen during training. To this extent, we implement a transformer-based architecture with shared modular recurrence and evaluate its (generalization) performance on a large set of MuJoCo robots. The results show a substantial improvement in zero-shot generalization performance on robots with unseen dynamics, kinematics, and topologies, in four different environments.
  </details>

- **[Flide: Accelerating Decision Inference with Exact State Forking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42148)**  `arXiv:2610.42148`  
  _Bobo Li, Jiajia Song, Hao Fei, Wynne Hsu, Mong Li Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models such as Jev have emerged as a highly promising paradigm for LLM agent systems, providing lightweight and reliable categorical reasoning for rapid System One execution. However, existing generation-oriented inference engines fail to support these workloads effectively, suffering from severe prefix recomputation overheads or distorted output probabilities. To address this challenge, we propose Flide, an efficient and exact inference engine that decouples the logical tree structure of a decision request from its physical execution within a single flattened token stream. Through ForkAttention, Flide navigates request dependencies using GPU-resident indices across both standard attention and recurrent Gated DeltaNet layers, guaranteeing in exact arithmetic that every query branch evaluates identically to an isolated forward pass. By expressing branch topologies directly in GPU-resident metadata, Flide accommodates diverse request mixtures with a compact set of pre-recorded execution graphs while enabling expanding agent histories to resume from checkpoints saved in earlier passes. Extensive evaluations on public hybrid and decision models demonstrate that Flide answers queries up to 9.5 times faster than vLLM and SGLang while staying closest to a float32 reference in total variation distance. By unifying physical execution throughput with mathematical exactness, Flide establishes a principled foundation for scalable, latency-critical decision intelligence in agent systems.
  </details>

- **[DecisionRank: On-Policy Distillation for Decision-Model Rerankers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42128)**  `arXiv:2610.42128`  
  _Yanran Wu, Dian Yu, Chongyang Bai, Jieyi Zhang, Rui Liu, Tianyi Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models target structured tasks by directly predicting choices or scores, avoiding autoregressive decoding latency. However, transferring a generative LLM's ranking knowledge to a decision model remains challenging: the teacher expresses preferences through a ranked list of document ID sequences, while the student determines the ranking using scores from the first-token position. To bridge this gap, we introduce DecisionRank, a decision model trained through item-space on-policy distillation (OPD). Item-space OPD constructs the student's rollout by sorting its document scores into a ranked sequence. A generative teacher takes the resulting ranking list as inputs and provides conditional feedback to directly supervise those scores. Combined with offline ranking supervision, DecisionRank achieves state-of-the-art performance on both general and reasoning-intensive retrieval benchmarks such as TREC DL, BEIR, and BRIGHT. Across four benchmarks, item-space OPD consistently outperforms alternative training objectives. Even at 1.7B parameters, DecisionRank surpasses its 27B generative teacher on TREC DL19--23. At 8B, it outperforms its generative listwise counterpart while running $24.5\times$ faster on TREC DL and $16.7\times$ faster on BRIGHT.
  </details>

- **[Subquadratic Attention with a Hierarchical Tree-Structured Indexer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42112)**  `arXiv:2610.42112`  
  _Leon Lufkin, Cutter Dawes, Alexander Speicher, Kamesh Krishnamurthy, Tomás Figliolia, Beren Millidge_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The quadratic scaling of attention is a fundamental bottleneck limiting the long-context capabilities of large language models (LLMs). A variety of approaches have attempted to circumvent this, but they either fail to match attention's expressiveness (e.g., sliding-window or linear attention variants with fixed-size state) or only achieve constant-factor speedups (e.g., sparse attention methods whose indexers themselves scale quadratically). In this paper, we derive an asymptotically subquadratic sparse attention method based on a hierarchical, tree-structured indexer, which we call Zyphra Hierarchical Attention (ZHA). ZHA constructs a tree of recursively compressed key summaries over the KV cache, which future queries traverse from the top down to retrieve a sparse subset of keys. Crucially, by iteratively growing the tree's depth, the total cost including routing and final attention grows sublinearly in sequence length, resulting in overall subquadratic (but superlinear) scaling for attention. We demonstrate that ZHA can be cheaply applied to existing pretrained models with only a short KL distillation training stage over a few billion tokens, and maintains performance on long-context retrieval and challenging reasoning, mathematical, and agentic coding evaluation tasks. Moreover, we show that our method is highly robust to tree geometry including very deep trees of up to 6 levels. Finally, we develop a detailed performance model and show that our approach scales subquadratically, dramatically outperforms competing methods beyond 1 million context, and ultimately has the potential to extend effective context windows to the hundreds of millions or billions of tokens.
  </details>

- **[RangeSWA: Training-Free Length Extrapolation for Continuous Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42084)**  `arXiv:2610.42084`  
  _Guangfu Guo, Xiaoqian Lu, Linsey Pang, Weiran Yao, Haolin Chen, Long Cheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous diffusion language models show promising potential in text generation tasks, but are prone to problems such as text repetition, semantic drift, and generation quality degradation when the generation length exceeds the training length. To address these challenges, we propose RangeSWA, a training-free length extrapolation method. The method combines local sliding-window attention, remote block summaries, and dynamic content retrieval to preserve local details and support long-range context access. In addition, generation degradation caused by length extrapolation is mitigated through positional remapping, conditioning attention correction, and denoising-stage-adaptive window scheduling. We conducted experiments on multiple continuous diffusion language models, and the results show that RangeSWA can effectively reduce generative perplexity and text repetition rates in long-text generation.
  </details>

- **[PersonaChatBench: Evaluating Chat Assistants with Diverse Simulated Users](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42060)**  `arXiv:2610.42060`  
  _Zheyuan Deng, Taoran Li, Jingwen Zhang, Yuante Li, Dachuan Zhao, Fulei Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational assistants are expected to help people with diverse backgrounds, preferences, and communication styles. Yet evaluations centered on a single cooperative user provide limited insight into how reliably an assistant serves users with different personas. We introduce PersonaChatBench, a framework for evaluating task competence and responsiveness to diverse user needs. PersonaChatBench pairs the same underlying task with multiple simulated users, each guided by a private persona card that specifies distinct needs, preferences, and interaction behaviors. By holding the task fixed while varying the user, the framework examines how assistant performance changes across user conditions. We evaluate 20 language models across six domains, separately assessing native task outcomes, fulfillment of expressed persona needs, and simulator fidelity. Our results show that task performance and need fulfillment vary across personas and domains. Strong task performance does not necessarily translate into satisfying individual needs, and models' relative strengths in meeting those needs are not consistent across domains. PersonaChatBench makes these differences measurable, revealing gaps obscured by aggregate scores and helping identify which users an assistant serves well and where adaptation is needed. It provides a basis for evaluating consistency across users and guiding the development of assistants that better accommodate diverse needs in everyday applications.
  </details>

- **[CRoS: Causality Reward on Scrambled CoTs Improves Accuracy and Reasoning Faithfulness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42054)**  `arXiv:2610.42054`  
  _Xiao Yu, Justin Chih-Yao Chen, Zaid Khan, Hyunji Lee, Mohit Bansal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become the standard method for training language models to reason. However, RLVR typically relies solely on outcome correctness, which does not guarantee that the CoT causally contributes to the final answer. Without this connection, outcome rewards may fail to reinforce high-quality reasoning. Existing causality metrics assess CoT causality by perturbing the CoT, such as deleting reasoning steps or injecting errors, and measuring changes in the final answer. However, these perturbations inevitably alter information in each step. Consequently, high causality scores may reflect sensitivity to information changes introduced by perturbation rather than true causality. Such confounding effects further induce undesirable reasoning behaviors when these metrics are used as training rewards, including generating less informative CoTs and reducing exploration and self-correction. To address this problem, we propose CRoS (Causality Reward on Scrambled CoTs), a novel CoT causality metric that perturbs the order of reasoning steps without altering content, thereby preserving information within each step. On Reasoning Gym with Gemma-3-4B, CRoS outperforms existing causality and outcome rewards in out-of-domain accuracy, CoT quality, and CoT faithfulness. Specifically, CRoS achieves 46.8% accuracy on five unseen logic and games datasets, outperforming the strongest baseline (43.8%). It also achieves better CoT quality, consistently outperforming baselines in CoT-Pass@K from K=1 to 128, averaging 43.4% vs. 41.4%. For CoT faithfulness, CRoS achieves a hint acknowledgment score of 34.0% vs. 20.2%.
  </details>

- **[Asynchronous Flow Map Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42018)**  `arXiv:2610.42018`  
  _Chanhyuk Lee, Wonjung Kim, Patrick Pynadath, Jaehoon Yoo, Satoshi Hayakawa, Ruqi Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The order in which tokens are predicted in language modeling has a profound impact on the quality of generated sequences. Autoregressive and masked diffusion models iteratively fill in tokens and can exploit such orderings across generation steps, but how to leverage an ordering for one-step parallel language modeling is unclear. We introduce asynchronous flow map language models (aFMLMs), which incorporate prediction order into one-step modeling by learning the flow maps of asynchronous flows. These flows specify an ordering through a tokenwise time schedule that controls the relative denoising progress of tokens. We formulate a general class of asynchronous flows that admit both prescribed and learned, state-adaptive schedules, show that they define valid flow maps, and develop new methods for their flow map distillation. The resulting models can generate all tokens in a single forward pass by internalizing the ordering encoded in the flow into their hidden computations. On Sudoku solving and code generation for GSM8K math word problems, aFMLM establishes new state-of-the-art one-step performance.
  </details>

- **[TurnTurk-GPT: Text-Based Turn-Taking Prediction for Turkish](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42010)**  `arXiv:2610.42010`  
  _Ahmet Tuğrul Bayrak, Fatma Nur Korkmaz, Nur Yağmur Tuncer, Mustafa Sertaç Türkel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents TurnTurk-GPT, a text-based turn-taking predictor for Turkish obtained by fine-tuning a Turkish GPT-2 model with the TurnGPT objective on synthetic and real dialogue, and evaluates it on real Turkish conversation against rule baselines, a zero-shot Turkish language model and a widely used multilingual end-of-turn detector. Evaluation uses word-level decision points on unpunctuated, lower-cased text approximating the output of streaming speech recognisers, recording-disjoint and speaker-disjoint folds, nested threshold selection and paired cluster bootstrap tests. TurnTurk-GPT outperforms the multilingual detector under both recording-disjoint and speaker-disjoint evaluation. It outperforms the zero-shot language model only when the test speakers occur in training; on unseen speakers the two are statistically indistinguishable. Removing the suffixes of the turn-final word reduces AP by up to 44%, against 2% for a sentence-internal word, locating the Turkish end-of-turn cue in verb-final morphology. Synthetic dialogue alone yields the weakest trained model, and when a speaker pauses the zero-shot and multilingual baselines remain close to chance. The model and the evaluation protocol are publicly released.
  </details>

- **[Aligning MLLMs with the Latent Structure of Human Cognition via Behavior-Derived Semantic Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41951)**  `arXiv:2610.41951`  
  _Ning E, Changde Du, Yizhuo Lu, Huiguang He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved impressive performance across a wide range of reasoning tasks, yet it remains unclear whether their internal representations and decision criteria align with the latent structure of human cognition. In this work, we propose a novel framework to align MLLMs with the multidimensional mental representations underlying human similarity judgements. We build on a previously established behavior-derived semantic embedding space, in which objects are represented along 66 interpretable dimensions derived from 4.70 million human Odd-One-Out (O1O) judgements. For each triplet, we infer the most salient decision dimension and convert it into a dimension-grounded linguistic rationale. This enables MLLMs to learn both the final choice and a justification consistent with the inferred behavioral criterion. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach improves model consistency with human similarity judgements while retaining competitive performance on standard multimodal benchmarks. Furthermore, using searchlight Representational Similarity Analysis (RSA) on an independent fMRI Natural Scenes Dataset (NSD), we observe increased representational alignment between model and human. Ultimately, our findings point to a data-driven route for incorporating human cognitive structure into MLLMs alignment. The code is available \href{this https URL}{here}.
  </details>

- **[Iterated Integrals as Lexical Fingerprints: Path Signature Embeddings for Lexical Similarity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41855)**  `arXiv:2610.41855`  
  _Raj Shekhar Singh, Pratham Agarwal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While sentence embeddings are primarily designed for semantic generalization, their latent geometry implicitly encodes surface-level lexical properties, a relationship that remains poorly understood. Shallow pooling methods discard word order, while deep Transformer models obscure these lexical alignments behind computational complexity and uninterpretable spaces. We propose constructing sentence embeddings via the path signature of Word2Vec trajectories, leveraging the iterated integrals of rough path theory to create representations that explicitly capture sequential and lexical structure in a geometrically tractable manner. Through extensive ablations spanning Word2Vec dimensions, signature depths, and PCA compression, we correlate embedding distances against five lexical string metrics: Levenshtein, Jaro-Winkler, word Jaccard, phoneme Levenshtein, and character TF-IDF. We reveal that while average pooling almost entirely fails to capture character-level edit distances, path signatures strongly align with them, with depth 2 signatures optimally balancing character and word-level sensitivities. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the critical lexical variance is concentrated in a highly compact PCA subspace, and unlike pooling, signatures structurally preserve and quantify word order. These findings establish path signatures not as semantic generalizers, but as rigorous mathematical lenses that explicitly encode discrete lexical structures and word order, offering an interpretable alternative to opaque deep learning for lexical tracking.
  </details>

- **[Stepwise-Querying: Distilling Reasoning without Access to Teacher Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41769)**  `arXiv:2610.41769`  
  _Dicong Yu, Wenhua Wang, Tian Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning distillation is widely used to transfer the reasoning capabilities of large language models to smaller students through teacher-generated supervision. In the black-box setting, however, teachers may expose only final answers and brief solutions, leaving intermediate calculations and validity conditions unavailable and thus limiting the reasoning supervision available for distillation. We propose Stepwise Querying, which elicits step-specific explanations without requiring access to the teacher's internal chain-of-thought (CoT) traces. Independent queries to the same teacher request intermediate derivations, validity checks, and corrections for individual steps, the assembled solutions are then used for standard supervised fine-tuning. Across two teachers, two students, and four mathematics and code benchmarks, our method achieves the highest score in 14 of 16 comparisons with three baselines. We further introduce Stepwise Explanation Information Gain (SEIG), a diagnostic based on the log-ratio of a frozen student's probabilities of correct step completion under stepwise and global explanations. Analyses on GSM8K show that incorrect-answer trajectories can remain useful when they preserve valid intermediate reasoning, while further decomposition yields limited additional gains.
  </details>

- **[GraphNIAH: A Benchmark for Long-Context Reasoning over Real-World Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41704)**  `arXiv:2610.41704`  
  _Minh-Nam Tran, Abulhair Saparov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The needle-in-a-haystack (NIAH) benchmark was introduced as a test for measuring a model's ability to find small relevant facts within a very large body of input text. However, existing NIAH benchmarks each suffer from one or more of the following limitations: (1) reliance on synthetic data, so their evaluations may not generalize to real-world settings; (2) reuse of previously published datasets, leading to the risk of benchmark contamination; (3) dependence on manual annotation, which requires human effort to create and update data and is prone to future benchmark contamination; and (4) unrealistic simplification of long-context multi-hop reasoning where each next step is trivial given previous steps, while real-world reasoning often requires planning over multiple possible proof paths. To address these limitations, we introduce \Benchmark, a dynamic benchmark built from real citation relationships among scientific papers published after the evaluated models' knowledge cutoff date, which utilizes real-world information while ensuring no benchmark contamination. The fully automated benchmark construction pipeline samples representative subgraphs of the citation graph formed by these papers and converts each subgraph into a long-context QA example with a verifiable answer. We use GraphNIAH to evaluate eight LLMs from the GPT-5.6, Gemini-3, and Qwen3.5 families. We find that accuracy falls as questions require longer reasoning paths: at question depths of 16 and 32, the GPT-5.6 and Qwen3.5 models answer YES/NO questions no better than random guessing, mostly by answering NO; four of the eight models answer no counting example correctly, and the others model get worse as the subgraph size increases. These findings suggest that current LLMs often stop searching the citation relationships in their context before they reach the answer, even though every fact they need is in the context.
  </details>

- **[Precision--Recall Trade-off on the Inference Level for LLMs in Grammatical Error Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41698)**  `arXiv:2610.41698`  
  _Ryszard Staruch, Filip Graliński_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have recently been adopted for Grammatical Error Correction (GEC), achieving strong performance via both full fine-tuning and prompt-based approaches. As probabilistic sequence models, LLMs expose inference-time parameters that directly modify token generation behavior; however, their potential for controlling GEC-specific trade-offs has not been systematically studied. In this work, we investigate decoding strategies for grammatical error correction. We show that the repetition penalty parameter enables controllable adjustment of the precision--recall trade-off at inference time, without modifying the model architecture, training procedure, or prompt. Through experiments on standard GEC benchmarks, we demonstrate that repetition-penalty-based decoding enables inference-time control of the precision--recall balance, conceptually related to methods previously proposed for sequence-to-sequence GEC models, such as Align-and-Predict Decoding (APD), while remaining simple and broadly applicable to existing LLMs that expose inference-time decoding penalties.
  </details>

- **[When Steering Shifts Discourse: Identifying and Intervening on Discourse-Level Changes due to Activation Steering in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41661)**  `arXiv:2610.41661`  
  _Shivam Adarsh, Hetianyu Huang, Lau Safin, Maria Maistro, Christina Lioma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering is an inference-time technique used to control or modify the behavior, tone, or safety properties of a large language model (LLM) without retraining the model's weights or editing its input prompt, but simply by adding a vector to its residual-stream activations. However, activation steering also changes the generated text in unintended ways, unrelated to intended behavior change, that are not well understood. Prior work has measured these changes only through coarse signals such as perplexity. In this work, we show that steering systematically produces significant changes in discourse dimensions of the generated text, such as its formality, use of evidence, and ambiguity. We present a way of countering this by adding a second, complementary vector that targets the affected discourse dimension. This intervention restores about 78% of the unintended discourse changes while preserving the intended steering behavior in about 93% of the generations.
  </details>

- **[Saidzoda-Tajik-LFM-2.6B: Adapting a 2.6B Hybrid Model to Tajik Tool Calling, and What Continued Pretraining Takes Away](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41576)**  `arXiv:2610.41576`  
  _Tohir Saidzoda, Nekruz Maxudov, Bedil Karimov, Abubakr Abdullozoda, Amin Jalilov, Jonibek Kishvarov, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We release Saidzoda-Tajik-LFM-2.6B, a Tajik adaptation of the 2.6B-parameter hybrid model LFM2.5-2.6B-Base, built for tool calling in Tajik on modest hardware. The recipe has two stages: LoRA continued pretraining (CPT) on 1,485,319 cleaned Tajik web documents for 3 epochs, then one epoch of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on a mix of machine-translated Tajik tool calls, English tool calls kept as replay, and Tajik instructions. On a 174-item tool-calling test set built from BFCL v3 and machine-translated to Tajik, strict full-call accuracy on Tajik prompts rises from 20.69 for the base model to 64.14 (paired difference +43.45 points, 95% interval +35.2 to +51.7); a lenient parser that also accepts the base model's own call formats puts the base at 31.72. English shows no detectable change (76.55 and 77.24). Every CPT checkpoint scores zero: CPT removes the native tool-call format and, as the lenient re-scoring shows, part of the skill itself. Outside tool calling, accuracy on a Tajik knowledge benchmark rises from 28.0 to 35.0, Belebele Tajik shows no significant change, and English MMLU falls from 60.8 to 52.5. The model is not a general Tajik assistant. It almost never declines when no tool fits (6.9% correct on Tajik, against 31.0% for the base under the lenient parser), it is weak at multi-turn calls, and its calls should be checked before they are executed. We describe the data, the training settings and every evaluation in enough detail to repeat them.
  </details>

- **[BYOD: Build Your Own DLM - Efficient Autoregressive-to-Diffusion Language Model Conversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41574)**  `arXiv:2610.41574`  
  _Ruurd J.A. Kuiper, Robert A. Bagheri_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlike autoregressive (AR) models, diffusion language models (DLMs) permit parallel token updates, flexible generation, and bidirectional attention, but leading systems rely on costly pretraining or full-model fine-tuning. We present Build Your Own DLM (BYOD), a generalizable LoRA-only recipe for parameter-efficient AR-to-DLM conversion. Without architecture-specific tuning, BYOD induces diffusion generation in Gemma 2 9B, Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen2.5 7B, and Ministral 8B; to our knowledge, this is the first reported AR-to-DLM conversion of a Mistral model. The adapters comprise 4.1-5.8% of final parameters while every base-model parameter remains frozen; each 25,000-iteration conversion needs at most 102.4M tokens, processed in 3.1-4.0 hours on one Google Colab GPU. All four models generate from fully masked sequences, use bidirectional context in a controlled attention test, and retain substantial parent-model knowledge. BYOD-Gemma is the strongest BYOD conversion on our nine-task evaluation, reaching 50.6% average accuracy compared with 51.9% for LLaDA 8B Instruct in the same harness. Open-generation quality at low network function evaluation (NFE) budgets remains the principal limitation: BYOD perplexity is 8.3-11.1 versus 4.1 for LLaDA and 2.2-3.0 for the AR parents when generating 128 tokens in 64 steps. To enable further experimentation and inspection, we provide a standalone training and evaluation implementation and interactive demos for all four conversions. BYOD thus provides a practical route for extending diffusion generation to other existing model families while identifying the gap that efficient conversion must next close.
  </details>

- **[Digital sovereignty in professional translation workflows: a case study of migrating from Windows to Linux](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41561)**  `arXiv:2610.41561`  
  _Michael Farrell_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This study examines digital sovereignty in professional translation through a screening of computer-assisted translation tools and translation management systems, and a case study of migrating a translator's workstation from Windows to Linux. The framework combines three principles - Control, Continuity and Exit - with three practical requirements: native Linux operation, independence from mandatory external services, and independent access to essential data and resources. Drawing on documentary evidence and the limited number of responses received from providers, the ecosystem screening distinguishes Linux accessibility from operational independence: browser-based platforms may remain dependent on provider-controlled infrastructure. The migration case demonstrates the feasibility of reconstructing one professional workflow around a locally operable Linux core, using LoopCAT, LibreOffice and locally run TranslateGemma, alongside tools for optical character recognition, communication, credential management and backup. Selected cloud services remained available where needed. The findings suggest that digital sovereignty depends on how workflow dependencies are organized, rather than on individual applications or operating systems. It does not require technological isolation, but meaningful control over essential components, continuity of work and practical exit options. The framework is tailored to the case examined here and may require adaptation to other professional workflows.
  </details>

- **[CEFR Level Assessment across Languages and Text Granularities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41553)**  `arXiv:2610.41553`  
  _Eugene Matsumura, Yuki Arase_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic assessment of textual CEFR levels is an important task in educational natural language processing. However, accurate CEFR level prediction remains challenging due to the naturally imbalanced distribution of CEFR levels and the ambiguous boundaries between adjacent levels. To address these challenges, we propose a unified training objective that combines focal loss with supervised contrastive learning. Focal loss mitigates class imbalance by emphasizing underrepresented and difficult examples, while supervised contrastive learning learns more discriminative representations by encouraging intra-class compactness and inter-class separation. We evaluate the proposed method on sentence- and document-level CEFR assessment in both monolingual and multilingual settings. Experimental results demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms strong baselines across all settings, establishing new state-of-the-art performance on multiple CEFR assessment benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Sparse Text Diffusion under Dual Budgets: Context Connectivity and Expert Activation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41487)**  `arXiv:2610.41487`  
  _Alexandros Christoforos, Chadbourne Davis, Paul Raj_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context diffusion repeatedly pays for both token interaction and feed-forward computation. We formulate these costs as two separate budgets: the edges available to an attention graph and the experts activated for each token. This view connects local, dilated, and global attention with conditional expert computation without assuming that sparsity alone preserves document-level information. We specify normalized masked attention, distinguish denoising depth from sampling iterations, and derive graph and compute bounds. A reported sparse-expert configuration, denoted DualBudget, is analyzed across scientific summarization, multi-hop question answering, dialogue, and sentence-pair prediction. All supplied table measurements are retained. The length study shows gains over the listed baselines through 16K, together with measurable degradation as length increases. Ablations reveal mixed changes across the three reported scores, while the cost record indicates a 20% reduction relative to DiffuSeq under an unspecified timing convention. The analysis separates these observations from unmeasured claims about training speed, absorbing states, global coherence, and deployment memory.
  </details>

- **[KEEPER: Learning Control-Sufficient Textual Memory for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41482)**  `arXiv:2610.41482`  
  _Jinkyeong Choi, Chaebin Jeong, Junsung Jeon, Donghyeon Park_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive textual memory can carry earlier errors into later updates, yet task performance alone does not establish memory reliability. We define self-consistency bias as the persistence of incorrect claims already present in the incoming memory. We present KEEPER, a comparative analysis of four auxiliary objectives along information-content and update-sensitivity axes within a shared recursive interface, examining how supervision targets relate to agent performance. Among evaluated conditions, reward-related prediction achieve the highest task performance before and after offline reinforcement learning, outperforming joint supervision. A claim-level analysis of one baseline trajectory documents error persistence despite corrective observations. Together, these analyses motivate evaluating error persistence and valid-information retention alongside task outcomes.
  </details>

- **[KV-Cache Patching: Tracing How and Where Fine-Tuned Behaviors Arise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41480)**  `arXiv:2610.41480`  
  _Jonas Loos, Oliver Eberle_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning a language model changes how it responds, but where is this fine-tuned behavior instantiated? To better understand this, we evaluate whether the change in model behavior arises only during the generation of the answer tokens where the change manifests, or whether altered processing of previous tokens causes it via the attention mechanism. For this, we patch the KV cache from a LoRA fine-tuned model into the unmodified base model, and vice versa. Our results show that for some behaviors, like responding in German or emergent misalignment, patching the KV cache can indeed transfer them from the fine-tuned to the base model. Correspondingly, patching the KV cache from base to fine-tune can partially or fully remove fine-tuned behavior. Furthermore, we find that the effect of patching grows with how much of the KV cache is patched and concentrates at a few pivotal positions, with system-prompt and chat-template tokens having disproportionate influence.
  </details>

- **[BlindToChange: AI Agents Can Find Changes When Asked But Do They Look When Needed?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41435)**  `arXiv:2610.41435`  
  _Anirudh Phukan, Tanya Goyal, Danish Pruthi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given that AI agents can search the web broadly and deeply in real time, it is tempting to conclude that their outputs account for recent world events. We show that this conclusion does not hold in practice. While agents can search and easily find recent changes, the correctness of their responses depends on whether they search and what they search for. Popular agentic search benchmarks like BrowseComp cannot expose failures wherein agents do not recognize their own assumptions that should be verified, since these benchmarks explicitly state what needs to be found. In contrast, many user requests state only the final information need (e.g., an itinerary for a trip), leaving the agent to discover relevant changes (e.g., the temporary closure of a museum) and account for their consequences. To highlight such gaps, we introduce BlindToChange, a benchmark that measures whether agents proactively discover recent events that affect their responses. Our benchmark comprises requests for recommendations where an obvious candidate is no longer applicable due to a recent real-world event.
