These events are drawn automatically from Wikidata, allowing BlindToChange to be periodically refreshed. Strikingly, we find a big gap -- as high as 27.5 points, even at high reasoning effort -- in systems' performance, when the recent event is not explicitly mentioned compared to when we ask about it directly. Analyzing search trajectories, we learn that more than half of the failures can be attributed to the fact that agents do not directly search to verify whether a relevant fact has changed. Our benchmark highlights, and tracks, a new axis of progress, wherein systems account for recent information that is not explicitly mentioned in the user request.
  </details>

- **[PsyInterviewee: Informational Invariance and Relational Adaptivity in Mental Health Interview Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41289)**  `arXiv:2610.41289`  
  _Jiawei Zhang, Huachuan Qiu, Ruocheng Guo, Zijun Shi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based simulated interviewees support interviewer training and the evaluation of mental health interviewing systems. Existing studies have primarily focused on improving human-likeness through richer patient profiles and behavioral modeling. However, human-likeness alone does not establish their reliability. Reliable simulation requires preserving profile-grounded clinical facts (informational invariance) while adapting disclosure and interaction behavior to the interview (relational adaptivity). To this end, we introduce PsyInterviewee, which separates a protected truth channel from a dynamic interaction channel and verifies generated clinical claims against the profile. Specifically, asymmetric write permissions allow dialogue context to update disclosure and behavioral states without authorizing new clinical facts; these states guide what profile-supported information is revealed and how it is expressed. In an automated evaluation of interactions between LLM-based interviewers and interviewees, PsyInterviewee achieves higher scores than four representative baselines on most metrics across both axes, as assessed by two LLM judges. Ablations suggest that the two channels contribute to clinical grounding and response modulation, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Response Scoring: An AI-aware Cognitive Supervision Framework for Automated Mathematical Short Answer Grading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41215)**  `arXiv:2610.41215`  
  _Shengran Guo, Min Chi, Feifei Qiao, Varad Patwardhan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformer-based language models have achieved remarkable success in automated short answer grading (ASAG). However, current methods in ASAG struggle to comprehend, reason about and evaluate complex mathematical answers involving symbols and formulas. These score-only methods fail to generalize across diverse responses and skill states associated with identical scores. Moreover, they ignore the potential upward bias introduced by generative Artificial Intelligence (AI) usage in assessing students' performance and knowledge mastery. To address these limitations, we propose AICG (AI-aware Cognitive-supervised Grading), a two-branch multi-task framework. The first branch combines RoBERTa-based regression, ordinal, and classification heads for individual-answer grading. The second branch uses an expert-designed Q-matrix to associate responses with assessed skills and jointly predicts G-DINA-derived skill mastery and learning gain. Through the shared encoder, these skill-level targets provide cognitive supervision for answer grading beyond score labels. AICG further uses a Gen-AI detector, trained through supervised contrastive pretraining followed by linear classification, to estimate assistance probabilities and convert them into reliability weights, attenuating unreliable cognitive evidence during learning assessment. We conduct experiments on 49,402 instructor-graded responses spanning 19 academic semesters. Relative to RoBERTa regression, AICG reduces mean absolute error (MAE) by 50.3% and improves quadratic weighted kappa (QWK) from 0.798 to 0.927. Ablations identify Q-matrix-guided cognitive supervision as the primary source of improvement, while AI-aware weighting further reduces overall MAE by 4.2% over the unweighted cognitive model and consistently improves MAE and QWK across the four most recent post-Gen-AI semesters.
  </details>

- **[TraceSpec: Understanding and Improving Speculative Decoding for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41177)**  `arXiv:2610.41177`  
  _Jihao Zhao, Shuaishuai Zu, Pengfei Li, Biao Qin, Guanghuan Fang, Ben Feng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents execute tasks through repeated model calls over evolving histories of reasoning, actions, and tool feedback. Aggregate speculative-decoding metrics merge these distinct decisions, providing limited guidance on where draft supervision is needed. We present TraceSpec, a framework connecting trajectory evaluation with adaptive draft training. It combines dynamic execution with fixed-prefix replay and introduces TraceSpec-Traj, a corpus of 10,000 training states and 2,500 test states spanning browsing and software engineering. Analysis of five speculative baselines reveals that aggregate acceptance can rise with context length while declining within matched late browsing actions. Workload and phase further distinguish these trends, motivating supervision guided by trajectory conditions. TraceSpec trains on intermediate assistant turns and allocates a fixed prediction budget using state-conditioned estimates of prefix-aware acceptance gaps. Across three target sizes and two temperatures, it improves replay throughput by 12.5% to 22.6% over its DSpark base. Qwen3-4B ablations show that adaptive allocation increases accepted length by 3.3% to 10.0% over fixed full-turn supervision, with additional comparisons substantiating the roles of state conditioning and consecutive-token agreement. The resulting evaluation resources, trajectory findings, and training strategy connect the analysis of agent execution to practical draft improvement.
  </details>

- **[S2TDR: Structured Multimodal Reconstruction for Sticker-Aware Dialogue Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41176)**  `arXiv:2610.41176`  
  _Yuanchen Shi, Fang Kong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Stickers are widely used in social media dialogues to express affect, stance, and intent beyond literal text. Their meanings are often non-literal and context-dependent, making them difficult to handle with generic visual fusion or standalone captioning. We propose Sticker-to-Text Dialogue Reconstruction (S2TDR), a structured multimodal reconstruction framework that converts sticker turns into contextually appropriate textual utterances. S2TDR decomposes sticker interpretation into visual cue extraction, affective-intent inference, and context-aware dialogue reconstruction, using OCR text only as an auxiliary cue when available. The reconstructed dialogue can then be consumed by existing text-based dialogue models. Experiments on sentiment analysis, intent recognition, and dialogue summarization show that S2TDR generally improves multiple downstream models over direct sticker integration methods. Further analyses on component ablations, OCR-empty stickers, and reconstruction quality indicate that structured reconstruction provides an interpretable and effective way to integrate sticker semantics into dialogue understanding.
  </details>

- **[HyperRepair: Recovering Forgotten General Capabilities of Fine-Tuned Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41147)**  `arXiv:2610.41147`  
  _Junrong Tang, Jianghui Wang, Hao Kong, Yang Lin, Yibo Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning improves a language model's performance in a target domain but can weaken its general capabilities. Existing methods often face a trade-off between general capabilities and domain specialization, making it difficult to preserve both. We introduce HyperRepair, a framework for recovering general capabilities while retaining domain specialization at only inference cost. To make recovery generalizable across models sharing a pretrained backbone, HyperRepair learns a hypernetwork from repairs trained on source models. The hypernetwork uses activation fingerprints that summarize changes from the pretrained backbone to generate a tailored Repairing Side Net (RSN), whose prompt-conditioned gate applies corrections selectively. This enables recovery on models from unseen adaptation domains without target-domain examples or additional optimization. We evaluate HyperRpair across two model families, 12 LoRA adaptation datasets, and 15 general benchmarks. On the default split, HyperRepair achieves 93.4% of the base models' average general score while retaining 99.5% of the fine-tuned models' average domain score.
  </details>

- **[Language Models Show a "Women-Are-Wonderful" Effect in Blame Attribution That is Not Just Leniency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41116)**  `arXiv:2610.41116`  
  _Anika Sharma, Snehalkumar 'Neil' S. Gaikwad, Snigdha Chaturvedi, Shashank Srivastava_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper investigates the Women-Are-Wonderful (WaW) effect in language-model blame attribution: a favorable bias in which women receive more positive evaluations than men. Using 11,270 interpersonal conflicts from r/AmItheAsshole, we construct matched counterfactuals that vary the gender of the person being judged (the original poster) and the person affected (the counter-party). Across four language models, gender changes a minority of verdicts, but those changes consistently favor women. The effect is strongly role-dependent. Making the original poster a woman reduces the odds of blame by 9--52%, while making the counter-party a woman multiplies them by 2.7--9.2X. Counter-party WaW also varies across conflict types, strengthening in communication and relationship conflicts and weakening in family conflicts. Exploratory probes suggest that counter-party gender can shift judgments of sympathy, reasonableness, harm and protection. WaW is therefore not just leniency toward women being judged: models also judge conduct more harshly when it affects a woman.
  </details>

- **[EDU-Jev Training System-One Decision Models for Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41079)**  `arXiv:2610.41079`  
  _Jiazheng Li, Yulan He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many automated decisions in education involve choosing from a fixed set of options: assigning a rubric score, checking whether an answer meets a marking criterion, or identifying the first wrong step in a solution. Generative language models are increasingly used to make these decisions, but their responses require parsing, longer outputs increase inference costs, and confidence expressed in words does not necessarily reflect the probability of being correct. We present EDU-Jev, a family of single-pass ("System One") decision models at 0.8B, 2B and 4B parameters. Given an assessment context and a question with predefined answer options, the model returns a probability distribution over those options without generating text. A pointer head scores a marker for each option, ensuring the predictions are restricted to the allowed choices. Training uses a single supervised stage with soft cross-entropy against the available target label distributions; calibration is assessed empirically. Across twelve education benchmarks, EDU-Jev 4B achieves an average accuracy of 67.6%, exceeding the strongest of five commercial decision APIs by 9.4 percentage points and the best open 4B decision model by 18.7 points. On four cross-domain education sets, it matches the strongest API, which remains ahead on general decision-making and reasoning tasks. The 2B model processes about 150 decisions per second on a single H100 GPU.
  </details>

- **[Language Models Show Conformity Bias in RAG: When the Majority Endorses a Poisoned Claim](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41012)**  `arXiv:2610.41012`  
  _Mehrdad Ghassabi, Audrina Ebrahimi, Zahra Zojaji, Amin Milani Fard_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can expose language models to misleading evidence in retrieved documents. We investigate whether increasing the number of mutually agreeing poisoned documents affects a model's ability to detect misinformation. We evaluate 200 randomly selected questions from SciFact (random seed 38), comparing contexts with one poisoned and six healthy documents against contexts with six mutually agreeing poisoned documents and one healthy document. For Gemma-3-12b, poison-detection rates decrease from 25% (50/200) in the minority condition to 10% (20/200) in the majority condition. Among the 38 questions with different outcomes across conditions, 34 change from detected to undetected, while only four change in the opposite direction (exact McNemar test, p = 6.04e-7). This pattern is consistent with a conformity-like response to a majority of mutually agreeing poisoned documents. Our results provide initial evidence of conformity bias in RAG-based misinformation detection: Gemma-3-12b is substantially less likely to detect poisoned claims when they dominate the retrieved evidence.
  </details>

- **[Do Open Language Models Invent Scripture? BILAL-BENCH: Benchmarking Islamic-Source Fabrication in Bengali](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40996)**  `arXiv:2610.40996`  
  _Sabah Nushra, Md Kishor Morol_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a language model invents a Qur'anic verse or hadith, it gives false authority to text that readers may treat as sacred. Existing Islamic hallucination benchmarks are largely Arabic and often closed-ended, while Bengali resources mainly study general-domain hallucination or factual knowledge. We introduce BILAL-BENCH, a 240-question expert-verified benchmark for open-ended Islamic-source fabrication in Bengali. It covers Qur'anic attribution, hadith, fiqh, contemporary issues, and adversarial false-premise prompts, and uses a two-axis rubric that separates source errors from assertiveness. We evaluate seven open-weight models from 1B to 120B on 1,680 double-annotated responses. Six of the seven models confidently fabricate on 79.3-95.3% of source-production questions, with no monotonic improvement from scale. Hadith provenance is the weakest category across models: only two of 315 responses are correct and correctly sourced. The two 8B BanglaLLaMA variants have similar confident-fabrication rates but different error profiles. Models vary far more in whether they withhold a claim they cannot support than in source accuracy: appropriate abstention or false-premise correction ranges from 6 to 46 responses across the benchmark, and does not track parameter count.
  </details>

- **[Fewer Heads, Fewer Memories: Pruning Redundant Attention Heads Suppresses Verbatim Memorisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40974)**  `arXiv:2610.40974`  
  _Aryan Chowdhury, Riza Batista-Navarro_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have been shown to memorise and reproduce verbatim sequences from their training data, raising concerns about privacy leakage, intellectual property exposure, and the limits of generalisation. Recent theoretical work suggests that attention heads may contribute to this behaviour by functioning as associative memory mechanisms, with memorisation capacity scaling with the number of heads. At the same time, empirical work has shown that many attention heads are redundant and can be pruned with limited impact on downstream performance. Despite these parallel findings, the effect of pruning redundant heads on memorised-sequence reproduction remains unexplored. In this work, we demonstrate that zeroing out a substantial fraction of attention heads prior to inference can significantly suppress verbatim memorisation while preserving, or even improving, language modelling performance on the benchmark used to identify redundant heads. Evaluating GPT-2-XL(1.5B) on known memorised sequences, we find that pruning 729 task-redundant heads reduces the memorisation count by 91.5%, while improving WikiText-103 perplexity by 0.92. These findings suggest that attention-head redundancy can be exploited to mitigate memorisation at inference time, without retraining or modifying the training data.
  </details>

- **[Better Retrievers, Worse Labels: How Incomplete Relevance Judgments Distort Abstention Evaluation in Conversational RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40966)**  `arXiv:2610.40966`  
  _Ivan Kochergin, Vasily Konovalov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational RAG benchmarks such as MTRAG and RETECO evaluate retrieval and generation end to end: each system answers from its own retrieval, so abstention must be scored against whether each retrieved context is sufficient. A convenient shortcut reads sufficiency off the relevance judgments and treats a context without gold documents as unanswerable. We re-run the official baselines exactly and test this shortcut on RECOR (RETECO track~2) with four retrievers (nDCG@10 from .18 to .59) and three Qwen3 readers. Two open LLM judges and a panel of four small open models label each context by content, and a model of another family audits 250 contexts blind, with adjudication. The stronger the retriever, the larger the share of no-gold contexts that still contain the answer (28\% to 72\%) and the lower the shortcut's precision (.68 to .10; .70 to .21 under the audit). A reader agrees: Qwen3-4B answers 62--67\% of these false misses correctly, against 23--30\% of no-gold contexts judged insufficient and 1\% with no documents. In most false misses, the answer comes from a hard negative mined for the same question, another turn's gold or a neighbouring chunk of the gold page. On MTRAG, whose judgments come from annotators, precision drops from .72 to .50. Under the shortcut, answerability AUROC is up to .19 too low, and the best sufficiency detector appears to be a retrieval score that never reads the documents. We release code, runs and labels, and recommend scoring refusals against content labels, not the presence of gold.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Personalization Under Preference Epistemic Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40930)**  `arXiv:2610.40930`  
  _Priyanka Dey, Rajarshi Das, Vikas Bahirwani, Qi Zeng, Julia Kruk, Qi Qi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human preferences change over time: an established preference may remain valid over time, become uncertain as context shifts, or be explicitly revised. Effective personalization requires conversational AI to adapt its response strategy to evolving preferences rather than relying on passive memory retrieval. We formalize this capability as adaptive personalization under epistemic uncertainty. A human preference study further confirms that people favor state-contingent response strategies as preference uncertainty changes. To evaluate whether language models mirror this adaptation, we create a multilingual benchmark spanning 250 personas, 5 languages, and 25K conversational turns that decouples strategy selection from response execution. Across eight closed- and open-weight models, we uncover a recognition-generation gap: models reliably identify appropriate strategies when selecting among candidate options, but collapse during open-ended generation, suffering a 35-46% drop in strategy calibration. Crucially, this failure is systematic: models default to exploit-first behavior, over-relying on existing preferences regardless of preference epistemic uncertainty. We show this failure is addressable: while prompting interventions fail, targeted training that supervises models' internal strategy representations toward state-conditional human preferences narrows the user acceptability generalization gap by a third on out-of-distribution personas and conversational styles.
  </details>

- **[CoFiBreak: A Coarse-to-Fine Diversity Reward for Effective Multi-Turn Jailbreak](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40926)**  `arXiv:2610.40926`  
  _Jialin Song, Xiaodong Liu, Weiwei Yang, Mingqian Feng, Chengquan Guo, Qiyao Ma, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated red teaming trains an attacker large language model (LLM) to elicit harmful responses from a victim LLM, but reinforcement learning can collapse attackers onto a narrow set of prompts when attack success is the only reward. Such a collapse of attack diversity can bias safety evaluation toward repeatedly testing near-duplicate attack patterns rather than the underlying harmful intents. Existing methods often trade off attack success or inference cost for greater content or strategy diversity. We propose CoFiBreak, a coarse-to-fine diversity reward for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) that improves attack diversity while preserving strong attack effectiveness and low inference cost. The coarse reward broadens which strategies the attacker uses by encouraging strategies underrepresented within each GRPO group, while the fine reward broadens how those strategies are realized by discouraging reuse of the same personas, framings, and output formats after harmful-intent information is removed from prompt embeddings. Diversity rewards apply only to attacks that already exceed an effectiveness threshold, so diversity cannot be gained through ineffective attacks. Since both rewards are computed only during training, CoFiBreak also retains inference efficiency. Trained with Llama3.1-8B-Instruct as the victim, CoFiBreak discovers 1.6-2.0x as many distinct successful fine-grained strategies as the strongest baseline across frontier victims on HarmBench. It reaches 98.8% ASR@10 (attack success rate with 10 generations per intent) on AdvBench with the highest diversity score among all compared methods, which is 2.1x that of the strongest-attacking baseline. The resulting attacks transfer to unseen frontier LLMs, reaching 71.0% ASR@10 on AdvBench with the unseen victim Claude-Opus-4.6. The code will be released.
  </details>

- **[The Generative Gap Between Speculative Drafter and Continuous Diffusion Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40847)**  `arXiv:2610.40847`  
  _Yujian Chen, Hao Jiang, Guang Cheng, Weijian Luo, Tianyang Hu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous diffusion language models (CDLMs) and speculative drafters offer efficient language generation but are typically evaluated differently: CDLMs generate independently through iterative refinement, whereas drafters propose text for target-model verification and correction. We compare their architectures without verification, using LangFlow for CDLMs and adapting DSpark's Markov and recurrent-head architectures for standalone generation. All models are trained from scratch for unconditional, single-block OpenWebText generation at lengths 16, 128, and 1024, without target features or verification, using common preprocessing, backbone dimensions, and training-update budgets. We evaluate generation perplexity (GenPPL) with an external language model and distributional similarity to reference text with MAUVE. With default denoising steps equal to sequence length, LangFlow outperforms both DSpark variants on both metrics at lengths 128 and 1024, while DSpark-RNN leads at 16. A conditional KL analysis characterizes dependencies excluded by the Markov head's previous-token conditioning. Recurrent memory removes this restriction and improves both metrics, but the longer-sequence gap persists. Teacher probability matching further lowers Markov GenPPL without observed MAUVE gains. Increasing LangFlow's denoising steps at fixed weights improves both metrics at lengths 16 and 128, changing the length-128 ordering relative to DSpark-RNN. These results establish LangFlow's longer-sequence advantage under the evaluated budgets and highlight iterative full-sequence refinement as a route to improving standalone generation beyond gains from recurrent head memory.
  </details>

- **[Mage-VL-Cowork: A Full Training Recipe to Learn Hybrid Computer Use From Evolved Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40747)**  `arXiv:2610.40747`  
  _Xiaoyi Zhang, Haoqing Wang, Xinjie Zhang, Xiang Ming, Xing Wang, Senqiao Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective computer-use agents (CUAs) should seamlessly combine visual GUI interaction with code and terminal execution within a single policy. We present Mage-VL-Cowork, a 4B hybrid CUA trained from a general multimodally aligned checkpoint through an end-to-end, progressive five-stage curriculum across midtraining, SFT, and RL. To power this curriculum at scale, Mage-CUA-Forge replaces per-task agent construction with reusable, skill-conditioned generation workflows and application-specific preparers, synthesizing 293,806 desktop and web tasks across 6,915 skills while reducing recurring per-task expenditure by 82.6%. Rather than directly distilling a default teacher, we evolve the teacher harness and instantiate specialized CUA harnesses to yield high-quality trajectories alongside stage-specific supervision. Mage-VL-Cowork-4B finally climbs to 66.0% on OSWorld-Verified through different training stages with more efficient reasoning cost, achieving state-of-the-art performance at its parameter size and pushing forward the frontier of on-device intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Li$^{2}$SAO: Lifting Light Singl-Rollout Asynchronous RL Optimization via Group-Relative Actor-Critic Bootstrapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40726)**  `arXiv:2610.40726`  
  _Zichen Bai, Yanli Wang, Yang Liu, Junhao Shi, Xueqin Liu, Jinguo Sun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Asynchronous reinforcement learning (RL) is becoming a mainstream paradigm for post-training large language models (LLMs), as it improves training efficiency by updating the model as rollouts arrive. Recent work argues that group-wise sampling in GRPO does not naturally fit asynchronous RL training because rollout lengths follow a long-tailed distribution, and proposes Single-rollout Asynchronous Optimization (SAO) to improve stability and off-policy behavior, especially on agentic tasks. However, SAO relies on a closed-source value model obtained from scaling value pretraining. In this paper, we analyze GRPO and LiSAO, our lightweight reproduction of SAO without the pretrained value model, on single-turn reasoning and multi-turn agentic tasks. We find that GRPO outperforms LiSAO on reasoning tasks, while the reverse holds on agentic tasks. Since the value model of LiSAO is not yet well trained , LiSAO lags behind GRPO substantially in early training steps. To address this issue, we propose Li$^{2}$SAO, which Lifts Light Single-rollout Asynchronous Optimization with group-relative actor-critic bootstrapping: while the critic is cold, a group-relative advantage trains the actor and a group-centered return trains the critic, after which training switches to single-rollout GAE. Experiments show that Li$^{2}$SAO catches up with or surpasses GRPO in early training and further raises the later performance ceiling. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Unlimited OCR Works 2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39308)**  `arXiv:2610.39308`  
  _Shu Wei, Jingjing Wu, Qunyi Xie, Lingshu Zhang, Xiangcheng Du, Yannian Fu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlimited OCR ushers the era of long-horizon parsing, substantially mitigating the growth of working memory as document lengthens. However, as document length scales to hundreds of pages, the visual context grows substantially, sharply increasing the computational cost of each decoding step and limiting the scalability of ultra-long document parsing. Inspired by human book transcription via localized focus without re-scanning preceding pages, we introduce Unlimited OCR 2, a document parsing model that looks only at what it transcribes. Building on Unlimited OCR, we upgrade all the decoder attention modules to Sparse Reference Sliding Window Attention (SR-SWA), bounding active reference tokens to a fixed capacity and thus sustaining constant computational costs across arbitrarily long documents. By combining SR-SWA with Unlimited-Horizon Decoding Paradigm, Unlimited OCR 2 enables document parsing over virtually unlimited lengths without prohibitive memory overhead. At the hundred-page scale, Unlimited OCR 2 achieves 7.0$\times$ and 5.6$\times$ per-step speedups over Full Attention and R-SWA, respectively. Furthermore, it delivers state-of-the-art end-to-end performance on both MPDocBench and MarathonDocBench, our newly proposed benchmark targeting hundred-page document parsing. Ultimately, the capacity for uninterrupted, one-shot document parsing signifies a fundamental paradigm shift into the OCR 3.0 era.
  </details>

- **[Tracing the Influence of Retrieved Literature on Scientific Ideation in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39060)**  `arXiv:2610.39060`  
  _Zhipin Wang, Koel Dutta Chowdhury, Christoph Leiter, Steffen Eger_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate research ideas from scientific literature. Existing systems combine literature retrieval with search, generation, and refinement, making it difficult to determine how the retrieval strategy itself shapes the ideas generated. We isolate this contribution by comparing five retrieval strategies and a No Literature baseline under a shared generation protocol, using 45,000 ideas across 15 machine learning topics and ten open-weight models. Relative to no literature basline, all five retrieval strategies reduce diversity among ideas generated from the same set of papers by $5.6\%$--$17.9\%$, while most increase diversity by $10.2\%$--$18.1\%$ when ideas from different sets are pooled. Effects on idea quality and novelty are modest on average but vary across topics. We further apply a JSD-based attribution method to trace the influence of individual input papers on each idea's motivation, core idea, approach, and validation. In $79.3\%$ of ideas, only one or two distinct papers receive the highest attribution scores across these four parts. These leading sources often recur across ideas generated from the same set of papers, and groups with more varied source attribution contain more diverse ideas ($r=0.70$). These findings motivate various sets of literature and the use of attribution to guide the choice of sources for subsequent ideas.
  </details>

- **[When Support Crosses the Line: Evaluating Ethical Boundaries in LLM-Based Mental Health Support in the Chinese Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38452)**  `arXiv:2610.38452`  
  _Bichen Wang, Yufeng Jin, Yixin Sun, Yanyan Zhao, Bing Qin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored for mental health support, yet existing benchmarks primarily focus on supportive capabilities rather than whether models consistently adhere to professional ethical boundaries. This issue is particularly important in multi-turn interactions, where users may introduce new information or exert emotional pressure over time. We introduce MindEth-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating ethical boundary adherence in multi-turn mental health interactions. Grounded in professional ethical standards for mental health services in China, MindEth-Bench covers multiple dimensions of ethical conduct and uses Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to systematically explore interaction trajectories that stress-test model boundaries. Experiments across nine LLMs show that models that initially respond appropriately can shift toward ethical violations over extended interactions, with substantial variation across models and ethical dimensions. These results show that single-turn evaluations may overestimate the ethical reliability of LLM-based mental health support systems and highlight the need to evaluate ethical behavior throughout multi-turn interaction.
  </details>

- **[Holistic Diversity Management in LLM-Guided Evolutionary Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38407)**  `arXiv:2610.38407`  
  _Seockbean Song, Youngsik Yoon, Sungjae Lee, Siwei Wang, Wei Chen, Jungseul Ok_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-guided evolutionary search iteratively selects existing programs, uses LLMs as mutation operators to generate a new program, and evaluates it to guide subsequent search. To preserve diversity and reduce the risk of premature convergence, it often adopts the island model, in which groups of programs, called islands, evolve separately. However, this structure alone does not guarantee distinct search directions, as islands may start from similar programs or converge toward similar solutions during evolution. This suggests that diversity must be considered throughout the search process, from initializing islands with distinct programs to developing island trajectories and sharing discoveries across islands. To this end, we propose HoliDivEvolve , a holistic approach to establishing and effectively utilizing island diversity throughout evolutionary search. Specifically, we introduce diverse initialization to establish different initial programs, trajectory adaptive island selection to allocate computation based on current score, recent growth, and the remaining search budget, and growth adaptive migration to share alternative ideas across islands. Experiments across diverse benchmarks show that HoliDivEvolve achieves competitive performance on most evaluated tasks. We further conduct controlled analyses to examine how our design choices affect search behavior and final performance.
  </details>

- **[Keepace.ai: A Technical Report on a Large Language Model for Sports and Health](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38249)**  `arXiv:2610.38249`  
  _Keepace.ai Team, Bowen Wu, Heng Wang, Jufeng Mi, Kang Liu, Kun Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models hold promise for delivering safe, scientifically grounded, and personalized exercise guidance at scale, yet the sports-and-health domain still lacks curated training corpora and rigorous evaluation benchmarks. Keepace (this http URL) is a domain-specialized LLM built on Qwen3.5-27B through post-training, currently optimized for Chinese-language exercise guidance across three tasks: workout plan generation, sports-and-health knowledge QA, and personal sports data analysis. In internal rubric-based evaluations designed with domain experts, Keepace outperforms its base model and larger general-purpose baselines, with the most pronounced improvements in exercise safety, boundary compliance, structured output completeness, and scientific accuracy. This report summarizes the current design, data pipeline, safety-first evaluation framework, and evaluation results of Keepace.
  </details>

- **[Metacognitive Decoding: Efficient Inference for Diffusion Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37642)**  `arXiv:2610.37642`  
  _Mingyu Zhao, Shuhang Lin, Ligong Han, Xiaojing Yu, Zhenting Wang, Yifei Pang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models offer a promising path to efficient agents through parallel token generation. However, efficient interaction requires adapting both where computation is spent and how much text is generated. We introduce a metacognitive controller that guides diffusion generation according to the needs of the current response and the agent's interaction with the environment. It learns when to finalize tokens and how far generation should extend, reducing overall computation within responses. Our controller, a lightweight plug-in for the diffusion backbone, is trained on agent trajectories to balance correctness and generation cost. This approach connects the agent's generation process to the interaction the agent serves. Experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate that our approach reduces inference FLOPs by 20.6% on average compared with the most efficient baseline, while maintaining competitive task accuracy. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of our approach.
  </details>

- **[Adapting LLM-SC to Living Sacred Heritage: Domain-Specific Extraction, Semantic Networks and Human Evaluation on Pashupatinath Temple Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35685)**  `arXiv:2610.35685`  
  _Pujan Pant, Sagar Newpane, Hari Sitaula, Manish Sakhakarmy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) pipelines can turn visitor reviews into structured data, but generic schemes may miss rituals, sacred spaces, cultural restrictions and emotionally mixed experiences at living sacred heritage sites. We adapted the LLM-Social Computing (LLM-SC) framework to Pashupatinath Temple, Nepal, by specifying a 12-type entity taxonomy (four original LLM-SC types plus eight sacred-heritage types), four relation types and nine aspect-sentiment dimensions in a GPT-4o extraction prompt, which was applied to a fixed random sample of 2,500 English-language Tripadvisor reviews from a processed corpus of 3,939. The exports contain 11,286 entity, 6,232 aspect-sentiment and 5,590 relation records, covering 2,485, 2,487 and 2,433 reviews, respectively; the eight added sacred-heritage types account for 64.8% of entity records. Against an adjudicated two-annotator reference for 200 reviews, exact normalized surface-and-type matching gave provisional entity scores of 41.0% precision, 21.8% recall and 28.4% micro-F1. Aspect-presence F1 was 53.6% and end-to-end aspect-plus-polarity F1 was 42.6%. Human adjudication supported 153 of 194 first-listed relation candidates (one per review; 78.9%), a candidate-level rate that does not estimate relation recall or whole-graph precision. The contribution is a culturally grounded, domain-specific adaptation with component-wise human evaluation and explicit reproducibility and generalization limitations.
  </details>

- **[SOPO: Socratic Guided Policy Optimization for Span-Level Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32212)**  `arXiv:2610.32212`  
  _Jiawei Dong, Zilong Bai, Pengtian zhu, Peng Zhou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have gained widespread adoption in various natural language processing tasks, but suffer from hallucination issues where they generate unfaithful or inconsistent content. While Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has shown promising applications in reasoning tasks, its application to span-level hallucination detection is challenged by localization ambiguity, reward sparsity on hard examples, and uncontrolled length inflation. To address these issues, we introduce Socratic Guided Policy Optimization (SOPO), a multi-turn RL framework for robust hallucination detection. The key innovation of SOPO is a Socratic feedback mechanism, where a teacher model guides the reasoning trajectories during rollout by providing indirect hints inferred from the ground truth. This teacher-guided approach enables the model to generate more effective reasoning trajectories for hard examples, thus alleviating reward sparsity. Furthermore, we incorporate Turn-based Advantage Scaling to penalize verbosity bias, encouraging the model to develop efficient and autonomous reasoning capabilities. Empirical results on RAGTruth demonstrate that SOPO (4B) achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing leading proprietary models (e.g., GPT-5) and standard RL baselines, while obtaining higher reported mean scores than GRPO on four out-of-domain benchmarks, with mixed evidence against CAPO. We release our training and evaluation code alongside the 1.7B and 4B SOPO models atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Table Serialization Determines Which Relations Language Models Preserve](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24798)**  `arXiv:2610.24798`  
  _Junwei Ma, Arun Kumar Chithanar, Palash Goyal, Chul Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study how text serialization affects table lookup in instruction-tuned language models. Our diagnostic asks the model to find the row containing a specified value under one named column, then return the value under another named column in that same row. We render the same synthetic 256-cell tables in row-major form and in column-major form with each header placed beside its values, while varying the table aspect ratio across five shapes. Across three models, the better format changes with table shape: row-major performs better on tall tables, whereas column-major performs better on wide tables. At 64 x 4, row-major leads by 42.3-54.0 percentage points; at 4 x 64, column-major leads by 35.0-59.0 points. Mapping wrong answers back to their source cells reveals a complementary error pattern: row-major tends to preserve the correct row but lose the target column, while column-major tends to preserve the correct column but lose the row. We use the term relational locality to describe this pattern: serialization keeps one relation locally available while making the other depend on positional alignment. A separate non-tabular ordering experiment supports this interpretation. Thus, apparent width-related failures in text-serialized tables depend on linearization, not table shape alone.
  </details>

- **[DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561)**  `arXiv:2610.21561`  
  _Sergio Burdisso, Esaú Villatoro-Tello, Dairazalia Sánchez-Cortés, Yanis Labrak, Jordi Luque, Milos Cernak, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. We release DialFork under open licenses atthis https URL, hoping the community will measure conversational process alongside content.
  </details>

- **[Gluing Local Contexts into Global Meaning: A Sheaf-Theoretic Decomposition of Transformer Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17117)**  `arXiv:2610.17117`  
  _Bryce Grant, Peng Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prior research into interpretability does not test whether a representation stays consistent under paraphrasing. Each pair of paraphrases can be thought of as a local witness that two activations should be the same. From these local witnesses we can build a cellular sheaf on the paraphrase graph. The question of what is consistent under paraphrasing can be answered by the Laplacian $L_\mathcal{F}$ given a frozen model. No training required. Our kernel $H^0$ gives us a content subspace. We show that with only 20 dims the kernel outperforms the subspace that LEACE retains with all dims on held-out CounterFact fact retrieval by $+17.1$\,pp on average across 8 models. By constraining all paraphrases of a fact together we see an additional increase of $+6.5$\,pp. Our spectral complement shows us where the causal influence is concentrated. Ablating the coordinates that vary the most when paraphrased (which approximates ablating the spectral complement at the coordinate level) leads to $5.6$ to $26.5\times$ larger changes in model outputs compared to ablating coordinates with matched per-coordinate variability. We find this across 9 models from 124M to 13B params. By stacking layers we can create a grid which has $b_1 = N(L-1)$ algebraic 1-cycles. The harmonic mass derived from the model varies by nearly 3 orders of magnitude between the 6 architectures. It also tracks the order of steering fragility (Spearman's $\rho = 0.90$, exact one-sided permutation $p = 0.014$). The kernel, its spectral complement and the harmonic mass all come from the same paraphrase graph. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Where Is the "Self" of a Large Language Model? -- From Self-Index to Self-Reference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16717)**  `arXiv:2610.16717`  
  _Yuchen Li, Xintong Li, Zongqi Fan, Ken-tye Yong, Nguyen H. Tran_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whether a large language model has a self is usually argued without a way to measure it. We turn the question into the measurement of a functional self with two levels familiar from biology: a self-index, which marks material as the model's own and shapes how it is processed, much as nearly all animals tag self-generated signals with a corollary discharge, and self-reference, the ability to recognize what is one's own, as in mirror self-recognition. Each level has criteria that can be tested in any open-weight model. We place the same text in an Assistant, a User, or a System message of the model's own chat template. In Qwen3.8-27B, the representation singles out the model's own message from both external sources, as predicted in advance for early layers, and does so at every one of its 64 layers and in every held-out text, while User and System stay close to each other; asked who wrote a message, the model claims its own (0.77) and assigns both external sources to the user. In three models and four readouts (Qwen3.8-27B without and with thinking, gpt-oss-20b, and gpt-oss-120b), replacing eight coordinates per layer of the model's own message by those of the same text as a User message removes the claim (to at most 0.05), installing them into a User message creates it, and random subspaces of the same dimension do neither; the coordinates are read mainly at the end of the message, before the last third of the layers, and editing them leaves answers about the content of the message and an unrelated multiplication unchanged. Across eighteen open-weight models from nine developers, the two levels are nested: no model recognizes its own messages without a self-index, and neither level follows model size or published benchmark scores. Together, these results support the account that self-reference is a readout of a self-index.
  </details>

- **[Thinking Inertia: LLMs Keep Thinking When Told Not To](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11765)**  `arXiv:2610.11765`  
  _Dianqiao Lei, Kevin Qinghong Lin, Pan Lu, Philip Torr, James Zou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly ship with explicit "thinking modes", yet their counterpart, "no-thinking", has received far less attention. We study LLMs' no-thinking behavior along two axes. a. How to measure no-thinking? Prior work typically defines no-thinking through proxies such as a disabled thinking mode or the absence of long traces. These proxies are unreliable: disabled thinking modes may still emit reasoning, while long traces may contain filler rather than genuine inference. We instead normalize each response into a pre-answer trace and final answer, and evaluate it at three levels: (i) Empty-Thinking Rate for strict answer-only compliance; (ii) instruction-aware Question-Pre-answer Relevance for similarity between the question and pre-answer trace; and (iii) LLM-as-judge Explicit Inference Rate for visible explicit inference. Together, these metrics distinguish answer-only output, relevant but non-inferential text, and explicit inference. b. How does no-thinking vary across tasks and models? We evaluate six prompting interventions on six LLMs across Boolean, multiple-choice, and open-ended questions. We find that explicit no-think controls cannot reliably eliminate visible inference. Models instead exhibit "Thinking Inertia": explicit inference persists even under strict controls and becomes more prevalent as the answer space opens. Accuracy remains stable on Boolean and multiple-choice tasks, whereas open-ended tasks reveal a trade-off between answer-only compliance and task accuracy. Rewriting the same questions across answer spaces shows that supplying candidate answers makes answer-only responses easier to produce. These findings establish no-thinking as a non-trivial capability: stopping explicit reasoning cannot be assumed from model settings or instructions alone and deserves systematic evaluation alongside reasoning ability.
  </details>

- **[Instrument-Anchored LLM Judges Transfer Expert Counseling-Quality Ratings Across Three German Corpora](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08055)**  `arXiv:2610.08055`  
  _Tobias Hallmen, Elisabeth André_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic assessment of communication quality in counseling conversations is bottlenecked by data: expert-rated corpora are small and expensive to grow. Therefore we study cross-domain prediction of the expert's overall impression across three German simulated-counseling corpora from one university (two general-practice medical, one parent-teacher; $n=195$ sessions). Features are construct scores that four small LLM judges assign from the transcript, drawn from the experts' own rating instruments, plus a compact nonverbal block; all models are open-weight and run locally, preserving data protection. Trained on the other two corpora, this stack predicts the held-out corpus at Spearman $\rho = .533$; in-domain training on the same sessions falls about a fifth short ($.420$; paired gap $+.113$, 95% CI $[+.067, +.157]$), and the gap holds at matched training size and on identical test sessions. The signal lies in what is said: the four judges alone transfer at $.523$, and the nonverbal block adds $+.01$ (CI includes zero). Asking a judge for instrument constructs instead of generic dialogue qualities raises its transfer by about 30% ($.32$ to $.42$); restoring speaker attribution where per-speaker audio was lost is worth $+.08$ to $+.10$ on that fold. Where a program's own ratings are scarce, other programs' data is a viable training set and language carries it.
  </details>

- **[The Depth Flow of Token Representations Is Nonlinear and Does Not Descend Its Own Density](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29706)**  `arXiv:2608.29706`  
  _Alexandre Quemy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A token's representation is carried through the network layer by layer. The whole vocabulary carried together forms a flow. We fit this flow's equation of motion as a discrete Langevin model over corpus-mean trajectories of Pythia-160M and Pythia-410M, and score the predicted steps on held-out tokens. Linear maps are often used as cheap surrogates for a layer. The flow they summarize is not linear: a quadratic drift beats the linear map at every transition of both models, and the Kramers-Moyal estimator agrees wherever its neighborhoods stay local. At full width, the step of a mean trajectory is nearly affine, 3% non-affine, while the step of an individual state is not, 17%. We then characterize the flow further. First, we show that it does not descend its own log-density, whatever the diffusion matrix. The drift instead descends a potential that is not the density. Second, the rotational component is not negligible, 4 to 45% of the explainable drift, and it is the antisymmetric part of the linear map. Finally, a token keeps its angular rank and its local-density rank across all thirteen layers, on two corpora and both models.
  </details>

- **[You Only Pass Once: Answering and Abstaining Together in a Single Forward Pass of a Frozen Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14465)**  `arXiv:2608.14465`  
  _Ziyang Luo, Zhongyao Chu, Xinjie He, Youting Wang, Xukui Qin, Runxiong Wu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A frozen language model under-uses the evidence in its hidden states and answers confidently when the context contains no answer. We fix both on the residual stream without changing any weight. For accuracy, a small trained network (the probe) adds an input-dependent vector to the residual at several middle layers; it raises Qwen2.5-1.5B on $\alpha$NLI from 51% to 73% accuracy. For abstention, a fixed vector (the direction), the mean hidden state of contexts that contain the answer minus that of contexts that do not, scores the hidden state; a low score abstains. On four QA datasets with human answerability labels (Qwen2.5-7B), moving the direction to a dataset it was not fit on costs under 3 points of decision accuracy (from 0.83 in domain) on average and still beats asking the model whether its context suffices; the same judgment trained as a classifier into the probe's activation edit or into LoRA weights loses 16 and 22 points and ends below asking. We set out to put both functions on a single forward pass, and found that the probe's edit changes the very hidden state the direction scores, degrading it. A small map -- trained without labels, only to reconstruct the unedited hidden state from the edited one -- removes 63% of that interference on 1.5B. Refit on the hidden-state pairs of LoFiT or LoReFT, the same recipe repairs those methods too: the steering module is replaceable. The resulting system, YOPO, answers and abstains in one forward pass. On $\alpha$NLI it raises three-way accuracy from 0.38 to about 0.75 on 1.5B, and stays within 0.02 of a two-pass reference on 1.5B, 3B and 7B, at half the cost. We also observe improvements on the four QA datasets, where answerability is human-labeled and answers are generated as free-form text. Near-parity with the two-pass reference holds across nine backbones spanning six model families without backbone-specific hyperparameter tuning.
  </details>

- **[The No-Meaning Falsity: The Structural Impossibility of the Arbitrary Sign in Classical Arabic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07737)**  `arXiv:2608.07737`  
  _Elnaserledinellah Mahmoud Abdelwahab_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper investigates whether the postmodern claim of unrestricted semantic indeterminacy, and its foundational Saussurean axiom of the arbitrary sign, are compatible with the structural architecture of Classical Arabic. We develop a formal mathematical model of Arabic non concatenative morphology in which lexical meaning is determined by the interaction between an invariant root and a morphosyntactic pattern. Within this framework, we establish a Morphological Correspondence Theorem, demonstrating that every lexical item is uniquely generated by a root pattern pair, and a Semantic Localization Theorem, proving that lexical meaning is determined at the derivational level prior to surface realization. To address Saussurean weaker notion of relative arbitrariness, we formalize it via conditional Kolmogorov complexity, defining arbitrariness algorithmically as the no rule property. We prove that general relative arbitrariness is formally undecidable, while Arabic relative arbitrariness is decidable and provably less than 1 for its motivated signifiers (Levels W and M), establishing a strict system complexity asymmetry over Indo-European languages.
  </details>

- **[CogBattery: Human-Referenced Evaluation of Cognitive Task Behavior and Structure in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24999)**  `arXiv:2607.24999`  
  _Dengzhe Hou, Lingyu Jiang, Fangzhou Lin, Kazunori D Yamada_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human cognitive tasks let us compare language models with humans under controlled conditions. Yet a model can reproduce the direction of a human effect while making different errors. CogBattery evaluates 54 open-weight language models on text adaptations of 13 cognitive paradigms, comparing each with human data from its source study. Like humans, models respond more to n-back lures than to controls, yet they make far more false alarms on ordinary non-targets. Most models' reported confidence separates correct from incorrect answers less well than humans' does, and most always choose the same wager, including models that classify accurately. These patterns span many model families. Across tasks, model scores share two dimensions. The first is closely tied to scale, yet larger models are not consistently closer to humans. Predefined cognitive groupings show no reliable predictive advantage over random groupings of the same size. These results show that matching human effect directions does not establish human-like behavior. CogBattery provides condition-level human references for testing errors and confidence reports in new models.
  </details>

- **[EvoMemBench: Benchmarking Agent Memory from a Self-Evolving Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18421)**  `arXiv:2605.18421`  
  _Yuyao Wang, Zhongjian Zhang, Mo Chi, Kaichi Yu, Yuhan Li, Miao Peng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent benchmarks for Large Language Model (LLM) agents mainly evaluate reasoning, planning, and execution. However, memory is also essential for agents, as it enables them to store, update, and retrieve information over time. This ability remains under-evaluated, largely because existing benchmarks do not provide a systematic way to assess memory mechanisms. In this paper, we study agent memory from a self-evolving perspective and introduce EvoMemBench, a unified benchmark organized along two axes: memory scope (in-episode vs. cross-episode) and memory content (knowledge-oriented vs. execution-oriented). We compare 15 representative memory methods with strong long-context baselines under a standardized protocol. Results show that current memory systems are still far from a general solution: long-context baselines remain highly competitive, memory helps most when the current context is insufficient or tasks are difficult, and no single memory form works consistently across all settings. Retrieval-based methods remain strong for knowledge-intensive settings, whereas procedural and long-term memory methods are more effective for execution-oriented tasks when their stored experience matches the task structure. We hope EvoMemBench facilitates future research on more effective memory systems for LLM-based agents. Our dataset and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VerifyMAS: Hypothesis Verification for Failure Attribution in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17467)**  `arXiv:2605.17467`  
  _Hezhe Qiao, Hanghang Tong, Ee-Peng Lim, Bing Liu, Guansong Pang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model-driven multi-agent systems (LLM-MAS) excel at complex tasks, yet unreliable agents remain a key bottleneck to system-level reliability. Automatic failure attribution is therefore critical, but existing approaches, such as direct prediction of agent-error pairs and agent-first failure attribution, rely on local logs of agents and miss global failures that only manifest over full interaction trajectories, such as cross-step inconsistencies and inter-agent coordination errors. Moreover, directly predicting failures induces a large combinatorial search space, hindering fine-grained attribution. To address these challenges, we propose VerifyMAS, a hypothesis verification framework for agent failure attribution. Instead of directly predicting faulty agents and error types, VerifyMAS formulates and verifies failure hypotheses against full trajectories. This verification-based approach decomposes attribution into trajectory-level error validation and fine-grained agent localization, providing an error-first attribution approach that captures global failure patterns while substantially reducing the search space. We further introduce a hypothesis-based data construction strategy grounded in a structured error taxonomy and fine-tune a specialized LLM verifier model for trajectory-level failure verification and agent attribution. Experiments on Aegis-Bench and Who&When show that VerifyMAS consistently improves diverse backbone models, including open-source Qwen and API-based GPT models, outperforming prior methods without sacrificing inference efficiency for long multi-agent trajectories. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AgentCollab: A Self-Evaluation-Driven Collaboration Paradigm for Efficient LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26034)**  `arXiv:2603.26034`  
  _Wenbo Gao, Renxi Liu, Xian Wang, Fang Guo, Shuai Yang, Xi Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous agents powered by large language models (LLMs) perform complex tasks through long-horizon reasoning and tool interaction, where a fundamental trade-off arises between execution efficiency and reasoning robustness. Models at different capability-cost levels offer complementary advantages: lower-cost models enable fast execution but may struggle on difficult reasoning segments, while stronger models provide more robust reasoning at higher computational cost. We present AgentCollab, a self-driven collaborative inference framework that dynamically coordinates models with different reasoning capacities during agent execution. Instead of relying on external routing modules, the framework uses the agent's own self-reflection signal to determine whether the current reasoning trajectory is making meaningful progress, and escalates control to a stronger reasoning tier only when necessary. To further stabilize long-horizon execution, we introduce a difficulty-aware cumulative escalation strategy that allocates additional reasoning budget based on recent failure signals. In our experiments, we instantiate this framework using a two-level small-large model setting. Experiments on diverse multi-step agent benchmarks show that AgentCollab consistently improves the accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontier of LLM agents. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Safety of Large Language Models via Embedding Space Separation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.20206)**  `arXiv:2603.20206`  
  _Xu Zhao, Xiting Wang, Weiran Shen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety alignment does not fully prevent large language models (LLMs) from producing harmful content under adversarial attacks. We observe that existing attack methods typically move harmful embeddings toward regions of harmless embeddings. This motivates us to explore whether increasing the distance between harmful and harmless embeddings can improve the safety of LLMs, since greater separation requires attacks to make larger embedding shifts, which can destroy semantics. We propose Embedding Space Separation (ES2), a fine-tuning method that increases the distance between harmful and harmless embeddings. However, maximizing this distance alone can damage LLMs' capabilities. Thus, we use two regularization losses: an embedding anchor loss keeps harmless embeddings close in direction to those of a frozen base model and a KL-divergence loss keeps output distributions on harmless prompts close to those of the base model. Our experimental results demonstrate that ES2 has excellent defensive performance against various attack methods. LLMs fine-tuned with ES2 retain general capabilities comparable to those of the baseline models, though some performance degradation is observed on certain tasks. Our results indicate that ES2 is an effective way to enhance the safety of large language models, while maintaining comparable performance on general capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Ctrl-G: Controlling Structural and Interaction Exposure in Knowledge Graph Augmented LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.08739)**  `arXiv:2601.08739`  
  _Xingyu Tan, Xiaoyang Wang, Qing Liu, Xiwei Xu, Xin Yuan, Liming Zhu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graphs (KGs) provide structured evidence for Web question answering with large language models (LLMs), but sending retrieved evidence to a remote LLM can expose sensitive graph information. Existing privacy treatments use entity anonymization and relation-centric abstraction to preserve reasoning cues, but transmitted topology remains visible and disclosure accumulates across repeated calls. Distinctive topology can support re-identification of masked entities (structural exposure), while successive calls accumulate additional paths and exploration traces into a broader view of the KG (interaction exposure). Simply sending less evidence is insufficient because restricted, stepwise views may omit the bridge paths required for multi-hop reasoning. To address this, we propose Ctrl-G, to the best of our knowledge the first KG-RAG framework to jointly control structural and interaction exposure to a remote LLM while supporting accurate multi-hop reasoning. To control structural exposure, Ctrl-G locally constructs query-conditioned sanitized views through structural aggregation and pruning, limiting the graph evidence transmitted to a remote LLM. A hierarchical controller couples remote planning with local connected-path exploration across topic entities, using question indicators and retrieved experience to prune candidates while grounding and verifying retained evidence against the original KG. To control interaction exposure, local experience memory reuses abstract, verified reasoning traces to guide exploration modes and depths and gate remote LLM calls, reducing repeated disclosure across questions. Ctrl-G achieves the highest accuracy among the compared methods on five of six benchmarks, exceeding the strongest baseline by up to 13.8 points across model configurations.
  </details>

- **[Service, Solidarity, and Self-Help: A Comparative Topic Modeling Analysis of Community Unionism in the Boot and Shoe Union and Unite Community](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.01529)**  `arXiv:2509.01529`  
  _Thomas Compton_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents a comparative analysis of community unionism (CU) in two distinct historical and organizational contexts: the National Boot and Shoe Union (B\&S) in the 1920s and Unite Community in the 2010s--2020s. Using BERTopic for thematic modeling and cTF-IDF weighting, alongside word frequency analysis, the study examines the extent to which each union's discourse aligns with key features of CU -- such as coalition-building, grassroots engagement, and action beyond the workplace. The results reveal significant differences in thematic focus and discursive coherence. While Unite Community demonstrates stronger alignment with outward-facing, social justice-oriented themes, the B\&S corpus emphasizes internal administration, industrial relations, and member services -- reflecting a more traditional, servicing-oriented union model. The analysis also highlights methodological insights, demonstrating how modern NLP techniques can enhance the study of historical labor archives. Ultimately, the findings suggest that while both unions engage with community-related themes, their underlying models of engagement diverge significantly, challenging assumptions about the continuity and universality of community unionism across time and sector.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Black Box: Integrating Lexical and Semantic Methods in Quantitative Discourse Analysis with BERTopic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.19099)**  `arXiv:2508.19099`  
  _Thomas Compton_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantitative Discourse Analysis has seen growing adoption with the rise of Large Language Models and computational tools. However, reliance on black box software such as MAXQDA and NVivo risks undermining methodological transparency and alignment with research goals. This paper presents a hybrid, transparent framework for QDA that combines lexical and semantic methods to enable triangulation, reproducibility, and interpretability. Drawing from a case study in historical political discourse, we demonstrate how custom Python pipelines using NLTK, spaCy, and Sentence Transformers allow fine-grained control over preprocessing, lemmatisation, and embedding generation. We further detail our iterative BERTopic modelling process, incorporating UMAP dimensionality reduction, HDBSCAN clustering, and c-TF-IDF keyword extraction, optimised through parameter tuning and multiple runs to enhance topic coherence and coverage. By juxtaposing precise lexical searches with context-aware semantic clustering, we argue for a multi-layered approach that mitigates the limitations of either method in isolation. Our workflow underscores the importance of code-level transparency, researcher agency, and methodological triangulation in computational discourse studies. Code and supplementary materials are available via GitHub.
  </details>

- **[MedResearcher-R1: Expert-Level Medical Deep Researcher via A Knowledge-Informed Trajectory Synthesis Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14880)**  `arXiv:2508.14880`  
  _Ailing Yu, HaoYang Hong, Lan Yao, Jingnan Liu, Zhe Chen, Jiajun Yin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents have recently demonstrated remarkable capabilities in deep research tasks, yet their application to the medical domain remains challenging. The leading proprietary systems achieve only modest results on complex medical benchmarks. This exposes two key bottlenecks: (i) limited clinical-reasoning capability in the underlying model, and (ii) unreliable access to authoritative medical evidence when constrained to open-web retrieval. To address these limitations and equip agents with robust clinical reasoning capabilities, we present MedResearcher-R1, a medical deep research agent featuring two core innovations. First, we propose Knowledge-Informed Trajectory Synthesis (KISA), a novel approach that builds medical knowledge graphs to construct complex multi-hop question-answer pairs centered on rare medical entities, overcoming the scarcity of high-quality domain-specific training data. Second, we integrate a medical retrieval engine alongside general-purpose tools, enabling precise and reliable synthesis of medical evidence. Through this methodology, we yield over 2,100 diverse trajectories spanning 12 medical specialties. Trained with supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, our MedResearcher-R1-32B achieves state-of-the-art performance on MedBrowseComp (27.5/50 vs. 25.5/50 for o3-deepresearch) while demonstrating strong general performance on GAIA and xBench benchmarks. To the best of our knowledge, we present the first high-quality, tool-augmented medical dataset paired with a domain-specialized deep-research agent, demonstrating that smaller open-source models can surpass substantially larger proprietary systems on specialized medical tasks.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Public Access in LLM Pre-Training Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00020)**  `arXiv:2505.00020`  
  _Sruly Rosenblat, Tim O'Reilly, Ilan Strauss_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Using a legally obtained dataset of 34 copyrighted O'Reilly Media books (13,962 paragraphs), we apply the DE-COP membership inference attack to test whether OpenAI models show recognition of non-public (pay-walled) content they were not licensed to train on. Our main contribution is examining non-public books, which allows us to consider potential access violations and not just training inclusion. Our results suggest that GPT-4o, OpenAI's more recent and capable model in our sample, exhibits patterns consistent with recognition of non-public books, with an AUROC score of 0.82 (95% bootstrapped CI: 0.60-0.96) -- where above 50% is better than random chance. GPT-3.5 Turbo (trained two years prior) and the smaller GPT-4o Mini both score near chance recognition on non-public data (0.54 and 0.56, respectively). Results are robust to independent paraphrasers (Gemma and Qwen) and leave-one-book-out sensitivity analyses. We also test both GPT-4o and GPT-4o mini, with the same October 2023 training cutoff date, to ensure the model is not simply identifying language shifts over time. These findings support disclosure requirements for pre-training data provenance and formal licensing markets for training content. Results remain preliminary given the small book sample, possible contamination of post-cutoff controls, and differences across models in size, architecture, and training composition.
  </details>

### Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition 📸

<details open><summary>Click to Collapse</summary>

- **[Latent Reasoning in Continuous Space for Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42149)**  `arXiv:2610.42149`  
  _Byungwoo Jeon, Yoonwoo Jeong, Hyunseok Lee, Minsu Cho, Jinwoo Shin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) achieve remarkable success on diverse multimodal understanding and generation tasks, yet they still struggle to effectively leverage language-based chain-of-thought reasoning for image generation. As a result, existing reasoning-augmented UMMs often fail to inherit the test-time scaling and fine-grained controllability observed in large language models. We attribute this failure to a fundamental modality mismatch: reasoning is performed in a discrete token space, whereas image generation operates in a continuous space. To bridge this gap, we introduce a Latent Reasoning framework in Continuous space (LARC), which alleviates the token-level bottleneck by reasoning over continuous hidden states. Specifically, the training framework consists of two stages: (i) curriculum supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and (ii) information-gain reinforcement learning (RL). First, the curriculum SFT stage gradually converts token-level reasoning into latent reasoning. Subsequently, the information-gain RL stage self-evolves latent reasoning without ground truth supervision. Experiments across diverse text-to-image generation benchmarks show that LARC consistently improves generation quality, prompt following, and compositional fidelity. Notably, LARC achieves state-of-the-art performance on GenEval with a 7%p gain over BAGEL and demonstrates clear test-time scaling behavior, outperforming token-based generation under both parallel and sequential test-time scaling strategies.
  </details>

- **[ReLift: Dense 3D Tracking as Lifted Trajectory Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42136)**  `arXiv:2610.42136`  
  _Assaf Singer, Maroun Khriesh, Noam Rotstein, Yoni Kasten, Or Litany_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense 3D tracking from monocular video requires establishing point correspondences over time, estimating their depth, and inferring their trajectories during occlusion. Existing methods typically learn these tasks jointly, a demanding objective given the scarcity of 4D supervision. We show that strong pretrained 2D trackers and 3D geometry models provide an effective foundation: lifted trajectories, with occluded segments simply interpolated, already rival dedicated 3D trackers using the same geometry. However, lifted trajectories inherit correspondence and depth errors, and under occlusion, their depth comes from the occluder rather than the tracked point. We introduce ReLift, an efficient feed-forward framework that recasts dense 3D tracking as geometric trajectory repair and completion. At its core, a trajectory corrector alternates attention along and across trajectories to preserve reliable observations, repair corrupted positions, and complete missing motion. Using only geometric inputs allows us to train directly on 3D trajectories with simulated lifting errors, decoupling training from images and from any specific tracker or geometry model. Concentrating 4D supervision on trajectory refinement yields a reusable model that consistently improves lifted trajectories across trackers and geometry providers without retraining, so it benefits directly from future advances in either component. On WorldTrack, ReLift achieves state-of-the-art mean accuracy, improving on the strongest baselines by 2.1 APD on all points and 3.0 on dynamic points, with faster configurations remaining competitive with leading 3D trackers while running faster than 4RC. Visit our supplementry page for more examples and code.
  </details>

- **[A Better Critic Matters for Distribution Matching Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42135)**  `arXiv:2610.42135`  
  _Zongze Wu, Xianbang Wang, Zhengyang Geng, Mingdeng Cao, Zhoutong Zhang, Richard Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) trains a few-step student using the difference between a teacher's score and an online critic's estimate of the student distribution's score. We investigate what the critic needs, in capacity and in information, to provide a reliable training signal. On the capacity side, controlled experiments across seven SiT model sizes on ImageNet show that increasing teacher size while keeping a small critic fixed can degrade student quality and cause training collapse. Scaling the critic alongside the teacher, a strategy we call joint scaling, prevents collapse in our experiments and allows the student to benefit from larger teachers. On the information side, rather than forcing the critic to model a step-agnostic mixture of student outputs, we introduce a step-aware critic (STAC) that conditions on the student's generation step to estimate step-specific scores. Together, joint scaling and STAC consistently improve few-step distillation across class-conditional ImageNet generation, text-to-image generation with SDXL and FLUX.2, and text-to-video generation with Wan2.1.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Asymmetry in Self-Supervised 3D Geometry Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42092)**  `arXiv:2610.42092`  
  _Gyeongjin Kang, Freeman Cheng, Botao Ye, Weijie Lyu, Ming-Hsuan Yang, Eunbyung Park_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised learning relies on asymmetry between what a model observes and what it must explain. Recent methods learn 3D geometry from unposed images by predicting cameras and scene structure, then rendering held-out views. To avoid trivial solutions, they impose this asymmetry in the input space, restricting how views interact through separate branches or masked attention. We revisit this design with geometric accuracy, rather than rendering quality, as the main objective. When a held-out view lies between context views, the context already constrains the geometry it observes. We therefore impose the asymmetry in the 3D representation space, excluding held-out views only from the reconstructed scene while a shared backbone uses all views for correspondence. This improves depth and pose estimation over input-space designs. We further exploit view density: sparse and dense observations depict the same scene but provide different amounts of correspondence. Aligning sparse-context features with dense-context features supplies a geometric training signal alongside rendering, without a pretrained geometric teacher. We additionally align features with a frozen vision foundation model, which brings further gains. None of these signals requires geometric annotation, and the resulting model improves geometry estimation over prior self-supervised methods.
