  Reconstructing 3D scenes from a single image is a fundamental challenge in computer vision, with broad applications in virtual reality, robotics, and content creation. Recent methods achieve outstanding performance by leveraging camera-controlled video diffusion models, but rely on iterative diffusion sampling, which greatly limits their practical deployment. We observe that geometric forward warping alone can cover the majority of a target view directly from the input image, with only a compact residual left for the encoder to correct. Motivated by this observation, we propose PRISM, a feed-forward framework that decomposes multi-view latent prediction into a parameter-free geometric prior and a learned residual correction, with no diffusion sampling required at inference. To enable generalization from purely synthetic training data, we devise a two-stage training strategy combining latents supervised distillation for geometric generalization and perceptual fine-tuning for appearance quality optimization. Extensive experiments on three benchmarks demonstrate that PRISM achieves competitive reconstruction quality compared with diffusion-based methods, while reducing inference time dramatically to only 36 seconds per scene. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Lightweight Fiducial-Based Pipeline for 3D Hyperspectral Mapping of ex-vivo Lumpectomy Specimens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14534)**  `arXiv:2606.14534`  
  _Anna Bicchi, Alberto Rota, Leonardo Passoni, Nicola Ancellotti, Andrea Peroni, Lorenzo Vinco, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperspectral Imaging (HSI) is a promising modality for intraoperative assessment of resection margins in Breast-Conserving Surgery (BCS), but its clinical translation requires aligning the inherently 2D spectral information onto the 3D shape of the excised tissue so that suspicious regions can be precisely localized for targeted follow-up. We present a fully automated, calibration-free pipeline that produces a 3D hyperspectral point cloud of an ex-vivo lumpectomy specimen from a set of consumer-camera RGB images and a single top-down HSI acquisition. The 3D geometry is reconstructed with a deep-learning Structure-from-Motion backbone, stabilized in a metric reference frame by a custom bundle adjustment that enforces consistency on the corners of four ArUco markers placed around the specimen. The HSI cube is then registered to the reconstruction without recovering the HSI camera pose: the markers, visible in both modalities, define 16 corner correspondences that drive a planar homography, and 3D coordinates are recovered by lookup on an orthographically rendered depth map. Evaluated on two ex-vivo lumpectomy specimens, the pipeline achieves a median 3D registration error below 1 mm and a 2D reprojection error below 0.02 mm, with a total per-specimen processing time under 4 minutes on accelerated hardware. These results support the feasibility of integrating HSI-guided spatial localization into intraoperative margin assessment workflows for breast-conserving surgery.
  </details>

- **[How Do Video Foundation Models Encode Intuitive Physics? Probing Across Pretraining Paradigms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09646)**  `arXiv:2606.09646`  
  _Samuele Punzo, Niccolò Caselli, Ippokratis Pantelidis, Francesco Massafra, Salvatore Lo Sardo, Mohammadreza Salehi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Do video foundation models encode a usable sense of how the physical world works, and does intuitive physics emerge naturally from large-scale video pretraining? In this work, we study whether pretrained video foundation models encode intuitive-physics information in their frozen representations, and how its accessibility varies across pretraining paradigms, layers, backbone variants, and probe types. Using frozen-feature probing on IntPhys2 and Minimal Video Pairs (MVP), we compare predictive joint-embedding models (V-JEPA), masked reconstruction models (VideoMAE), and diffusion-based video generators (LTX-Video). We find that across linear, MLP, and temporal attentive probes, physics-relevant information is generally weakest in early layers and most accessible at intermediate to late depth. On MVP, temporal attentive probes substantially outperform linear readouts, with V-JEPA achieving 95.0% pair consistency. On IntPhys2, linear probes recover more signal, and VideoMAE attains the strongest temporal attentive result at 73.9% violation-of-expectation accuracy. On both benchmarks, attentive probe performance on LTX consistently underperforms compared to the other two pretraining paradigms. Together, these results suggest that intuitive-physics is broadly decodable from video foundation models, but it is not represented as a single, uniform capability. Its accessibility, and especially its temporal grounding, depends much more on the benchmark and readout than on a simple hierarchy of pretraining objectives or model scale.
  </details>

- **[Analyzing the Optimization Conflict Between Image- and Text-Based Person Re-Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02242)**  `arXiv:2606.02242`  
  _Karina Kvanchiani, Timur Mamedov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unifying image-based (I2I) and text-based (T2I) person re-identification (ReID) within a single model is complicated by a fundamental conflict between their training objectives. While I2I ReID focuses on identity-level invariance across images of the same person captured by multiple cameras, T2I ReID is driven by instance-specific textual descriptions tied to unique visual traits. When both objectives are optimized on a shared vision encoder, they impose opposing requirements on the same embeddings - I2I contracts intra-identity distances while T2I requires them to persist - so improving one task's representation actively degrades the other's. This paper analyzes this optimization conflict and shows empirically that the two objectives are not jointly satisfiable by a single unconstrained encoder. Motivated by this finding, we investigate the conflict through a controlled two-stage pipeline: a shared vision encoder is first trained for I2I invariance, then frozen while only the text encoder is aligned to the fixed visual space, preventing the T2I objective from unwinding the identity-level structure already learned. We conduct extensive experiments across multiple configurations, varying domain-mixing procedures, learning strategies, and task objectives. We observe that I2I pre-training improves subsequent T2I performance, while textual supervision during this pre-training further improves T2I performance, although the vision encoder is frozen during subsequent cross-modal training. We believe these insights provide a meaningful step toward unified ReID systems that reconcile identity-level and instance-level supervision within a single representation.
  </details>

- **[DRScaffold: Boosting Dense-Scene Reasoning in Lightweight Vision Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26038)**  `arXiv:2605.26038`  
  _Xinrui Shi, Kai Liu, Ziqing Zhang, Jianze Li, Anqi Li, Yulun Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lightweight vision-language models perform competitively on standard benchmarks yet fail systematically in dense-scene reasoning, where multiple objects, attributes, and relations must be jointly grounded and resolved through multi-step inference. Such capability is critical for real-world applications where models must reliably interpret cluttered environments. Yet existing training signals provide no explicit grounding between reasoning steps and the underlying visual entities and relations, leaving lightweight models free to generate fluent but visually unanchored reasoning chains. To address this gap, we first introduce DRBench, a benchmark of 14,573 questions across 2,943 images, organized into five task categories spanning three progressive reasoning layers. Building on DRBench, we propose DRScaffold, a supervised fine-tuning framework that decomposes the supervision target into four causally ordered stages, promoting grounded reasoning without architectural modification. Experiments on three lightweight VLMs demonstrate substantial gains on DRBench while preserving or improving performance on general-purpose benchmarks. Notably, Qwen2.5-VL-3B trained with DRScaffold surpasses the frozen Qwen2.5-VL-32B under zero-shot direct-answer prompting on DRBench, demonstrating that structured supervision can substitute for a significant portion of model scale in dense-scene reasoning. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Sparkle: Realizing Lively Instruction-Guided Video Background Replacement via Decoupled Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06535)**  `arXiv:2605.06535`  
  _Ziyun Zeng, Yiqi Lin, Guoqiang Liang, Mike Zheng Shou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, open-source efforts like Senorita-2M have propelled video editing toward natural language instruction. However, current publicly available datasets predominantly focus on local editing or style transfer, which largely preserve the original scene structure and are easier to scale. In contrast, Background Replacement, a task central to creative applications such as film production and advertising, requires synthesizing entirely new, temporally consistent scenes while maintaining accurate foreground-background interactions, making large-scale data generation significantly more challenging. Consequently, this complex task remains largely underexplored due to a scarcity of high-quality training data. This gap is evident in poorly performing state-of-the-art models, e.g., Kiwi-Edit, because the primary open-source dataset that contains this task, i.e., OpenVE-3M, frequently produces static, unnatural backgrounds. In this paper, we trace this quality degradation to a lack of precise background guidance during data synthesis. Accordingly, we design a scalable pipeline that generates foreground and background guidance in a decoupled manner with strict quality filtering. Building on this pipeline, we introduce Sparkle, a dataset of ~140K video pairs spanning five common background-change themes, alongside Sparkle-Bench, the largest evaluation benchmark tailored for background replacement to date. Experiments demonstrate that our dataset and the model trained on it achieve substantially better performance than all existing baselines on both OpenVE-Bench and Sparkle-Bench. Our proposed dataset, benchmark, model, as well as the full data generation pipeline, are fully open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MuCALD-SplitFed: Causal-Latent Diffusion for Privacy-Preserving Multi-Task Split-Federated Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04108)**  `arXiv:2605.04108`  
  _Chamani Shiranthika, Hadi Hadizadeh, Parvaneh Saeedi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated Learning enables decentralized training by aggregating model updates across clients without sharing raw data, while Split Federated Learning further partitions the model between clients and a server to reduce computation and communication at the client side. However, decentralized medical institutions rarely operate on a single shared task, making standard Federated and SplitFed collaborations poorly aligned with real clinical workflows. Multi-task FL extends these frameworks by allowing clients to handle different tasks, but often introduces instability and privacy vulnerabilities. This study proposes \textbf{MuCALD-SplitFed}, a multi-task SplitFed framework that integrates causal representation learning and latent diffusion. Experiments show MuCALD-SplitFed consistently improves segmentation, while baseline SplitFed fails to converge. The proposed approach further reduces information leakage at split points, mitigating reconstruction-based and membership inference attacks. Additionally, MuCALD SplitFed outperforms state-of-the-art personalized FL and multi-task FL approaches. The code repository is:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Autoregressive Diffusion for Compositional Text-to-Scene Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16552)**  `arXiv:2604.16552`  
  _Zhenggang Tang, Yuehao Wang, Yuchen Fan, Jun-Kun Chen, Yu-Ying Yeh, Kihyuk Sohn, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent text-to-scene generation approaches largely reduced the manual efforts required to create 3D scenes. However, their focus is either to generate a scene layout or to generate objects, and few generate both. The generated scene layout is often simple even with LLM's help. Moreover, the generated scene is often inconsistent with the text input that contains non-trivial descriptions of the shape, appearance, and spatial arrangement of the objects. We present a new paradigm of sequential text-to-scene generation and propose a novel generative model for interactive scene creation. At the core is a 3D Autoregressive Diffusion model 3D-ARD+, which unifies the autoregressive generation over a multimodal token sequence and diffusion generation of next-object 3D latents. To generate the next object, the model uses one autoregressive step to generate the coarse-grained 3D latents in the scene space, conditioned on both the current seen text instructions and already synthesized 3D scene. It then uses a second step to generate the 3D latents in the smaller object space, which can be decoded into fine-grained object geometry and appearance. We curate a large dataset of 230K indoor scenes with paired text instructions for training. We evaluate 7B 3D-ARD+, on challenging scenes, and showcase the model can generate and place objects following non-trivial spatial layout and semantics prescribed by the text instructions.
  </details>

- **[SUREON: A Benchmark and Vision-Language Model for Surgical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.06570)**  `arXiv:2603.06570`  
  _Alejandra Perez, Anita Rau, Busisiwe Mlambo, Lee White, Aina Brunet, Juan Verde, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A capable surgical AI model should reason from visual evidence; yet, many existing surgical benchmarks can be partially solved without visual input, as models may exploit procedural priors or common anatomy-instrument interactions to infer answers from language alone. To address this, we introduce SUREON, a video question-answering (QA) dataset and benchmark designed to evaluate visually grounded surgical reasoning. Our pipeline generates QA pairs from narrated surgical academic videos, refines incorrect options using multiple models to reduce linguistic biases, and uses trained adversarial guessers to filter out questions answerable without visual evidence. The resulting dataset contains 76K training pairs covering 168 procedures and 10 specialties, alongside a benchmark of 340 questions across 10 categories verified by expert surgeons. Benchmarking leading commercial and open-source models shows that surgical visual reasoning remains largely unsolved--no model exceeds 53% accuracy and even expert surgeons reach only 64.9%. We also introduce SUREON-VLM, a baseline trained on our curated data that avoids language shortcuts, outperforms existing open-source models on SUREON, and matches larger specialist models on multiple standard surgical benchmarks. Our training ablations show that distractor refinement and blind curation strengthen visual grounding, increasing the multimodal gain from 8.7 to 29.1 percentage points while reducing the training set from 176.6K to 76K filtered samples. SUREON thus offers both a verified testbed for surgical visual reasoning and training data that strengthens visual grounding.
  </details>

- **[Place-it-R1: Unlocking Environment-aware Reasoning Potential of MLLM for Video Object Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.06140)**  `arXiv:2603.06140`  
  _Bohai Gu, Taiyi Wu, Dazhao Du, Jian Liu, Shuai Yang, Xiaotong Zhao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video object insertion is fundamental to video editing, yet existing diffusion methods often produce visually plausible but physically inconsistent results. We present Place-it-R1, an end-to-end framework for physically plausible video object insertion driven by environment-aware MLLM reasoning. Rather than treating reasoning as a generic text prompt, Place-it-R1 uses the MLLM to analyze the target environment, infer object-scene interactions, and determine where an insertion is physically valid. The resulting reasoning is translated into two complementary forms of guidance for video diffusion: semantic guidance that describes the intended physical interaction and spatial guidance that provides a valid insertion region in each frame. To further align generation with local physical realism, we introduce Spatial Direct Preference Optimization, which leverages an MLLM to rank generated candidates, and introduces a region-aware preference objective that explicitly localizes physical-violation penalties to the inserted-object region. Place-it-R1 further offers flexible and standard modes to trade off environment adaptation and scene preservation. Extensive experiments show that Place-it-R1 produces more physically coherent and visually natural insertions than state-of-the-art methods and achieves competitive results against commercial systems.
  </details>

- **[Generalizable Multiscale Segmentation of Heterogeneous Map Collections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05037)**  `arXiv:2603.05037`  
  _Remi Petitpierre_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Historical map collections are highly diverse in style, scale, and geographic focus, often consisting of many single-sheet documents. Yet most work in map recognition focuses on specialist models tailored to homogeneous map series. In contrast, this article aims to develop generalizable semantic segmentation models and a shared class ontology. We introduce Semap, a new open benchmark dataset comprising 1,439 manually annotated patches designed to reflect the variety of historical map documents. We also evaluate a segmentation workflow that combines procedural data synthesis with multiscale integration. Under the harmonized benchmarks considered here, this workflow attains the strongest performance among the evaluated approaches and viably learns from heterogeneous map data. The results indicate that segmentation performance remains largely stable across map collections, scales, geographic regions, and publication contexts. By providing benchmark data, a shared ontology, and an evaluation of generic segmentation across heterogeneous maps, this work contributes toward integrating the long tail of cartographic archives into historical geographic studies.
  </details>

- **[AdaGS: Confidence-Gated Diffusion Adaptation for Unposed Sparse-View 3D Gaussian Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21535)**  `arXiv:2602.21535`  
  _Beizhen Zhao, Sicheng Yu, Guanzhi Ding, Yu Hu, Hao Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse-view outdoor 3D reconstruction remains challenging for Gaussian splatting. Although diffusion restorers can provide additional pseudo-view supervision, we identify a failure pattern in diffusion-guided reconstruction: an unguided diffusion model may misinterpret floating artifacts and complete them into plausible but non-existent scene structures. To mitigate this challenge, we propose a confidence-gated diffusion adaptation framework for unposed sparse-view reconstruction. We introduce an artifact-aware adapter that is trained in two stages on our constructed Gaussian artifact dataset. The adapter suppresses floater-induced visual cues before diffusion restoration, while bidirectional confidence fusion accepts generated regions only when they are supported by cross-view geometric evidence. Furthermore, we introduce a Gaussian stabilization strategy to attenuate Gaussians that exhibit weak multi-view inconsistency, preventing them from repeatedly triggering hallucinations in later optimization. Extensive experiments on three outdoor datasets demonstrate that our method improves rendering fidelity and pose accuracy over existing approaches.
  </details>

- **[Parabolic Positional Encoding: A Vision-Centric, Principled, Extrapolatable, and General Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01418)**  `arXiv:2602.01418`  
  _Christoffer Koo Øhrstrøm, Rafael I. Cabral Muchacho, Yifei Dong, Filippos Moumtzidellis, Ronja Güldenring, Florian T. Pokorny, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose Parabolic Positional Encoding (PaPE), a parabola-based positional encoding for vision modalities in attention-based architectures. Given a set of vision tokens-such as from videos, event camera streams, images, or point clouds-our objective is to encode their positions while accounting for the characteristics of vision modalities. Prior works have largely extended positional encodings from 1D-sequences in language to nD-structures in vision, but only with partial account of vision characteristics. We address this gap by designing PaPE from principles distilled from prior work: translation invariance, rotation invariance (PaPE-RI), distance decay, directionality, and context awareness. Extrapolation experiments on ImageNet-1K show how PaPE extrapolates remarkably well, improving in absolute terms by up to 10.5 over the next-best encoding. Generality experiments on 8 datasets across 4 modalities show that PaPE is a general vision positional encoding, as PaPE matches the best baseline on 5 datasets and exceeds all on 2 datasets. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Differential Vector Erasure: Unified Training-Free Concept Erasure for Flow Matching Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01089)**  `arXiv:2602.01089`  
  _Zhiqi Zhang, Xinhao Zhong, Yi Sun, Shuoyang Sun, Jiahui Wang, Bin Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image diffusion models have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in generating high-quality images, yet their tendency to reproduce undesirable concepts, such as NSFW content, copyrighted styles, or specific objects, poses growing concerns for safe and controllable deployment. While existing concept erasure approaches primarily focus on DDPM-based diffusion models and rely on costly fine-tuning, the recent emergence of flow matching models introduces a fundamentally different generative paradigm for which prior methods are not directly applicable. In this paper, we propose Differential Vector Erasure (DVE), a training-free concept erasure method specifically designed for flow matching models. Our key insight is that semantic concepts are implicitly encoded in the directional structure of the velocity field governing the generative flow. Leveraging this observation, we construct a differential vector field that characterizes the directional discrepancy between a target concept and a carefully chosen anchor concept. During inference, DVE selectively removes concept-specific components by projecting the velocity field onto the differential direction, enabling precise concept suppression without affecting irrelevant semantics. Extensive experiments on FLUX demonstrate that DVE consistently outperforms existing baselines on a wide range of concept erasure tasks, including NSFW suppression, artistic style removal, and object erasure, while preserving image quality and diversity. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DiTGaze: Geometry-Conditioned Diffusion Augmentation for Few-Shot Gaze Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.11231)**  `arXiv:2511.11231`  
  _Abiram Panchalingam, Indu P.Bodala, Stuart E.Middleton_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate person-specific gaze estimation benefits from labeled user data, but collecting images that densely cover gaze directions is burdensome. We present DiTGaze, a calibration-efficient augmentation system that follows a two-stream 3D Gaussian Splatting backbone and reformulates its convolutional rendering stage as a geometry-conditioned latent Diffusion Transformer (DiT). A learnable projection and frozen VAE bridge rasterized Gaussian features to latent tokens, while timestep and identity/expression conditioning are integrated through Adaptive Layer Normalization. Intermediate yaw-pitch sampling densifies gaze supervision, and an orthogonality constraint reduces interference between gaze, pose, and expression. On ETH-XGaze, DiTGaze reduces generator-level gaze error by 4.1 percent relative to GazeGaussian. In our runs of the expanded downstream protocol, it ranks first at every calibration budget from one to nine real images; the repeated primary comparison at N = 9 reduces subject-level estimator error by 0.73 degrees and improves 14 of 15 identities (p < 0.001). These results connect the proposed generator changes to statistically supported few-shot personalization gains.
  </details>

- **[NP-LoRA: Null Space Projection for Subject-Style LoRA Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.11051)**  `arXiv:2511.11051`  
  _Chuheng Chen, Xiaofei Zhou, Geyuan Zhang, Yong Huang, Gang Xiong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fusion enables the composition of subject and style representations for controllable generation without retraining. However, existing approaches primarily operate through weight-level merging, without explicitly modeling how independently trained LoRAs interact in the shared parameter space. We adopt a geometric perspective on LoRA fusion, interpreting content and style LoRAs as occupying overlapping, non-orthogonal low-rank subspaces, where such overlap can lead to conflicting parameter updates that affect generation quality. This observation motivates us to reformulate LoRA fusion not merely as parameter combination, but as a problem of controlling how updates from overlapping subspaces are combined. Based on this insight, we propose Null Space Projection LoRA (NP-LoRA), a training-free framework that employs projection as a fusion operator to explicitly modulate cross-LoRA interactions. Specifically, NP-LoRA uses principal directions of the style LoRA to define a projection subspace and projects the content LoRA onto the complementary subspace (i.e., the null space of the style LoRA), suppressing interference along dominant style directions while preserving complementary information. To avoid the overly aggressive suppression of hard projection, we further formulate soft projection as a regularized optimization problem that balances content preservation against style-subspace suppression. This objective admits a closed-form solution, yielding a projection operator controlled by a single parameter that continuously interpolates between linear merging and hard projection. Extensive experiments across multiple pretrained LoRA pairs show that NP-LoRA achieves more balanced content-style composition compared to strong baselines, without requiring retraining. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MIC-BEV: Multi-Infrastructure Camera Bird's-Eye-View Transformer with Relation-Aware Fusion for 3D Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.24688)**  `arXiv:2510.24688`  
  _Yun Zhang, Zhaoliang Zheng, Johnson Liu, Zhiyu Huang, Zewei Zhou, Zonglin Meng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Infrastructure-based perception plays a crucial role in intelligent transportation systems by providing global situational awareness and enabling cooperative autonomy. However, camera-based 3D object detection remains challenging under multi-view infrastructure setups, heterogeneous camera configurations, degraded sensing conditions, diverse road layouts, and adverse environmental conditions. We introduce MIC-BEV, a Transformer-based bird's-eye-view (BEV) perception framework for infrastructure-based multi-camera 3D object detection. MIC-BEV supports a variable number of cameras with heterogeneous intrinsic and extrinsic parameters and uses a graph-enhanced fusion module to adaptively integrate multi-view image features by modeling geometric relationships between cameras and the BEV grid. To support training and evaluation, we introduce M2I, a synthetic dataset featuring diverse camera layouts, road geometries, environmental conditions, and sensor-degradation cases. Experiments on the simulated M2I dataset and the real-world RoScenes dataset show that MIC-BEV achieves strong 3D detection performance and improved robustness under challenging conditions. Ablation studies validate the effectiveness of its key design choices, while weight analyses show that the learned fusion weights are associated with view-reliability factors and adapt to degraded camera inputs. We further report computational cost and deployment-related factors, providing additional evidence of practical feasibility and real-world deployment potential. These results indicate that MIC-BEV is a promising framework for infrastructure-based perception. The dataset and source code are available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[On the Faithfulness of Visual Thinking: Measurement and Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.23482)**  `arXiv:2510.23482`  
  _Zujing Liu, Junwen Pan, Qi She, Yuan Gao, Guisong Xia_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent large vision-language models (LVLMs) can generate vision-text multimodal chain-of-thought (MCoT) traces after reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT). However, we observe that the visual information in MCoT is often inaccurate even when answers are correct, and task accuracy remains nearly unchanged despite the visual information being corrupted. This indicates a lack of faithfulness in the vision part of MCoT reasoning. We attribute this to the RL reward design in RFT, which solely incentivizes the format of interleaved vision-text cues, encouraging the model to incorporate visual information into its text reasoning steps without considering its correctness. In this paper, we first probe MCoT faithfulness by measuring how much the prediction changes when its visual and textual thoughts are intervened. Surprisingly, the model's predictions remain nearly unchanged under visual intervention but change significantly under textual intervention, indicating that the visual evidence is largely ignored. To further analyze the visual information, we introduce an automated LVLM-based evaluation metric that quantifies the faithfulness of visual cues from two perspectives: relevance and sufficiency. Our evaluation reveals that the visual information in current MCoT traces is simultaneously irrelevant and insufficient. To address this issue, we propose Sufficient-Component Cause Model (SCCM) learning. This approach encourages the MCoT to generate sufficient yet minimal visual components that are independently capable of leading to the correct answer. The proposed SCCM is annotation-free and plug-and-play, compatible with various RFT for MCoT. Empirical results demonstrate that SCCM substantially improves the visual faithfulness across a suite of fine-grained perception and reasoning benchmarks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PPS: Plug-and-Play Saccadic Vision for Fine-Grained Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15688)**  `arXiv:2509.15688`  
  _Johann Schmidt, Sebastian Stober, Joachim Denzler, Paul Bodesheim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-grained visual classification benefits from high-resolution inputs to resolve subtle, localized cues, but at the cost of large compute and memory budgets. Inspired by human saccadic vision, we propose a lightweight extension that turns any pretrained backbone with spatial feature maps into a saccadic classifier through glimpse-induced inference. The only architectural additions are a small priority head and a single cross-attention fusion block. The same weight-tied encoder encodes a downsampled peripheral glance, exposes the spatial feature map from which the priority head predicts a glimpse-priority distribution and re-encodes high-resolution glimpse crops sampled from that distribution in parallel. A sequential sampler with non-maximum suppression draws a runtime-configurable number of glimpses. A residual multi-head cross-attention block then fuses the glance against the glimpses. To improve the quality of the priority maps, we train the priority head by a per-glimpse information-gain target from a lightweight per-image memory bank. This requires no reinforcement learning. Complexity is linear in the number of glimpses and peak memory is decoupled from input resolution. Across nine benchmarks our framework improves consistently over its backbones.
  </details>

- **[VisualSphinx: Large-Scale Synthetic Vision Logic Puzzles for RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23977)**  `arXiv:2505.23977`  
  _Yichen Feng, Zhangchen Xu, Fengqing Jiang, Yuetai Li, Bhaskar Ramasubramanian, Luyao Niu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision language models (VLMs) are expected to perform effective multimodal reasoning and make logically coherent decisions, which is critical to tasks such as diagram understanding and spatial problem solving. However, current VLM reasoning lacks large-scale and well-structured training datasets. To bridge this gap, we propose VisualSphinx, a first-of-its-kind large-scale synthetic visual logical reasoning training dataset. To tackle the challenge of image synthesis with grounded answers, we propose a rule-to-image synthesis pipeline that extracts and expands puzzle rules from seed questions and generates executable rendering programs for puzzle assembly. Using standard GRPO with binary correctness rewards as a validation recipe, experiments show that VLMs trained on VisualSphinx benefit from the logical coherence and readability of our dataset and exhibit improved performance on logical reasoning tasks. The enhanced reasoning capabilities developed from VisualSphinx also benefit other reasoning tasks such as algebraic reasoning, arithmetic reasoning and geometry reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Denoising Mutual Knowledge Distillation in Bi-Directional Multiple Instance Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12074)**  `arXiv:2505.12074`  
  _Chen Shu, Boyu Fu, Yiman Li, Ting Yin, Wenchuan Zhang, Jie Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multiple Instance Learning is the predominant method for Whole Slide Image classification in digital pathology, enabling the use of slide-level labels to supervise model training. Although MIL eliminates the tedious fine-grained annotation process for supervised learning, whether it can learn accurate bag- and instance-level classifiers remains a question. To address the issue, instance-level classifiers and instance masks were incorporated to ground the prediction on supporting patches. These methods, while practically improving the performance of MIL methods, may potentially introduce noisy labels. We propose to bridge the gap between commonly used MIL and fully supervised learning by augmenting both the bag- and instance-level learning processes with pseudo-label correction capabilities elicited from weak to strong generalization techniques. The proposed algorithm improves the performance of dual-level MIL algorithms on both bag- and instance-level predictions. Experiments on public pathology datasets showcase the advantage of the proposed methods.
  </details>

- **[Position: Next-Generation Game Engines Should Be Built on Interactive Generative Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.17359)**  `arXiv:2503.17359`  
  _Jiwen Yu, Yiran Qin, Haoxuan Che, Quande Liu, Xintao Wang, Pengfei Wan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern game development faces significant challenges in creativity and cost due to predetermined content in traditional game engines. Recent breakthroughs in video generation models, capable of synthesizing realistic and interactive virtual environments, present an opportunity to revolutionize game creation. This position paper argues that next-generation game engines should be built on Interactive Generative Video (IGV). We term such engines Generative Game Engines (GGE), with IGV as the perceptual and world-generation core under designer control. We argue why IGV is uniquely suited for this role, identify what is missing, and examine progress across six functional modules to show how each supports our position, culminating in a five-level maturity roadmap (L0-L4). Drawing on discussions with researchers, game developers, and community participants, we present nine alternative views and ethical considerations to stimulate debate.
  </details>

- **[point2vec for Self-Supervised Representation Learning on Point Clouds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.16570)**  `arXiv:2303.16570`  
  _Karim Knaebel, Jonas Schult, Alexander Hermans, Bastian Leibe_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present point2vec, a self-supervised method for learning point cloud representations through masked prediction of a teacher network's latent features. Building on data2vec, we investigate a limitation of applying this approach to point clouds: positional information associated with masked patches reveals the overall object shape to the student, weakening the learning task even under heavy masking. point2vec addresses this issue by passing only visible patches to the student encoder and introducing a shallow decoder to predict representations for masked patches. The teacher processes the complete point cloud to provide contextualized training targets. These targets capture global context through the teacher's self-attention, providing supervision beyond the local geometry of individual patches. This design conceals masked-patch positions from the student and reduces its computational cost during pre-training. Our ablations show that the decoder alone provides only part of the improvement; concealing positional information is key. After pre-training on ShapeNet, point2vec outperforms the evaluated self-supervised baselines on shape classification on ModelNet40 and ScanObjectNN and few-shot classification on ModelNet40, while achieving competitive part segmentation results on ShapeNetPart.
  </details>

- **[Fast Privacy Loss Accounting for Random Allocation via Multiscale FFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42117)**  `arXiv:2610.42117`  
  _Vitaly Feldman, Moshe Shenfeld_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In the random allocation sampling scheme each user's data is used in $k$ steps chosen randomly and uniformly from a sequence of $t$ steps. This scheme is known to improve on Poisson sampling in private optimization and in communication-efficient aggregation. The existing privacy loss distribution (PLD) accountant for this scheme runs in time quadratic in the inverse of the accuracy parameter $\alpha$ (Feldman and Shenfeld, 2026). We give an algorithm, \emph{multiscale FFT}, that represents likelihood ratios on a grid that is uniform within each dyadic interval and computes the exact rounding of every pairwise sum using FFTs within each interval. It keeps the guarantees of the accountant and improves the dependence of its running time on $\alpha$ from $O(\alpha^{-2})$ to $O(\alpha^{-1}\log(1/\alpha))$. In addition, we demonstrate that rounding can be performed via the mean-preserving connect-the-dots projection of (Doroshenko et al., 2026). This reduces the errors that rounding introduces in the mean of the privacy loss and in the $\chi^2$ divergence of the computed pair from the order of $\alpha$ to $O(\alpha^2/\log(t))$. In our experiments, our algorithm achieves speedups of several orders of magnitude at high accuracy and gives improved privacy bounds for DP-SGD with PREAMBLE.
  </details>

- **[Hyperbolic Inverse Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42104)**  `arXiv:2610.42104`  
  _Iulia Rusu, Anoop Praturu, Sergei Gepshtein, Tatyana Sharpee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) seeks to infer the reward function underlying observed behavior, but its predictive accuracy and interpretability depend on how that reward is computed from the underlying features. While prior IRL reward functions have been implemented through a variety of techniques including linear weights, neural networks, or other function approximators, none make assumptions about the geometry of the underlying feature space. Inspired by geometric organization observed in neural circuits, we introduce Hyperbolic inverse reinforcement learning (Hyperbolic IRL), which parameterizes reward as a function of Hyperbolic distance between the feature vector and a learned parameter vector, as well as its Euclidean distance variant. We tested our methods against a range of linear and nonlinear IRL baselines under both deterministic and stochastic policies. Our Hyperbolic approach achieved comparable performance to state-of-the-art models on established benchmarks and excelled in the low data limit and across dense environments. Applied to human navigation among multiple exhibits in a museum room, Hyperbolic IRL reveals reward structure that depends not only on individual exhibit-distance features, but also on non-separable conjunctions between them. Together, these results demonstrate that a Hyperbolic reward parameterization can improve both the predictive accuracy and interpretability of IRL, providing a framework for studying how non-separable, conjunctive structure can begin to explain behavior.
  </details>

- **[Towards Understanding the Impact of Plasticity Loss on Reinforcement Learning in Stochastic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42100)**  `arXiv:2610.42100`  
  _Philipp Bordne, André Biedenkapp_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep reinforcement learning (RL) is a powerful framework for sequential decision-making. Despite many high-profile successes in recent years, deep RL is known to be brittle. An emerging line of research attributes this brittleness to a loss of plasticity in neural networks. However, plasticity loss has so far been understudied in stochastic environments. We study this interplay systematically, training SAC agents on three DeepMind Control tasks augmented with controlled levels of observation and action noise. We find that plasticity-preserving methods increase learning robustness on the two harder tasks, with their advantage growing as observation noise increases, while combining resets with layer normalization underperforms resets alone at every observation noise level tested. Further, stochasticity can accelerate the loss of plasticity in harder environments, whereas in easier settings noise can act as a regularizer that sustains plasticity. Lastly, plasticity loss is most pronounced in the early stages of training. In the most challenging stochastic settings, TD instability rather than gradual plasticity loss becomes the dominant failure mode.
  </details>

- **[There Is No Free Loop: When Does Looping a Trained Model Pay for Its Inference Compute?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42030)**  `arXiv:2610.42030`  
  _Rhea Sanjay Sukthanker, Pashmina Cameron_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looping re-applies a block of layers so that each token passes through the block more than once. Adding a loop to a trained model is attractive because it leaves the parameter count, and hence the disk size, the memory footprint and the training cost unchanged. Unfortunately, every generated token pays for the loop. Re-applying m of N layers makes each token about (N+m)/N times as expensive. Loops that a model learns during large-scale pretraining justify this price, but whether loops added after pretraining justify this cost remains unclear. We compare a looped model with two simple baselines. We first compare it with the same model trained identically without the loop (equal parameters). Secondly, we compare it with the best unlooped use of the same inference floating-point operations (equal FLOPs). Across Qwen3 and Qwen3.5 models from 0.8B to 9B parameters, only the looped 0.8B model beats both baselines. At 4B and 9B, the looped model does not beat the first baseline, i.e. the same model trained without the loop. This is not because the benchmarks are too easy, since on the same benchmarks the unlooped 9B model still scores up to 13 points higher than the 4B model, showing that there is ample room for improvement. Introducing loops in an already pretrained model therefore only pays for small models with a fixed parameter budget.
  </details>

- **[CausalXRL: Explainable Reinforcement Learning through Causal Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42028)**  `arXiv:2610.42028`  
  _Yanming Zhang, Eric Papenhausen, Klaus Mueller_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning is a powerful paradigm for training autonomous agents and has achieved impressive performance in complex environments. However, this success often comes at the cost of interpretability, diminishing trust and complicating efforts to debug and improve agent behavior. To address these challenges, we introduce CausalXRL, a novel framework for explainable reinforcement learning (XRL). A key feature of CausalXRL is its use of causal graph reasoning, which provides transparent, structured, multi-level explanations of agent decision-making. We validate CausalXRL through comprehensive case studies and a two-part evaluation: (1) a quantitative analysis of explanation fidelity and causal-structure learning efficiency in benchmark RL environments, and (2) a qualitative expert study assessing explainability in the real-time strategy (RTS) benchmark MicroRTS. The quantitative results show that CausalXRL can provide faithful explanations while efficiently learning causal structures, and the qualitative expert study suggests that participants found CausalXRL useful for inspecting high-level RTS strategies.
  </details>

- **[GhostPrefix: Synchronization-Free Universal Prefix Attacks for ASR Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42026)**  `arXiv:2610.42026`  
  _Shengding Liu, Ashwin Innuganti, Qiben Yan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present GhostPrefix, the first synchronization-free universal adversarial prefix attack against automatic speech recognition (ASR) systems. Unlike prior audio attacks, which are typically crafted per utterance, require synchronization with the victim waveform, or target narrow objectives, GhostPrefix learns a short, reusable audio segment prepended to arbitrary live speech to exert real-time control over ASR outputs without knowledge of the future utterance or temporal alignment. A key challenge is maintaining stable adversarial effects across unknown, evolving speech contexts. GhostPrefix combines token-level losses with a novel representation-level objective for stable, input-agnostic effects. GhostPrefix supports three attack modes: (i) prompted prefix attacks for semantic steering, (ii) targeted prefix attacks for transcript hijacking, and (iii) untargeted prefix attacks for transcription degradation. To remain perceptually inconspicuous, we regularize prefixes toward benign environmental-sound distributions and improve robustness against over-the-air distortions. We evaluate GhostPrefix across 13 open-source ASR models from four families. In digital settings, it achieves average attack success rates of 97.6% for prompted steering and 93.4% for targeted hijacking, while driving word error rate to 1.18 under untargeted attacks. Over the air, it achieves success rates of 91.5% and 89.5% for prompted and targeted modes, respectively. These manipulations propagate into downstream AI-assisted pipelines, including voice assistants and AI note-taking tools, causing systematic semantic misinterpretations. GhostPrefix exposes synchronization-free universal prefixes as a practical, under-explored threat to real-world ASR and downstream applications. Artifacts and demos are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Know When the Equation Is Wrong: Structural Uncertainty for LLM-Guided Symbolic Regression under Extrapolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42024)**  `arXiv:2610.42024`  
  _Mostafa Cham, Bayu Adhi Tama, Jianwu Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can help discover mathematical equations from data, but most LLM-guided symbolic regression methods focus on a single best equation without quantifying uncertainty over alternative functional forms. This is challenging because distinct equations can fit the observed data similarly while producing substantially different extrapolations. Our experiments on the LLM-SRBench dataset show that standard conformal intervals achieve nominal in-distribution coverage but frequently fail under extrapolation. To tackle this challenge, we introduce LLM-SR-UQ, a framework that retains competing equation structures and uses their disagreement to characterize uncertainty about the underlying functional form. We extend the standard fixed-width conformal approach to quantify structural intervals, which measure disagreement among candidate equations and identify where prediction intervals should widen. Extensive experiments show our framework substantially improves coverage attainment by incorporating structural disagreement while preserving in-distribution coverage. Our approach also shows good generalizability when it is applied to other symbolic-regression methods.
  </details>

- **[When Does Jacobian Fine-Tuning Stabilize Neural PDE Emulators?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42013)**  `arXiv:2610.42013`  
  _Shreeyam Bangera, Manya Gupta_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural emulators of chaotic PDEs are trained on one-step prediction but deployed autoregressively, where their errors compound. Jacobian-based regularisers aim to curb this growth, but in a chaotic system part of the growth is physical and cannot be trained away. We separate the two sources of this error with a first-order decomposition. We compare the emulator's error with a counterfactual that propagates the same one-step mistakes through the solver's Jacobian, giving an amplification ratio: the factor by which the model's own dynamics amplify its errors. We study 21 configurations across four PDEs, five architectures and two model sizes (8-10k and 33k parameters), with errors measured at one Lyapunov time. Jacobian fine-tuning reduced the error by more than 10% for 1 of 34 seeds whose amplification ratio was at most 1.25, and for all 20 seeds whose ratio was at least four; for the latter, the error fell by a factor of 8 (small models) and 55 (33k models) in geometric mean over seeds. The gain approximately equals the one-step accuracy cost of fine-tuning divided by the amplification ratio. Matching the solver's Jacobian along the model's own error direction (EDJM) outperformed random directions at the same cost on 28 of 35 seeds; input-noise injection performed comparably to EDJM.
  </details>

- **[Less Loss, Fixed Weights: Logit Corrections for Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42000)**  `arXiv:2610.42000`  
  _Hanyu Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a language model is fine-tuned to follow instructions, or further pretrained on a new subject, it often gets worse at predicting standard web text. This is known as "forgetting." We ask whether this loss can be undone by simple, fixed adjustments to the model's final token predictions. Across 14 pairs of base and derived models, from 1B to 7B parameters, we fitted a fixed bonus or penalty for each token (a vocabulary bias), alone or together with a temperature. On text held out from correction fitting, the largest top-4K bias recovery estimate is under 10%, with loss conditional on the retained vocabulary. Combining the bias with a temperature recovers up to 36%. Models further pretrained on math lose the most and recover almost nothing. Finally, in a controlled simulation where a model is damaged at random, with no planted bias or temperature change, the bias-plus-temperature fix still recovers 29%. Most of the real-model loss increase stays out of reach of our restricted fits.
  </details>

- **[New Lower Bounds for Private PAC Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41980)**  `arXiv:2610.41980`  
  _Prathamesh Dharangutte, Amartya Sanyal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study realizable PAC learning under approximate differential privacy, and give a framework for proving lower bounds on its sample complexity. We construct classes with identical VC and Littlestone dimensions but different private sample complexities at common privacy and accuracy parameters, showing that these dimensions do not determine the sample complexity, even for improper learners. To address this we introduce the Independent Class Packing (ICP) parameter, the largest total sample complexity of classes in $\mathcal{G}$ that can be packed independently into a class $\mathcal{H}$, and prove that every $(\varepsilon, \delta)$-DP, $(\alpha, \beta)$-PAC learner for $\mathcal{H}$, possibly improper, uses $\Omega\left(\mathrm{ICP}(\mathcal{H}, \mathcal{G})/(\alpha\varepsilon)\right)$ examples. Using this framework we prove tight lower bounds for axis-aligned rectangles, union of intervals and linear subspaces and improved lower bounds for halfspaces and convex polygons. Finally we also show that for product classes, and more generally when the same classes pack into $\mathcal{H}$ and cover its domain so that target labels can be used directly, ICP characterizes private PAC sample complexity up to logarithmic factors.
  </details>

- **[Robust Learning of Multi-Index Models under Logconcave Distributions via Weak Partial Learners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41961)**  `arXiv:2610.41961`  
  _Ilias Diakonikolas, Daniel M. Kane, Mingchen Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We develop a new algorithmic framework for learning Boolean concept classes under logconcave distributions. Our framework applies to broad families of multi-index models and yields significantly faster robust learners than were previously known in these settings. It extends to partial classification and testable learning. Consequently, we obtain improved learners for several fundamental concept classes, including functions of halfspaces and multiclass linear classifiers, under logconcave marginals.Our starting point is a new boosting framework based on weak partial learners. Classical boosting approaches require often-intractable distribution-free weak learners or yield weak approximation guarantees. We show instead that weak learners for partial classification, where labels may be undefined on part of the domain, suffice for strong agnostic learning. The key challenge is that boosting requires a weak learner that succeeds under conditioned distributions that are not guaranteed to be logconcave. To address this, we develop a recursive moment-based partitioning procedure: given a logconcave distribution $X$ and a conditional version $X'$, we partition the space into polynomially many convex regions so that, on most regions, the low-degree moments of $X$ and $X'$ approximately agree. On each good region, a well-behaved multi-index model correlates with a low-degree polynomial, giving a weak hypothesis. Combining these hypotheses yields the required weak learner.A key ingredient of our approach is an efficient method for detecting moment discrepancy. Specifically, we show how to find a low-dimensional subspace that captures any non-trivial discrepancy between $X$ and $X'$, if one exists. Our approach combines spectral analysis of moment tensors with recent progress on isoperimetry for logconcave measures. This discrepancy-finding procedure may be of independent interest.
  </details>

- **[Binary Sparse Codes: Where are we on Energy Efficient Representations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41933)**  `arXiv:2610.41933`  
  _Jose Miguel Ramos, Luis SaCouto, Andreas Wichert_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biological neural systems achieve impressive computational capabilities under tight metabolic constraints, relying on sparse cortical representations that minimise total neural activity. This combination of efficiency and representational power has motivated growing interest in sparse coding as a computational strategy for artificial systems. Artificial models designed to produce binary sparse codes span three broad paradigms, theoretical methods grounded in efficient coding, deep learning methods trained via backpropagation, and biologically plausible methods that learn through local Hebbian rules, each yielding codes whose quality depends not only on achieving low activation density but also on preserving the informational structure of the input. The latter property, however, lacks an agreed-upon quantitative definition, leaving the field without a principled basis for comparing methods. We propose a set of metrics that formalise information preservation as similarity preservation, measuring how faithfully a binary sparse code reproduces the relational geometry of the input space, and apply this framework to compare four representative methods under matched conditions. The results reveal a consistent trade-off in which models that achieve high information preservation do so at the cost of sparsity, while models approaching the uniformity of an optimal random sparse code tend to sacrifice it. Competitive Sparse Codes, a biologically plausible approach, is the only architecture to occupy a region of near-maximal sparsity while retaining meaningful information preservation. These findings frame the simultaneous satisfaction of both factors as an open challenge for the field and provide a reusable evaluation framework for measuring progress toward it.
  </details>

- **[On k-nearest-neighbor regression in metric spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41896)**  `arXiv:2610.41896`  
  _László Györfi, Vladimir G. Pestov, Harro Walk, Roi Weiss_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study mean squared consistency of k-nearest-neighbor regression for metric space valued features. For a fixed feature distribution mu with atomless distance distributions, we describe how neighbor selection redistributes probability through the density of a uniformly chosen neighbor of an independent query. This gives separate conditions for the approximation error and the conditional response variance, and weakens Stone's domination condition when stronger response moments are available. In particular, when k_n tends to infinity and k_n/n tends to zero, a uniform L2(mu) bound on the selection densities suffices under a finite fourth response moment. We give a corresponding condition in terms of neighborhoods of fixed population mass and recover the Euclidean density bound. Finite global Nagata dimension gives Stone's domination. While sigma-finite Nagata dimensionality is known to ensure universal consistency of k-NN classification in complete separable spaces, we show that this is not the case for mean squared consistency with unbounded responses: we construct a space in this class on which one fixed noiseless response with all finite moments has diverging mean squared error.
  </details>

- **[Trainable Vector Quantization for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41822)**  `arXiv:2610.41822`  
  _Anastasiia Prutianova, Alina Bogdanova, Mikhail Shutov, Dmitrii Poimanov, Anatoly Gorkov, Ilia Zharikov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vector Quantization (VQ) is a rapidly evolving technique for compressing Large Language Model (LLM) weights, reducing memory usage, and speeding up inference. However, extreme compression deteriorates model performance, which is challenging to recover due to the discrete nature of VQ parameters. Existing methods employ training-free approaches that are limited to layer-wise reconstruction loss minimization. To address this limitation, we propose TVQ, a novel method that leverages straight-through estimation to enable gradient descent-based optimization of vector-quantized LLMs. By reformulating VQ LLM optimization with trainable latent weights, TVQ enables arbitrary objective minimization. We constitute our approach by applying TVQ in a block-wise knowledge distillation paradigm, which balances quality and training efficiency, and propose several practical solutions to accelerate training. In experiments on modern LLMs, we demonstrate that TVQ is compatible with modern VQ schemes. As a result, TVQ achieves state-of-the-art quality in low-bit post-training quantization settings with a modest training budget and straightforward PyTorch implementation. For instance, TVQ achieves near-lossless 2-bit quantization for LLaMA2-70B with only 1% accuracy drop on popular Question-Answering benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[ElTab: Elastically Looped Tabular Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41819)**  `arXiv:2610.41819`  
  _Mieszko Komisarczyk, Saurabh Mathur, Maurice Kraus, Sriraam Natarajan, Kristian Kersting_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular transformer models such as TabPFN achieve strong predictive performance but incur substantial inference costs, making them difficult to deploy in resource-constrained settings. While looped transformers reduce parameter count through weight sharing, they can lose significant predictive power when stopped early. To address this challenge, we introduce the Elastically Looped Tabular Transformer (ElTab), a single model trained to operate across different computational budgets through supervision at randomly sampled early exits. Across 30 OpenML classification datasets, a single ElTab model matches six depth-specific looped models while cutting training cost by more than half, and matches a 12-layer TabPFN-style baseline with 25% fewer inference FLOPs. These results show that elastic-depth training yields a single model that adapts to the deployment budget at hand, offering a favorable trade-off among predictive performance, storage, and computational cost.
  </details>

- **[DIFFNAS: Predictor-Free Neural Architecture Search via Diffusion Model and Zero-Cost Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41818)**  `arXiv:2610.41818`  
  _Yize Sun, Hanzhang Ma, Zixin Wu, Xuechun Li, Volker Tresp, Yunpu Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural Architecture Search (NAS) has advanced the automated design of deep networks, but remains computationally expensive due to reliance on model training or performance predictors. We introduce DIFFNAS, a predictor-free NAS framework that leverages a discrete graph diffusion model guided by zero-cost performance proxies. Unlike prior diffusion-based NAS methods that require learned performance predictors, DIFFNAS conditions the generative process on training-free metrics such as MeCo and SWAP, enabling efficient architecture search with minimal computational overhead. Experiments on NAS-Bench-201 show that DIFFNAS finds the optimal architectures for CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 (94.37% and 73.51%) without a pretrained predictor, while reducing search cost by up to 17.5x compared to DiffusionNAG. On the MobileNetV3 search space, its accuracy is lower than that of DiffusionNAG. These results highlight the promise of diffusion models combined with zero-cost proxies for scalable and effective architecture search.
  </details>

- **[Continual Reinforcement Learning Is an Integration Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41809)**  `arXiv:2610.41809`  
  _Nis Meinert, Jakob Gawlikowski, Edward Grant, Max Cyprys, Nathan Schurr, Neil Boston, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents an opinionated set of ten desiderata intended to inform the design and evaluation of reinforcement-learning agents that learn continually throughout a continuing lifetime. We use these desiderata to examine a representative selection of existing approaches. Progress on individual desiderata is valuable, but the longer-term aim should be to integrate all ten within a single agent. To our knowledge, no published evaluation has yet provided evidence for such an integration. Likewise, the representative benchmarks we review probe different parts of this target, but separate evaluations do not establish that the corresponding properties coexist within one agent throughout a continuing lifetime. As a secondary contribution, we therefore present a compact persistent-world scenario as a playable companion to the desiderata and outline a proposed benchmark that examines a broad range of associated behavioral capabilities together within one such lifetime.
  </details>

- **[Backpropagation-free Deep Learning for Time-Series Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41805)**  `arXiv:2610.41805`  
  _Miha Kapš, Blaž Bertalanič, Carolina Fortuna_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep neural networks for time series classification (TSC) are trained almost exclusively by error backpropagation, which imposes update locking and the weight-transport problem. Backpropagation-free learning rules such as Direct Feedback Alignment (DFA), Direct Random Target Projection (DRTP), and the diffusion-inspired NoProp relax these constraints but have been evaluated almost exclusively on 2D image benchmarks. We adapt these three learners to univariate TSC with 1D convolutional architectures and present the first systematic study of them across 112 datasets from the UCR time series archive, with one hyperparameter-optimized configuration per method. The optimized learners reach mean F1 scores of 0.76 to 0.79, below ROCKET (0.83) and InceptionTime (0.84). We propose an analysis framework that relates the per-dataset gap to InceptionTime to dataset properties and tests convolutional feature learning by replacing the flatten readout with global average pooling, measured as a difference-in-differences (DD) against a backpropagation control on the identical architecture. Intra-class temporal variability is the dataset property most consistently associated with the gap for all three learners (partial Spearman $\rho$ from $0.28$ to $0.40$). Pooling harms DFA and DRTP more than backpropagation (DD $-0.025$ and $-0.055$), consistent with near-random convolutional features, whereas NoProp tolerates pooling as backpropagation does (DD $+0.013$). Relative to backpropagation on the same architectures, DRTP reduces training floating-point operations (FLOPs) by 33% and trains faster only on series longer than about 700 time steps, DFA yields no computational reduction, and NoProp reduces peak memory by 38% at the cost of 48% longer training.
  </details>

- **[On-device Detection of Wearer-induced NLOS on Chest-worn UWB Tags: Machine Learning, Link Indicators, or Both?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41799)**  `arXiv:2610.41799`  
  _Markus Gallacher, Vu Anh Minh Le, Matteo Trobinger, Davide Vecchia, Carlo Alberto Boano, Gian Pietro Picco_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ultra-wideband (UWB) enables high-accuracy distance estimation (ranging), but performance degrades in non-line-of-sight (NLOS) conditions. When UWB tags are worn, the human body inevitably causes a wearer-induced NLOS (WNLOS), yielding significant errors; still, despite its relevance, WNLOS remains understudied. Moreover, WNLOS detection typically must occur on-device at the tag, requiring classification accuracy to be balanced against tight runtime and energy constraints often hard to meet with machine learning (ML) -- the dominating NLOS classification approach. Meanwhile, UWB radio link indicators (LI) offer a faster and lightweight alternative that has rarely been explored. We study existing on-device ML and LI classifiers when applied to WNLOS, and propose novel hybrid ones exploiting their complementarity. We focus on chest-worn tags and analyze 21 classifiers on a dataset of 1.5 million samples across channels, PHY settings, environments, subjects, and orientations. Results show that while all of them achieve good classification accuracy, relative performance, including ranging errors due to misclassification, depends heavily on channel and PHY configuration. Runtime and energy efficiency also vary dramatically; some techniques offer marginal accuracy gains while consuming up to 39.6x more energy. Therefore, we also introduce a recommender tool to identify optimal classifiers under user-defined constraints, publicly released with our dataset.
  </details>

- **[Accurate and Auditable ECG Classification via Visibility Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41795)**  `arXiv:2610.41795`  
  _Artur Xavier, Guilherme Henrique Gomes Evangelista, Aitazaz Khan, Antonio Luiz Pinho Ribeiro, Mohammed J. Zaki, Srinivasan Parthasarathy, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physicians read electrocardiograms (ECGs) from waveform shape and timing, yet accurate deep-learning classifiers rarely show which waveform regions drive their predictions. We introduce LYNX-ECG, a graph neural network that represents each heartbeat as a sparse visibility graph of timestamped turning points. On CODE-Test, LYNX-ECG reaches a macro F1 score of 0.889, outperforming five baselines including the ECGFounder foundation model, while its graphs need about 14% of the storage of graphs built from every sample. Because each node maps to a specific ECG time, we can delete clinically defined waveform regions and alter beat timing to audit predictions. For conduction disorders, the clinically expected region was the strongest evidence in more than 90% of correct predictions. Bradycardia and tachycardia calls follow heart rate with conduction intervals held fixed, and atrial fibrillation calls vanish when beat-to-beat variation is removed. Sparse visibility graphs thus pair accurate ECG classification with clinically grounded, testable explanations.
  </details>

- **[Implicit Assumptions About Tasks in Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41792)**  `arXiv:2610.41792`  
  _Kathrin Korte, Christian Metzner, Akshay Bedhotiya, Viet Anh Khoa Tran, Eleni Nisioti, Sebastian Risi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual learning is the study of how a model accumulates knowledge from data received over time, rather than all at once. In practice, this is typically achieved by dividing a dataset into tasks and presenting them sequentially. Explanations of the resulting measurements take into account factors such as the similarity between segments, the rate at which the data changes and the order of presentation. Each of these factors can be defined in terms of the underlying data, the partition imposed on it, or the learner that encounters it. These levels do not necessarily coincide, and it is usually left implicit which level a reported quantity refers to. We separate these and identify which properties acquire a value at each level. In controlled teacher--student experiments, we demonstrate that partitioning the same underlying trajectory at different levels of granularity, changing the order of presentation, and varying the similarity structure of the underlying data all result in different measured outcomes. We review representative benchmarks to determine which of these properties are stated. Where the underlying data cannot be inspected, as in foundation models built on undisclosed pre-training corpora, its properties are inherited by the model's initial state, and the training applied on top of it can be described.
  </details>

- **[Latent-Lens: Visual Perception in Small Language Models Through Latent Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41751)**  `arXiv:2610.41751`  
  _Mo Shahdloo, Faegheh Sardari, Mathew Salvaris_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bridging independent vision-language models (VLMs) and small language models (SLMs) in a modular multi-agent system typically forces them to exchange information as discrete text, which creates a lossy bottleneck for spatial and fine-grained details. This constraint is especially acute for SLMs, whose limited capacity makes them more reliant on the quality of the input signal. Recent latent-communication methods exchange states between language models. How a VLM can pass visual information to a separately pretrained text-only SLM though remains unexplored. We propose \textbf{Latent-Lens}, a lightweight codec that bridges the representation gap between a frozen VLM and a frozen SLM directly in latent space, enabling them to communicate purely through hidden states. The codec consists of a domain encoder that projects hidden states from the VLM into the SLM's embedding space, and an attention decoder formalised as a LoRA that teaches the SLM to attend to these visual prefixes, while training only ${\sim}1.6$M parameters (0.2\% of the combined model size). On visual question answering (CLEVR, GQA), Latent-Lens achieves $81.0\pm7.9$\% and $65.4\pm5.4$\%, compared with $58.3\pm5.7$\% and $61.8\pm1.0$\% for text communication with the same LoRA budget (+30.2--69.8pp relative to frozen text communication), while on image captioning (Flickr8k) it improves over the same baseline by +16.5 (+18.8 frozen). We also evaluate Latent-Lens with TinyLlama-1.1B and InternVL2-1B, which use different architectures, vocabularies, and hidden dimensions.
  </details>

- **[Do LLMs Know When They Are Wrong About Molecules? In-Context Property Prediction with Self-Reported Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41745)**  `arXiv:2610.41745`  
  _Matthias Busch, Marius Tacke, Tamadur Albaraghtheh, Sviatlana V. Lamaka, Mikhail L. Zheludkevich, Roland C. Aydin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are used in many domains, but molecular property prediction with them is still little explored. In this work we evaluate the capability of 7 frontier LLMs to predict molecular properties together with a self-reported uncertainty from in-context examples, and compare them with classical baselines trained on the same molecules. We use 4 datasets and screen each for contamination of the training data of the LLMs: three are clean, and the fourth, ESOL, is memorised by most of the models and serves as a comparison. On the clean datasets every LLM improves with the context, at 30 examples the most accurate one beats every baseline. The requested reasoning effort changes accuracy little. The self-reported uncertainty ranks the prediction errors weakly, with Spearman rank correlations of up to 0.46, about the level reached by the uncertainties of the baselines. Combining uncertainty and prediction, on lipophilicity the most certain half of the best LLM is the most accurate at small context sizes; on BACE and antiviral, and on lipophilicity at large context sizes, the best LLMs are on par with the best baseline. On the contaminated ESOL dataset the LLMs show no improvement with the context size, as expected for a memorised dataset, and their stated uncertainty is smaller on the molecules whose value they recall.
  </details>

- **[Task Vectors Can Be Linearly Decomposed into Interpretable and Controllable Components](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41743)**  `arXiv:2610.41743`  
  _Yukiya Horiba, Hakaze Cho, Takahiro Yakoh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Task Vectors (TVs) provide a compact way to steer large language models, but they are typically treated as atomized directions merely extracted from hidden states, leaving their internal functional structure poorly understood. Motivated by prior work that decomposes in-context learning into Task Recognition (TR) and Task Learning (TL), we ask whether a single TV can likewise be decomposed into functionally meaningful components. We formulate a TR/TL-based decomposition from candidate-label unembeddings, separating each TV into TR, TL, and residual components, and try to optimize the TR and TL coefficients using Bayesian optimization for more effective intervention. We evaluate this formulation across multiple model families, classification tasks, and both instruction- and demonstration-based TVs. Across these settings, TR/TL-based optimization identifies intervention directions that outperform the original TVs. Although optimization over random directions can also improve performance, TR/TL-based search yields more reliable gains, particularly for instruction-based TVs. Moreover, isolated interventions on the decomposed components produce distinct logit-level effects that are consistent with their intended functional roles. Together, these results suggest that TVs are not monolithic steering directions, but can be decomposed into functionally meaningful components that support both interpretation and control.
  </details>

- **[Leakage-Controlled Structure-Preserving Augmentation and Template-Aware Evaluation for Bangla Symptom-Based Disease Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41733)**  `arXiv:2610.41733`  
  _Sabah Nushra, Shovan Samanta Turzo, Moinul Hossain, Md Kishor Morol_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bangla symptom-based disease prediction is commonly evaluated on a small, imbalanced structured benchmark containing many profiles that differ by only one or two symptoms. We test whether train-only asymmetric Bernoulli perturbation improves classification using 725 unique profiles spanning 85 diseases and 172 binary symptoms. Experiments use ten repeated real-profile holdouts and parent-grouped inner cross-validation. The 2 x 2 design separates augmentation from hyperparameter tuning, with exact duplication, random oversampling, class weighting, and SMOTEN as balancing controls. For Bernoulli Naive Bayes (BNB), the gain over tuned raw training does not withstand Holm correction, although BNB attains the highest mean accuracy (97.50%). The boosted-tree models benefit substantially: XGBoost, LightGBM, and CatBoost improve by 18.10, 28.45, and 10.09 percentage points, respectively. A Hamming-distance audit places 91.0% of standard held-out profiles within one symptom bit of the training data. Accuracy decreases on a same-disease 1-bit-purged challenge; nevertheless, XGBoost and LightGBM retain large augmentation gains. We also evaluate robustness to missing and spurious symptoms and the faithfulness of BNB explanations. Overall, Bernoulli perturbation is useful for some model families, but benchmark accuracy should be interpreted together with template proximity.
  </details>

- **[UpLoop: Training Looped Transformers via Upcycling at No Extra Pretraining Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41720)**  `arXiv:2610.41720`  
  _Cheng Liang, Tengfei Zhang, Ya Zhang, Yanfeng Wang, Zhouhan Lin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped language models can strengthen reasoning through repeated computation with shared weights, increasing execution depth without adding independent parameters. However, each additional pass increases training floating-point operations (FLOPs), making these gains more expensive to obtain. To address this cost, we propose UpLoop, a pretraining schedule that uses upcycling to train looped Transformers while exactly matching a standard dense baseline in final parameter count, total training tokens, and training FLOPs. Specifically, UpLoop begins with a shallow Transformer, progressively grows its depth by copying learned layers, and uses the computation saved early to support repeated execution of a shared middle block later in training. Experiments across model scales show improvements in language modeling and downstream performance, with the average score across 22 downstream tasks increasing from 22.99 to 27.52 at 1.3B parameters. These gains also extend to mathematical reasoning after supervised fine-tuning.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Residuals: A Minimalist Decoupling of Residual Width from Compute Width](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41711)**  `arXiv:2610.41711`  
  _Ziteng Wang, Jianqiao Lu, Xinyu Ma, Jintao Zhang, Jun Zhu, Yiyuan Ma, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
