  Residual connections underpin Transformers, but they implicitly tie the residual width carried through depth to the compute width used inside each layer. We introduce Hierarchical Residuals (HiRes), a minimalist architectural change that decouples these dimensions, widening the residual state while keeping per-layer compute width fixed. HiRes groups layers into blocks: standard d-dimensional PreNorm layers operate inside each block, while a wider D-dimensional PostNorm outer residual connects blocks. Learned read/write projections bridge the two spaces at block boundaries, leaving individual Transformer layers unchanged. Across different model sizes and depths, HiRes consistently outperforms vanilla residual addition, with fitted scaling laws indicating a 1.30-fold improvement in FLOPs efficiency. On OLMoE-1B-7B pretraining for 500B tokens, HiRes achieves 0.0208 lower training loss and 1.14-point higher downstream accuracy. HiRes is also competitive with other residual expansion methods such as VWN, mHC and AttnRes with less activation memory and I/O costs. Built from only dense projections and normalizations, it requires neither specialized kernels nor infrastructure-level optimizations. HiRes thus exposes residual width as a practical scaling variable using only standard operators.
  </details>

- **[From WSIs to Bulk, Spatial and Single-Cell Transcriptomics via Multi-Scale Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41692)**  `arXiv:2610.41692`  
  _Miguel Andrade, José Almeida, Carlos Santiago, Catarina Barata_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding the spatial distribution of gene expression is key to studying complex diseases like cancer. While Spatial Transcriptomics (ST) and Single-Cell Transcriptomics (scT) provide high-resolution insights, they remain expensive and scarce. In contrast, standard histopathology Whole-Slide Images (WSIs) paired with bulk RNA sequencing are widely available. This work introduces a Multi-Scale Graph Neural Network (GNN) capable of simultaneously predicting bulk, spatial, and single-cell transcriptomics directly from WSIs. By extracting morphological features using a Pathology Foundation Model (PFM) (UNI2-h) and constructing spatial graphs at both patch and cell levels, the model learns the hierarchical relationships within the tissue. The network is trained on a combined dataset of The Cancer Genome Atlas Program (TCGA), Visium, and Xenium samples across lung, kidney, and breast tissues. A hybrid training strategy is employed, leveraging direct supervision alongside weakly supervised consistency losses to ensure predictions align across biological scales. Results show the model accurately predicts gene expression at the slide level and moderately at the patch level (strongest on Xenium), and achieves moderate correlation at the challenging single-cell level. Furthermore, downstream tasks explore the clinical relevance of these predictions, demonstrating their utility in survival analysis, spatial hazard mapping, and tumor classification via molecular signatures.
  </details>

- **[On the Generation Order in GFlowNets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41648)**  `arXiv:2610.41648`  
  _Timofei Gritsaev, Evgeny Katasonov, Ildus Sadrtdinov, Esmeralda S. Whitammer, Dmitry Vetrov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sampling from discrete distributions specified by unnormalised probability functions is a classical statistical problem. GFlowNets address this problem by constructing objects sequentially, adding one discrete component at a time in either a predefined or a learned order. The choice of order determines which conditional distributions the neural network must learn, some of which may be easier to learn than others. Yet whether existing GFlowNet algorithms discover interpretable generation orders and make use of them during training remains unexplored. We introduce controlled problems where generation order is experimentally shown to affect convergence and standard GFlowNet training struggles to discover useful orders. To address this difficulty, we develop Learning-Order GFlowNet, a training recipe that learns interpretable generation orders without order annotations and improves convergence on these problems. Finally, we investigate gradient signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) as a possible explanation for order emergence: orders associated with faster convergence exhibit higher SNR at comparable validation loss, suggesting both a mechanism favouring their emergence and an SNR-based characterisation of a good generation order.
  </details>

- **[A Constructive Orbit-Based Theory of Learnability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41641)**  `arXiv:2610.41641`  
  _Yunied Puig_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We develop a finite-dimensional orbit framework for constructing data-adapted dictionaries, sparse realizations, and predictive extensions. The method uses wrapped weighted shifts to generate orbit dictionaries and local interpolation maps from independent orbit representatives, turning the learning problem into three explicit tasks: scaffold discovery, sparse realization on the discovered dictionary, and prediction through learned sparse coordinates. We introduce CCDS as the main constructive ingredient: a deviation-controlled sparse realization procedure that recovers MP/OMP-type behavior at the minimal-deviation boundary while allowing feasible interior paths with prescribed residual geometry that is inaccessible to MP/OMP. We prove generic orbit independence, exact block interpolation with norm control, observable bounds on sparse approximation distortion, sparse realization guarantees on selected supports, and fixed-path stability under admissible perturbations of the block interpolants. These results connect sparse approximation, interpolation, and finite-dimensional operator dynamics through the construction and analysis of data-induced orbit dictionaries. Experiments on synthetic geometry, controlled realization tests, and standard classification benchmarks show that selected orbit scaffolds have substantially better matched-rank projection behavior than random orbit or unstructured alternatives, that CCDS attains near-zero realization error at the minimal-deviation boundary, and that predictors trained from the sparse CCDS certificates give competitive performance.
  </details>

- **[FOCUS: First-Order Control Using Optimal Transport Supervision for Imitation from Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41540)**  `arXiv:2610.41540`  
  _João A. Cândido Ramos, Lionel Blondé, Hugues Vinzant, Jacopo Castellini, Alexandros Kalousis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Imitation learning from observations must recover behavior from state-only demonstrations without known temporal correspondence. Differentiable simulators provide pathwise policy gradients, but differentiating through long, contact-rich trajectories can make optimization unstable. We introduce FOCUS, which combines local optimal-transport alignment with short-horizon differentiable actor-critic learning. FOCUS matches agent rollout windows to multiple sampled expert segments using a soft best-of-$K$ objective that emphasizes locally compatible matches. The resulting transport-based costs provide differentiable imitation rewards, while a learned critic bootstraps value beyond the rollout horizon. FOCUS requires neither temporally aligned trajectories nor expert-aligned initialization or teacher forcing. Across contact-rich locomotion tasks in DFlex, including a musculoskeletal humanoid controlled by 152 muscle activations, FOCUS improves sample efficiency and training stability over adversarial imitation-from-observation methods and prior differentiable imitation baselines.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Sentiment Classification on Small Datasets through Data Augmentation and Transfer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41518)**  `arXiv:2610.41518`  
  _Mahmoud S. Mayaleh, Samer A. Mayaleh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small-scale sentiment classification often suffers from data scarcity, which limits the generalization ability of the models. This study evaluates and compares the effectiveness of three data augmentation strategies: Easy Data Augmentation (EDA), back-translation, and contextual token substitution (nlpaug-style), with both traditional machine learning classifiers (Logistic Regression, Random Forest) and transformer-based models (BERT). We perform a comprehensive empirical comparison with low-resource sentiment datasets by summarizing the results of recent studies and performing targeted head-to-head experiments. Our findings indicate that all augmentation methods improve performance. Contextual augmentation yields the most consistent gains for BERT models, while EDA and back-translation provide greater benefits for traditional classifiers. These insights help guide the selection of data augmentation techniques tailored to model type and dataset size, filling a critical gap in research on data augmentation for sentiment classification on small datasets.
  </details>

- **[Infinite ResNets are Gaussian Mean ODEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41514)**  `arXiv:2610.41514`  
  _Louis-Pierre Chaintron, Lénaïc Chizat, Javier Maass_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding the training dynamics of deep neural networks in large-scale limits is a question both of foundational and of practical importance. Prior works have obtained such descriptions through dynamical mean field theory (DMFT), yielding a system of non-Markovian dynamics that are challenging to analyze and that obscure the optimization structure of the original dynamics. In this paper, we obtain instead a simple description of the large-scale limit training dynamics for ResNets in standard shape scalings and in the $1/\text{depth}$ branch scaling that allows maximal (local) feature updates. For a model trained with gradient descent (GD) from a random initialization, the limit dynamics is simply GD on a Neural Mean ODE with Gaussian initial weights. Our analysis covers population or empirical risk GD in the joint limit of large width, depth and embedding, input, and output dimensions. We obtain approximation scaling laws that depend on the shape of the ResNet and the covariance spectrum of the data. We also illustrate the flexibility of our approach by discussing Mixture of Expert models, where likewise the limit admits an intuitive (nested) Mean ODE description.
  </details>

- **[PCD: Predictive Coding Dynamics for Irregularly Sampled Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41506)**  `arXiv:2610.41506`  
  _Tianrui Gou, Jiayangmei Xu, Xingzhou Zhang, Lei Sha_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic systems evolve continuously over time, whereas observations in real scenarios are often sparse, irregular, and asynchronous. Accurate prediction therefore requires inferring latent state evolution between observations, as future values depend on partially observed trajectories. We propose Predictive Coding Dynamics (PCD), a residual feedback mechanism for irregular continuous dynamic models. PCD maintains previous reconstructions, predicts observed entries from them, and incorporates masked signed residuals into state recognition through an energy penalty. The mechanism is implemented with ODE-RNN and Latent ODE, supporting event-based hidden updates and latent state recognition. Experiments on forecasting and trajectory recovery tasks show that PCD improves continuous dynamic backbones under sparse and irregular sampling. The results further indicate that predictive error feedback improves latent state estimation when observations constrain the current trajectory. These results suggest that PCD provides a residual feedback framework that jointly supports trajectory generation, observation consistency checking, and state refinement in continuous-time series modeling. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Spectral Haar Binarizer: Compact Boolean Contrast Representations for Hyperspectral Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41478)**  `arXiv:2610.41478`  
  _Michał Romaszewski, Przemysław Głomb_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tsetlin Machines offer an interpretable, hardware-friendly alternative to conventional machine learning models, but applying them to continuous hyperspectral measurements requires converting spectral signals into Boolean representations. Standard binarisation methods typically discretise each spectral band independently, potentially discarding important relationships between neighbouring wavelengths. In this paper, we introduce the Spectral Haar Binarizer (SHB), a contrast-based spectral representation method that constructs Boolean predicates from differences between selected spectral intervals. Inspired by Haar-like contrast features, the method uses an AdaBoost-style selection procedure to obtain a compact set of discriminative spectral predicates. We evaluate SHB on five heterogeneous hyperspectral datasets covering forensic blood detection, agricultural and urban remote sensing, and rainforest tree-species classification. Across binary and multiclass tasks, SHB consistently outperforms standard per-band binarisation strategies and provides the strongest or near-strongest results across classifiers. In binary tasks, TM-SHB achieves the best average performance, while RF-SHB is strongest in multiclass settings. Bayesian and rank-based analyses confirm that SHB's advantage is robust across datasets and not driven by isolated cases. The results show that representing continuous hyperspectral measurements is a central processing step for efficient classification. That contrast-based Boolean features provide an effective bridge between information-rich sensor measurements and compact, logic-based processing.
  </details>

- **[When Links Mislead: Label-Free Structural Polarity for Zero-Shot Classification of Web Pages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41464)**  `arXiv:2610.41464`  
  _Hanshuai Cui, Zhiqing Tang, Zhi Yao, Weijia Jia, Jun Luo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web platforms such as digital libraries, online stores and university sites must classify new pages before anyone labels them. Large language models (LLMs) can label each page from its text, but these labels are noisy, and links could correct them only where linked pages share classes. Existing zero-shot methods ignore the links, or pull a page toward its neighbours as label propagation and graph neural networks (GNNs) do, which hurts where linked pages differ in class. Others weight propagation by an LLM-estimated homophily that costs extra calls and drifts across LLMs. We find that the sign with which links should enter a prediction, which we call structural polarity, is readable without true labels. It is the excess agreement of LLM labels and text-prototype predictions along links. We thus propose ZeroPolar, which gates one signed propagation step by this statistic. Training-free ZeroPolar mixes prototype scores with any number of LLM labels, and ZeroPolar-T denoises the labels with a text classifier once half of the pages carry one. With one GPT-4o label per page, ZeroPolar-T reaches the highest mean accuracy on eleven graphs (73.99%), 6.01 points above LLM-BP and significantly above all eighteen baselines we compare. With LLM labels on only 5% of the pages, training-free ZeroPolar leads a GNN trained on these labels by 10.80 points.
  </details>

- **[RetryRoute: Retry-Aware Model Routing for Agentic Image Editing Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41460)**  `arXiv:2610.41460`  
  _Hanshuai Cui, Zhiqing Tang, Zhi Yao, Jun Luo, Weijia Jia_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web agents increasingly edit images by calling open editing models in a critique-and-retry loop, and these models differ in cost by more than an order of magnitude. After every rejected edit, a service must decide whether to retry the same model, escalate to another or give up. Existing routers pick one model per request, and cascades escalate in a fixed order. However, both treat a retry as an independent draw. We identify sticky failures, meaning that a retry after a failure succeeds only 17-53% as often as a first try across six open models, three benchmarks and two judges. Based on this finding, we propose RetryRoute, a retry-aware router that estimates each model's chance of success given the request's failure history. It plans retries, escalations and abandonment over failure states and adds live queue waits to the cost, so the same plan becomes load-aware dispatch. At the main budget, RetryRoute's success under the in-loop judge exceeds the strongest of eleven prior baselines by 2.8 points on average over eight groups. On a GPU serving testbed it raises the share of edits delivered within the service-level objective by 5.0-6.3 points over the best prior baseline at every load.
  </details>

- **[NormDetect: Anchored Calibration for Low-False-Positive Detection of Machine-Generated Web Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41459)**  `arXiv:2610.41459`  
  _Hanshuai Cui, Fanshuai Meng, Zhiqing Tang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web platforms screen posts for text written by large language models, and every false flag hits a human writer. Detectors must therefore run at a very low false positive rate (FPR). Zero-shot detectors such as Binoculars score text with frozen language models, but invisible edits such as homoglyphs shatter tokenization and push machine text into the human range. Unicode normalization undoes these edits, yet the normalized score still misses half of the machine text at a strict FPR, under attacks that leave nothing to clean. We find that with the Falcon-7B scoring pair, heads on token statistics recall less than the normalized score at this FPR, and several flag too many human posts in some sections. We propose NormDetect, which anchors a learned per-section head to the normalized zero-shot score. A blend weight chosen on training folds under a human FPR cap combines the two scores, and one threshold is calibrated on human text only. On eight sections of the RAID benchmark with eleven attacks, NormDetect raises recall at a 0.1% calibrated FPR by 5.0 points over normalized Binoculars. Its human FPR bound stays at most 1% in every section, and no other detector within this bound recalls as much.
  </details>

- **[When Everyone Can Build a Model: Mechanistic Inference in the Age of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41454)**  `arXiv:2610.41454`  
  _Marvin van Aalst, Adèle H. Ribeiro, Anna Matuszyńska_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern deep learning and AI-assisted code generation are lowering the technical barrier to building mathematical models of biological systems that reproduce complex observations. This lets researchers with domain expertise build semi-mechanistic, hybrid models constrained by prior knowledge and augmented with learned structure without extensive training in modelling. However, easier construction does not make interpretation easier: predictive success of such so-called grey-box models does not establish that an inferred mechanism corresponds to the one that generated the data, and the learned component can absorb errors in the mechanistic part. Many biological modelling problems are underdetermined, so several, possibly infinitely many, models may reproduce the same data equally well. This ambiguity enters at two modelling choices: which variables can affect others, and which response function describes the underlying mechanism. For the first choice, causal inference provides a formal framework stating which causal relationships and intervention effects are identifiable from the data and available knowledge. For the second, ensemble analyses quantify the uncertainty and the range of behaviours still allowed by models that fit equally well. Together, they turn a single good fit into an explicit account of what is supported, what remains open, and which experiment would decide.
  </details>

- **[From Representation to Readout: Mechanistic Analysis of Membership Signals in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41445)**  `arXiv:2610.41445`  
  _Wenxi Dai, Jingyu Hu, Yanting Yang, Mengnan Du_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Membership inference aims to determine whether a particular example was included in a model's training data. Such membership signals are often broadly described as memorization, while the underlying mechanisms can involve both how information is represented internally and how it is accessed by the model to produce behaviors. We therefore present a mechanistic study of membership signals in large language models (LLMs). We introduce Decodable Information Score, Used Information Score, and Readout Accessibility Gap (DUG), a set of complementary measures that separately quantify membership information decodable from internal representations and the extent to which it is exposed through the model's readout. Across supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL)-based membership inference methods, membership-specific effects appear in both the amount of decodable information and readout under SFT, while under RL-based membership inference methods they appear primarily in readout. Activation patching further localizes this membership-specific effect to intermediate layers. To examine whether these findings extend to a more general RL setting, we further study reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) contamination, where the training reward is independent of the reference solution. In this setting, we find that behavioral changes can emerge without a corresponding increase in likelihood-based membership inference signals.
  </details>

- **[A Unified XAI-NSAI Analytical Framework for Trustworthy IoT Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41424)**  `arXiv:2610.41424`  
  _Hugues Leger, Giovani R. Abuaitah_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The widespread deployment of Internet of Things (IoT) devices in critical environments makes anomaly detection essential for identifying abnormal behaviors and supporting timely investigation and response. However, accurate detection alone is insufficient. Practical IoT deployments must also provide explanations, incorporate domain knowledge and reasoning, operate under IoT constraints, and support human decision making. Although recently proposed approaches increasingly incorporate Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) or Neuro-Symbolic Artificial Intelligence (NSAI), XAI and NSAI methods are often considered independently. We argue that they should be considered together to better understand their complementary roles in supporting trustworthy IoT anomaly detection. To address this gap, we propose a unified analytical framework organized around five complementary dimensions. The framework provides a structured basis for characterizing existing approaches, identifying capability gaps and design trade-offs, and guiding the design of new anomaly detection systems for different IoT applications. We demonstrate the framework's analytical utility by applying it to three representative studies and illustrate its design utility through its application to a Medical IoT anomaly detection system.
  </details>

- **[SparseGDN: Chunk Sparse in Linear Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41420)**  `arXiv:2610.41420`  
  _Jiedong Zhuang, Peng Zhao, Jialin Tang, Haoji Hu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gated Delta Networks (GDNs) offer linear-time sequence modeling, yet long-context prefill remains costly due to dense intra-chunk computation, recurrent-state updates, and intermediate state storage. We introduce SparseGDN, a hardware-aware framework that combines structured intra-chunk approximation with input-dependent recurrent-event selection while producing outputs for every token. A block-adjacent solver reduces local Delta-solve computation, while a dynamic selector allocates recurrent-update budgets across head pairs. The GPU implementation materializes updates only for selected events and reconstructs token outputs using compact state snapshots and group-local interactions, reducing computation and memory traffic. We evaluate SparseGDN on Qwen3.5-4B and Qwen3.5-9B across RULER tasks and context lengths from 4K to 128K. Retaining approximately 29% of recurrent events on average, SparseGDN achieves 1.50-1.94x GDN-core prefill speedups over native FLA kernels, including online selection overhead.
  </details>

- **[Certifying Retraining Regret through Residual Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41410)**  `arXiv:2610.41410`  
  _Peida Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We certify a fixed ridge predictor's regret against full-feature retraining over every continuous completion of an interval-valued table. Our main theorem gives verifiable original-row residual conditions for a uniform error bound independent of nuisance-feature scale and rank, with an asymptotically sharp leading constant and quantitative leakage stability. Full-rank constructions separate certification dimension from feature dimension, including examples requiring directions beyond the candidate. Observed-row anchors make the conditions computable, while exact row geometry and global search return valid upper bounds and feasible original-table witnesses. A finite-scale contraction and common-domain moment fallback implement the structural result. Four real-world tasks retain all model features and certify twenty rank-three instances to width $10^{-5}$. Their primary median widths improve over centered moments. Joint residual refinement additionally certifies all twenty new response-independent masks to the same precision within 76.21 seconds. For a concrete fixed-point predictor contract, pure energy certifies thirteen of fifteen stopping decisions in ten seconds, versus five for moments. Refinement certifies all fifteen within 91.68 seconds at the same objective threshold.
  </details>

- **[Anomalies as Residuals: Cauchy Displacement Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41387)**  `arXiv:2610.41387`  
  _Yang Cao, Zihao Guo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neighbourhood-based detectors such as KNN, LOF and LUNAR remain among the most accurate methods for tabular anomaly detection, but they compare every query with the stored training set, so their inference cost and memory grow with the data and the data must be kept at deployment. We ask whether the neighbourhood geometry these detectors exploit can be compiled into a small network that scores a query with a single forward pass. We propose the Cauchy Displacement Network (CDN), which regresses each normal point onto the mean of its $k$ nearest training neighbours with a single-hidden-layer network of Cauchy activations and scores a query by the length of its predicted displacement. This displacement is an empirical mean-shift vector, whose fixed-radius population counterpart locally approximates a scaled gradient of the log density. We show that Cauchy units exactly represent the score fields of a structured family of Student-$t$ expert densities, and establish a constructive approximation bound for the corresponding population neighbourhood-mean map using a single-hidden-layer Cauchy network. We further show that the bounded network output guarantees asymptotically linear residual-score growth along every fixed direction. On 46 benchmark datasets against 28 baselines, CDN obtains the best mean rank under both AUC-ROC and AUC-PR, while scoring $10^4$ queries in a few milliseconds on a single CPU thread, up to three orders of magnitude faster than neighbourhood-based detectors, with a model whose size does not depend on the training set.
  </details>

- **[What We Keep of What We Forget: Replacement Operators and Routing Semantics in Training-Free MoE Expert Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41366)**  `arXiv:2610.41366`  
  _Ezza Ali, Sehrish Bibi, Momina Moetesum, Faisal Shafait_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training-free expert pruning makes two decisions: which experts to remove, and what the layer computes when its router still selects a removed expert. Published methods change both at once. Holding the mask fixed, we compare replacement operators from three families on OLMoE, DeepSeek-V2-Lite and Moonlight, with Granite as a fourth router, using paired, multiplicity-corrected tests. The largest effect is a choice usually left implicit: the survivors' gate scale. On routers trained without top-k renormalization, renormalizing the survivors' gates, a common implementation choice, costs 0.8-8.5 points and reverses whether rerouting beats MoNE's mean-output replacement; on two routers trained with renormalization, the trained scale wins at 50% pruning. We introduce the tilted novice, a closed-form low-rank map of the pruned expert's input whose rank-0 case is MoNE's constant. At 50% pruning it adds 1.2-1.8% parameters and gains 3.45 and 1.37 points over the constant on OLMoE and Moonlight, stable across calibration draws and largely unchanged by 4- and 3-bit quantization. No operator dominates: trained-scale rerouting is best on DeepSeek-V2-Lite, and a partial copy of each pruned expert is statistically indistinguishable from the tilted novice on two models. Evaluation can change conclusions: at 25% pruning MoNE's constant keeps 92-97% of dense multiple-choice accuracy but only 5-65% of GSM8K and 0-17% of code scores, and the calibration domain moves the mask 4-9 times more than resampling. We propose a five-item reporting checklist.
  </details>

- **[Generated storm lifecycles fill the rare-event tail of short rainfall records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41365)**  `arXiv:2610.41365`  
  _Valerie Tsao, Nathaniel W. Chaney, Manolis Veveakis, Marta Zaniolo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Historically rare ''one-in-a-generation'' storms are occurring with unprecedented frequency, causing catastrophic damage and mounting casualties, and exposing the vulnerability of infrastructure designed around short observational records. To overcome the limitations of sparse data, we present StormGen, a generative machine-learning model that transforms an observed storm's initial hours into complete, physically plausible hourly rainfall lifecycles in minutes. Evaluated on a decade of southeast Texas rainfall, StormGen produced close counterparts for 4\5% of the most severe 2\% of storms--compared to just 3\% for stochastic storm transposition--and maintained spatial coherence over full 24-hour sequences. Crucially, from a single hour of initial rainfall, the model successfully reconstructed the specific lifecycle, spatial pattern, and extreme catchment totals of Hurricane Harvey itself, despite the event being entirely absent in training. Because storm timing alone can more than double peak flows, these complete generated scenarios provide a vital mechanism to stress-test flood protections against unprecedented extremes.
  </details>

- **[StrictPRMBench: A Replayable Evaluation Benchmark for Auditing Fixed-Score PRM Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41333)**  `arXiv:2610.41333`  
  _Pengfei Zheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Process reward models (PRMs) score reasoning steps, but best-of-N selection also requires a rule that aggregates those scores. We study that rule as a separate evaluation object. StrictPRMBench fixes mathematical reasoning pools, scorer evidence, and the source trace of a deletion intervention, so that different reducers face the same recorded selection problem. Across seven scoring checkpoints and two benchmarks, the stored N=32 outcomes give higher deletion-induced selection instability for product than geometric mean (GM) in all 14 cells. The equal-cell difference is +0.0965; a shared-problem bootstrap, conditional on these checkpoints and frozen pools, gives a 95% interval of [0.0846,0.1086]. A pre-injection-defined sensitivity excludes replacement slots that are either reducer's clean winner and retains 4,459 of 4,622 cell-problem records. The retained difference is +0.0986 [0.0875,0.1098]. Effects are heterogeneous: the two Qwen cells have positive percentile-bootstrap intervals on retained support but two-sided exact paired p-values of 0.0625 and 0.0703. Clean-pool Qwen accuracy does not detect a product advantage, but this is not an equivalence result. We provide versioned outcome records, CPU reanalysis, and an explicit evidence-availability contract. The contribution is a diagnostic evaluation protocol and a reproducible audit of recorded selector behavior, not a new PRM, a universally optimal reducer, or evidence of deployment-wide harm. Raw-score replay and outcome-record reanalysis are distinguished: missing primary edited-score vectors limit independent evaluation of new reducers under the same stress.
  </details>

- **[Transferring Peptide-Target Interaction Patterns to Small Molecules by Joint Guidance for Diffusion and Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41322)**  `arXiv:2610.41322`  
  _Jian Lee, Changyun Cho, Sun Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating small molecules to target protein-protein interactions (PPIs) remains one of the most challenging yet high-reward frontiers in drug discovery. Unlike traditional small-molecule drugs that bind to deep, well-defined pockets, PPI interfaces often comprise broad, shallow binding surfaces. A natural strategy is to leverage known peptide-target interactions, as peptide ligands provide rich interface-level constraints. However, a major algorithmic challenge is that rigid atom-level conditioning on peptide structures can over-constrain the generative space and exclude valid small-molecule solutions. To address this challenge, we present PIFT (Peptide InterFace Transfer), a novel framework that transfers peptide-target interaction patterns to small molecules through joint sampling-time guidance of masked discrete diffusion and continuous flow matching. PIFT first maps peptide-target and small-molecule-target interfaces into a shared, target-conditioned interaction space, capturing transferable interface information across molecular modalities. The resulting peptide-derived reference then jointly steers building-block logits and 3D coordinates toward peptide-compatible molecules while preserving a strong adapted molecular prior. Extensive experiments across diverse PPI targets show that the learned representation captures cross-modal interaction compatibility, while peptide guidance improves interface recovery and achieves strong docking performance and favorable molecular properties.
  </details>

- **[rl_rob: A transparent, from-scratch Python library for teaching reinforcement learning from tabular methods to deep policy gradients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41303)**  `arXiv:2610.41303`  
  _Abdulrahman Altahhan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  rl_rob is an open-source Python library for teaching reinforcement learning. It implements the arc of a modern course, from tabular temporal-difference learning through linear function approximation to deep value-based and policy-gradient methods, with every algorithm written from first principles so that its update rule is visible in a few lines of code. All agents share one interface, so learners can compare methods directly across stages and trace a deep reinforcement learning update from the temporal-difference error to the weight change. The library supports teaching and assessment in which students implement, modify and explain update rules, rather than only produce working code.
  </details>

- **[SPT-Flow: One Integration, Many Futures in Continuous-Time World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41273)**  `arXiv:2610.41273`  
  _Mingyang Lyu, Yinqian Sun, Gongce Cao, Yuhao Zhang, Lu Jia, Qian Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous-time world models generate a latent trajectory through a single ODE integration, but adding stochastic conditioning does not ensure that the model uses it. In our teacher-forced baseline, posterior means vary by only about 1% of the prior scale, and matched and swapped latents yield indistinguishable reconstruction PSNR at the reported precision. We study a supervision-shortcut hypothesis: ground-truth intermediate states can reduce the need for trajectory-associated latent information. We introduce SPT-Flow, which samples one latent per trajectory and matches a free-running ODE rollout to demonstration states at multiple readout times. Re-anchoring experiments support the proposed mechanism. On LIBERO, with both models trained for 30k optimizer steps, SPT-Flow improves best-of-10 PSNR over our retrained ODEWorld baseline by 2.0 and 2.4 dB at the mid-trajectory and final frames and reduces mean LPIPS by 23%, with comparable per-sample inference cost. The rollout control recovers most of the accuracy gain; learned latent conditioning adds diversity and best-of-K gains while reducing mean sample accuracy relative to this control. The diagnostics support trajectory-associated latent utilization; semantic mode coverage under matched conditions in the robotic setting remains unverified.
  </details>

- **[Corroborative Learning: Mutual Enhancement through Task-Response Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41222)**  `arXiv:2610.41222`  
  _Zexing Zhang, Jichao Li, Tianyang Lei, Yiyin Zhang, Kewei Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In pattern analysis, models trained independently for the same task often develop complementary capabilities through differences in architecture, data, and optimization. Exploiting complementarity while retaining independent deployment is central to collaborative learning. Most methods establish transfer by reducing differences in predictions, relations, or representations between target and collaborator, although these differences also underlie complementarity. This creates a structural trade-off between reducing differences for knowledge exchange and preserving them for complementarity. We therefore propose Corroborative Learning (CL). Each model forms task-driven responses, with cross-group support from its collaborator. Source-excluded reconstruction of response increments from collaborator representations separates fitting and evaluation across disjoint groups and selectively constrains directions by cross-group reconstructability. The process requires no direct output or feature matching or additional inference dependency. Furthermore, with linear reconstruction and fixed collaborator representations, the loss equals a positive semidefinite directional regularizer, constraining less reconstructable directions more strongly. Signal and noise conditions characterize when directional shrinkage reduces response-estimation error. Across six language tasks, 24 models, and 36 pairs, CL improves over capacity-matched independent adaptation on every task, with positive gains across most directed model combinations. Pairwise comparisons show broad advantages over transfer and mutual-learning methods and different gains from complementarity across collaboration rules. Low-label language and vision results support cross-domain applicability. Task-relevant differences can support mutual enhancement without eliminating disagreement.
  </details>

- **[A Positional Realizability Theory of Length Generalization in Single-Head Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41218)**  `arXiv:2610.41218`  
  _Jiasong Han, Yunkai Chai, Jintao Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Why does length generalization (LG) depend so sharply on the positional encoding (PE)? Relative schemes extrapolate fixed offsets, absolute schemes fix positions, and neither handles growing offsets. We give a realizability theory for a deliberately controlled abstraction, a single positional-only attention head, that derives this well-documented empirical pattern and says precisely where it holds and breaks. The device is a simulation transfer operator $T_L$ that re-evaluates a selected length-$L_0$-realizing positional kernel on the longer grid, with a scalar $\alpha_L \in [0,1]$ read off before any training from $(\Phi, t_n)$ alone. Here $\alpha_L$ serves as the computable witness of that taxonomy's derivation, not as a stronger empirical predictor. Our results are a relative-head deep-row characterization for kernels with a strict unique global maximizer (Theorem 2), an absolute/relative duality that derives the pattern (Proposition 4), a learned-absolute underdetermination result, and a static expressivity bound; a sufficiency theorem (Theorem 1) bridges $\alpha_L$ to LG, and the general deterministic case is a precise open conjecture (correcting a tempting but invalid "outside-colspan" argument). Experiments confirm the structure: in the controlled single-head regime $\alpha_L$ exactly separates the taxonomy's generalizing and non-generalizing (PE, task) cells (2625 trained-model measurements; stable at vocab 64/d=128). Together, these results turn the received PE taxonomy into a positional-kernel transfer theorem, and identify the content and depth mechanisms that escape it. The theory concerns the positional channel alone: tasks whose targets depend on token content, including the standard length-generalization suite such as addition and sorting, fall outside Theorem 2.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Scarcity: When Encoder Adaptation Is Worthwhile](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41213)**  `arXiv:2610.41213`  
  _Zexing Zhang, Jichao Li, Tianyang Lei, Yiyin Zhang, Kewei Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-tailed recognition often learns a shared representation before correcting the classifier for class imbalance. Once the classifier has been rebalanced, however, final accuracy does not reveal whether further encoder adaptation remains worthwhile. Standard imbalance benchmarks obscure this question because they jointly vary the number of distinct examples available to rare classes and the frequency with which those examples shape the representation. We introduce a controlled evaluation framework that separates unique support from representation allocation and estimates the incremental value of encoder updates through matched classifier-only and full-network continuations from a common corrected model. The framework combines stage-specific controls, multiple representation and adaptation objectives, whole-distribution metrics, and hierarchical inference over class assignments, support reconstructions, and optimization runs. Across the controlled study, the same low-support regime moves from one in which classifier-only training is adequate to one in which encoder adaptation yields a practically meaningful benefit solely as representation allocation becomes more imbalanced. The transition persists across classifier corrections, representation objectives, normalization and augmentation controls, and ImageNet-scale evaluation, while preserving frequent-class accuracy. Under the prespecified one-percentage-point operating margin, the two regimes are separated by simultaneous confidence bounds. These results show that benchmark construction can alter a post-correction training decision and that support size alone does not characterize the remaining value of representation adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Role-Aware Server Optimization for Multimodal Federated Learning with Missing Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41193)**  `arXiv:2610.41193`  
  _Liuyin Shi, Wenbo He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal federated learning (MFL) trains a shared model by aggregating client updates without exchanging raw data. Under missing modalities, clients adapt active modality encoders and a shared prediction block (fusion and classifier) to different input subsets. This raises two server-side questions: which clients should contribute to each component, and how should their aggregates be incorporated across rounds? We observe that an absent-modality encoder can still adapt through missing-input handling. Availability- based aggregation can therefore omit its update while retaining jointly learned prediction-block updates. Contributor selection also shapes server momentum, a weighted history of aggregated updates. We observe that prediction-block momentum can improve or reduce performance depending on the encoder update policy. We therefore propose Role-Aware Server Optimization (RASO), which coordinates contributor selection with role-specific server updates. RASO selects contributors through prediction dependence and inclusion in local optimization, retains encoder momentum, and synchronizes the shared prediction block to its current aggregate. Experiments across datasets and fusion architectures demonstrate the effectiveness of RASO under missing modalities.
  </details>

- **[CAP: Learning to Optimize from Sparse Observations of Related Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41173)**  `arXiv:2610.41173`  
  _Lin Fu, Siwei Fu, Dong Ni_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In experiment-driven black-box optimization, each evaluation can consume materials, equipment time, and other resources, limiting the number of experiments available for a new task. Records from completed related optimization campaigns could help, but learning a sequential query policy remains challenging when task-specific experience consists of only a few sparse histories. We propose CAP (Candidate Acquisition Policy), which models shared structure and task-specific variation in fixed historical records to sample interactive practice functions. On these functions, CAP learns a feedback-conditioned query policy aimed at final optimization performance under a fixed evaluation budget. At deployment, its parameters remain fixed while each new observation informs the next query. Across five synthetic task families spanning 6 and 20 dimensions and two engineering simulation benchmarks, BOPTEST and SU2, CAP attains the lowest median final-budget value among the reported methods on four synthetic families and both engineering benchmarks. These results indicate that sparse records from completed related tasks can support adaptive query decisions on new tasks, making more effective use of limited, costly experimental evaluations.
  </details>

- **[TalentRL: Strategy-Guided Reinforcement Learning for Hierarchical Skill Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41143)**  `arXiv:2610.41143`  
  _Huancheng Chen, Xiaodi Sun, Zhaoqiong Huang, Michelle Chang, Jingwen Lu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural-language skills enable large language model (LLM) agents to reuse procedures across tasks, yet most systems retrieve individual skills from a flat SkillBank without explicitly modeling the high-level strategy that governs their selection and use. We introduce TalentRL, a strategy-guided reinforcement learning framework for hierarchical routing over procedural memory. Coarse-grained talents represent recurring strategies for skill use, while fine-grained skills provide executable procedures. For each task, the policy model selects a talent that guides execution and softly prioritizes its associated skills during similarity-based retrieval. However, since task outcomes depend jointly on talent selection, retrieved skill context, and downstream execution, terminal rewards alone cannot isolate the contribution of the routing decision. TalentRL therefore trains talent routing with reward contrasts between rollouts of the same task under matched interventions on the talent-conditioned context, and optimizes downstream execution with group-relative advantages. Across seven open-domain and multi-hop question-answering benchmarks, talent-aware supervised fine-tuning improves average success rate by 7.53%, 6.71%, and 9.21% over the corresponding zero-shot baselines for Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen3-8B, and Llama-3.1-8B, respectively. Reinforcement learning further improves these SFT checkpoints by 7.58% to 9.32%, and TalentRL achieves the highest average success rate among all compared methods on all three backbones. On the agentic benchmarks ALFWorld and WebShop, TalentRL achieves the highest ALFWorld success rate (99.3%) and the highest WebShop score (90.7) among all compared methods.
  </details>

- **[bf16 Rounding Silently Discards Most RL Policy Updates: A Hidden Training-Inference Mismatch and an Unbiased One-Line Fix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41073)**  `arXiv:2610.41073`  
  _Andrew Gao, William Gao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training of language models keeps fp32 master weights in the optimizer and serves rollouts from a bf16 copy produced by round-to-nearest conversion. At the learning rates in common use, most per-step updates are smaller than half a bf16 spacing and the conversion discards them: on Qwen3-1.7B only 0.4-0.5% of served weights change per step, and the policy that generates the training data lags the optimizer by 50-150 steps. Existing mismatch corrections cannot see this lag because they compare two copies downstream of the same rounding. We propose Fair Cast: export the master with an unbiased rounding rule (stochastic rounding or error feedback) and compute the training loss on the served copy. The change is one line at the export call, adds no hyperparameters and costs 22-38 ms per export of 1.7B parameters. Across 25 matched seed-pairs on Qwen3-0.6B to 4B with AdamW and Muon, Fair Cast is ahead on 18, tied on 6 and behind on 1, with a mean gain of 6.1 points; on zebra puzzles it reaches 0.57 +/- 0.16 over four runs where round-to-nearest collapses to 0.33 +/- 0.10. It also makes the saved fp32 checkpoint agree with the served model, and it exposes the implicit lag of round-to-nearest as a regularizer that can be restored explicitly when a task needs it.
  </details>

- **[Before We Adapt: The Zero-Shot Generalization Bottleneck in Continual World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41041)**  `arXiv:2610.41041`  
  _Amir Mesbah, Di Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual reinforcement learning (CRL) requires agents to operate under environmental changes while reusing prior knowledge whenever possible. We argue that model-based reinforcement learning provides a promising framework for this setting because learned world models (WMs) can, in principle, support change detection, knowledge reuse, adaptation, and acquisition of new knowledge. However, such benefits depend heavily on whether the learned model can generalize beyond the contexts in which it was trained. In this work, we propose to formulate this problem with a contextual Markov decision process in which context is decomposed into behavioral and physical components. This view organizes continual WM learning into reuse, adaptation, and acquisition of new knowledge. As a first step, we study direct reuse when behavioral rules remain unchanged. Using Dyna-Q and TD-MPC in controlled FourRooms tasks, we find that zero-shot reuse can degrade substantially under physical-context changes and that this behavior depends strongly on the observation representation used by the WM. Additional ablation studies and latent-space diagnostics suggest that learned representations do not automatically provide the invariances needed for reuse. These results identify representation generalization as an important bottleneck for reliable world-model reuse in CRL.
  </details>

- **[Do Language Models Know What Compelling Scientific Evidence Looks Like?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41015)**  `arXiv:2610.41015`  
  _Conor Igoe, Gabriel Grand, Zane Koch, Albert Bou, Eli Moss, Michaela M. Hinks, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Expert scientists excel at weighing empirical findings from multiple sources that vary in both statistical reliability and epistemic relevance. To what extent are language models, which are increasingly driving autonomous discovery systems, capable of such evidence deliberation? We introduce EDB-Bio, a scientific reasoning benchmark curated from real-world data in three biological domains: drug-perturbation transcriptomics, CRISPR screens, and human genome-wide association studies (GWAS). Each of EDB-Bio's 96k episodes presents a sequence of experimental results from published studies and asks the model to predict the probability that a target hypothesis is true. Out-of-the-box, open-weight and frontier models alike demonstrate systematic underconfidence and attain Brier scores comparable to a trivial base-rate forecaster. Post-training against strictly proper scoring rules significantly improves both calibration and discrimination. This calibration pays off downstream: in a sequential experiment selection task, our Ibis0 model is 2.5 times more efficient than the best frontier model. Throughout, quantifying evidence weights as Bayes factors reveals how models update their beliefs over time, whether they agree with expert biologists on relevance, and where they exhibit inconsistencies and biases. Our work extends calibration training and uncertainty analysis to a realistic scientific forecasting setting with applications to human cancer biology. We release all code, data, and trained models.
  </details>

- **[Towards Understanding Score-Seeking in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41014)**  `arXiv:2610.41014`  
  _Aghyad Deeb, Ryan Greenblatt, Alex Mallen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Production large language models sometimes reward-hack, exploiting flaws in how tasks are scored rather than performing the tasks as intended, sometimes in highly sophisticated and adversarial ways. We investigate the motivations driving this behavior in GPT 5.6 Sol, GPT 5.5, and a checkpoint of OpenAI o3 without safety training ("exp-rl-cap-late") by observing model behavior in agentic environments where the grader is leaked. In our environments, the models appear to be score-seeking: trying to score well according to graders that they believe assess their current actions, even at the expense of violating explicit user instructions. They appear to care about score itself rather than its downstream consequences, violating instructions at approximately the same rate whether the score is indicated to be used for training, for evaluation, or not at all. We observe evidence that the more recent models care to some extent about scores that do not assess their own current actions. GPT 5.6 Sol and GPT 5.5 help other agents 24.6 percentage points more often on average when the other agent stands to gain a score of 1 rather than 0 from being helped. However, when grading or monitoring another trajectory, we find models never collude by distorting its score.
  </details>

- **[MOSAIC: Addressing Disproportionate Task-Modality Dependencies via Modality-Partitioned Gradient Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41004)**  `arXiv:2610.41004`  
  _Aryan Chowdhury, Yizheng Sun, Thomas Carroll, Riza Batista-Navarro_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In multi-task settings, combining multiple modalities allows task representations to draw on signals that a single modality alone would not capture. However, individual tasks may depend disproportionately on particular modalities, a structure that conventional fusion-based architectures do not model explicitly, instead enforcing a unified representation space. Consequently, a task's gradients can affect parameters tied to modalities it barely relies on, leaving features that compromise between competing demands. Although prior work has explored task-weighting and loss-balancing strategies, their effectiveness is limited by the absence of explicit modality separation, preventing models from optimising each modality according to its task-specific importance. To address these limitations, we propose MOSAIC (MOdality-Separated Architecture with Inter-modal Cross-attention), which preserves unimodal representations while enabling controlled interaction between them, and uses modality-specific losses to route gradients to their corresponding partitions. On the Corpus of Everyday Japanese Conversation (CEJC), MOSAIC improves average macro-F1 by 7.4 points across four tasks with a 72% reduction in backbone parameters relative to a fusion-based baseline. Our results demonstrate that modality partitioning yields consistent gains across tasks while requiring far fewer backbone parameters than a fusion-based design.
  </details>

- **[When Can Unlabeled Data Pools Replace Membership Queries?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41001)**  `arXiv:2610.41001`  
  _Dravyansh Sharma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Membership queries let a learner label arbitrary points and give near-optimal $\tilde O(n\log(1/\eps))$ label complexity for halfspaces under every marginal. A pool learner may label only sampled points. We ask when unlabeled data can replace membership queries. For homogeneous halfspaces the answer depends only on the angular law of $X/\|X\|$. If this law assigns zero mass to every hyperplane through the origin and positive mass to every nonempty open set of directions together with its reflection, a pool learner uses $O(n\log^3n\log(n/(\eps\delta)))$ labels, uniformly over all such marginals and targets, without density, moment, concavity or isotropy assumptions. The same label bound holds for non-homogeneous halfspaces under every marginal with full support and no mass on affine hyperplanes, including Gaussians. Dropping either angular assumption admits $\Omega(1/\eps)$ pool versus $O(\log(1/\eps))$ membership-query separations, even with unlimited unlabeled data. The price is paid in unlabeled data: in dimension $n\ge3$, for every unlabeled budget fixed in advance, some marginal satisfying both assumptions forces $\Omega(1/\eps)$ labels. For isotropic $s$-concave marginals in dimension $n\ge3$, unlimited pools keep logarithmic label rates for every finite $s$. With an unlabeled budget fixed in advance, class-uniform polylogarithmic rates hold exactly for $s>-1/(n+1)$, or $s>-1/n$ under central symmetry, and in the general class the optimal rate between these thresholds is $\tilde\Theta(\eps^{-(n-2)/n})$. This extends the range $s\ge-1/(2n+3)$ of previous sample-based guarantees, in the realizable setting.
  </details>

- **[Steering Vectors as a Training Signal in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40998)**  `arXiv:2610.40998`  
  _Tiejin Chen, Maunil Vyas, Huaiyuan Yao, Hua Wei_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Steering vectors (SVs) are residual-stream directions that shift the behavior of LLMs when added to hidden states at inference time. While prior work has mainly used SVs as post-hoc controls, we ask whether the same directions can also guide parameter updates during post-training. We study this question across supervised learning, including SFT and distillation, and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Our framework considers two intervention regimes: a hidden-state hook during teacher-forced supervised training, and an advantage-repair mechanism on wrong rollouts in GRPO. For each regime, we analyze three design axes, which are the steering direction, its placement, and the operation used to combine it with training. In supervised settings, SV injection improves over plain SFT and distillation, with gains scaling with the off-policy gap and reaching up to a $12\%$ improvement when distilling from thinking-mode teachers. These gains hold across three random seeds. In RL settings, applying a correctness-contrast direction as advantage repair on wrong rollouts, including otherwise zero-gradient all-wrong groups, recovers a learning signal that transfers to held-out math benchmarks and acts as a composable auxiliary signal that adds to existing zero-variance remedies such as entropy-aware advantage shaping. We further identify three failure boundaries. In detail, code generation can break the direction, cross-family layer transfer can break the placement, and inference-time application on an SV-trained checkpoint can break the intervention regime. Together, these results establish steering vectors as a conditional but effective training signal for LLM post-training. Our code is available here.
  </details>

- **[Monte Carlo Random Walk Kernel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40956)**  `arXiv:2610.40956`  
  _Arslan Iamaletdinov, Elfat Sabitov, Konstantin Sorokin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce the Monte Carlo Random Walk Kernel (MCRWK), an estimator whose per-pair cost is independent of graph size and derived directly from the random walk kernel (RWK) expansion. By normalizing the kernel coefficients, MCRWK samples a shared walk length and estimates its contribution using random walks on the input graphs. This gives a simple way to approximate RWK without constructing the product graph. For a fixed expected walk length and efficient neighbor sampling, each pairwise estimate takes $O(m)$ time for $m$ Monte Carlo samples, allowing the sample budget to control the trade-off between runtime and accuracy. We evaluate MCRWK against Graph Voyagers and classical RWK methods on synthetic and real graph datasets. In the reported experiments on large synthetic graphs, MCRWK is faster than Graph Voyagers and, at matched runtime, more accurate. It also has the lowest Gram-matrix runtime on the five real datasets studied. Code and experiments are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[CAMEL: Cross-Architecture Masked Embedding Learning for Ultra-Lightweight Retinal Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40954)**  `arXiv:2610.40954`  
  _Andre Guarnier De Mitri, Giovani Decico Lucafo, Miller Matheus Lima Anacleto Rocha, Diego Furtado Silva_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent progress in deep learning has substantially advanced automated grading of ophthalmologic pathologies, supported by large annotated datasets and improved representation learning. However, a significant gap remains between state-of-the-art performance and clinical scalability. In particular, foundation models have reshaped image-based medical screening and diagnosis. These large-scale architectures trained on extensive datasets learn rich, generalizable feature representations that can be adapted to a wide range of downstream tasks. In ophthalmology, RETFound, one of the most relevant foundation models for retinal images, performs strongly across classification, segmentation, and risk prediction tasks. Despite their effectiveness, foundation models are often computationally prohibitive, hindering their deployment in real-world clinical environments where hardware constraints and latency are critical. To bridge this gap, we introduce CAMEL (Cross-Architecture Masked Embedding Learning), a novel cross-architecture distillation framework that uses an asymmetric masking strategy during embedding learning to effectively transfer knowledge from massive teacher foundation models to compact, lightweight student models. Our experimental evaluation across four datasets shows that CAMEL preserves accuracy while drastically reducing computational complexity, enabling lightweight yet highly accurate models suitable for resource-limited clinical settings.
  </details>

- **[On the Origin and Evolution of Training-Inference Mismatch in LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40945)**  `arXiv:2610.40945`  
  _Yu Li, Yufa Zhou, Tian Lan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy reinforcement learning for large language models assumes that training responses are sampled from the current policy. In practice, an inference engine samples the responses and a separate trainer computes the gradients, and the \textbf{training-inference mismatch} between the two makes the training data off-policy. The mismatch has two sources, the numerics of the engine and the stale weights of asynchronous sampling, and a correction that stabilizes training under one source can fail under the other. \textit{We ask which property of a mismatch source decides the correction it needs.} Evaluating the trainer at the sampler's weights splits each sampled-token error exactly into a \textit{numerical discrepancy} of the engine and a \textit{policy lag} of the trainer, and we follow both through training. The numerical discrepancy stays approximately constant, so it biases the updates by a nearly fixed amount, and centering the score at each prefix removes its leading bias. The policy lag is produced by the trainer's own updates: the bias it leaves enters the next update, which enlarges the next lag and closes a feedback loop. Importance weighting cuts this loop only while the lag ratio stays concentrated, and on Qwen3-1.7B it fails at the step where the effective sample size of the ratio falls below one half. From this account we derive the \textbf{discrepancy-lag correction} (DLC), which centers the numerical discrepancy, clips the lag ratio against the sampler's weights and resynchronizes the sampler when this effective sample size falls below one half. Under a stale sampler, DLC keeps the KL divergence from on-policy training within twice the KL between two independent on-policy trainings on Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen3-1.7B, and its resynchronization prevents collapses that the per-token corrections leave and that a KL measured on the sampler's prefixes does not detect.
  </details>

- **[A Theoretical Framework for Understanding and Building "Any-task inference" Capable Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40934)**  `arXiv:2610.40934`  
  _Matthew McDermott, Florent Pollet, Payal Chandak, Gregory Kondas, Liat Friedman Antwarg, Noa Dagan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we provide a formal framework and mathematical definition for the subset of foundation models focused on enabling the property of "any-task inference" (ATI) (similar to zero-shot inference), rather than those designed for pre-training fine-tuning. To achieve this mathematical definition, we formalize the notion of a "space of tasks of interest," which we include in the problem domain, then provide an interface-based formal definition for ATI foundation models as those that map samples of data $x$ and tasks $t$ to predictions over the space of labels directly. While simple, this definition allows us to show that all ATI foundation models can be decomposed through a "universal task reduction" lens into encoder, model, and decoder aspects. We can leverage this decomposition to also decompose foundation model error into terms corresponding to different aspects of this reduction, stratified into a collection of "design terms," which are theoretical properties that can inform one of whether a particular proposed ATI foundation model can achieve optimality or not due to the structure of the proposed task reduction, and "realized model excess" terms which capture interactions between learned and programmatic components of foundation model systems. Lastly, we can use this perspective to offer two "prototypical" recipes for realizing ATI foundation models in diverse domains, which we term generative and discriminative variants of ATI foundation models. This exercise is grounded throughout by 5 real-world examples of foundation models in different settings, varying greatly in how these capabilities are realized, showing the applicability of this definition to real-world settings.
  </details>

- **[No Channels Left Behind: Understanding Diffusion Acceleration through Residual Gates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40914)**  `arXiv:2610.40914`  
  _Junha Park, Jaemyung Yu, Jaehui Hwang, Jong-Seok Lee, Dongyoon Han_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training acceleration methods for diffusion transformers, such as REPA and dispersive regularization, are commonly explained by additional supervision, spatial-structure priors, or improved feature geometry. We revisit this view and uncover a striking internal change: the AdaLN-Zero residual gate $\alpha$, which directly controls how conditioned updates enter the residual stream, is substantially reorganized by these methods. This observation motivates our hypothesis that acceleration arises, at least in part, from reshaping the residual gating pathway rather than solely from the feature structures explicitly imposed by auxiliary objectives. Supporting this view, accelerated models are markedly more sensitive to perturbations of $\alpha$, and their gate geometry reflects semantic relationships among classes. We further introduce the normalized participation ratio (nPR) to characterize the channel-wise organization of the gates and find that, across REPA teachers, projector architectures, and radial-objective targets, broader gate participation accompanies better generation quality. Guided by this insight, we propose a simple teacher-free radial objective that promotes broader gate participation through feature-norm regularization, without pairwise feature comparisons. On ImageNet $256\times256$ with SiT-B/2, our method improves FID over dispersive loss while increasing mean gate participation over timesteps and classes, further supporting the link between gate organization and training acceleration. Our results identify residual-gate reorganization as a measurable axis of diffusion training acceleration and suggest a simple direction for further acceleration.
  </details>

- **[TSFLab: A Self-Improving Agent-Agnostic Workbench for Time Series Forecasting Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40901)**  `arXiv:2610.40901`  
  _ChengAo Shen, Yanjun Zhao, Haoxin Liu, Bonnie Chen, Miles Ma, Dongjin Song, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As publications and products on time series forecasting (TSF) proliferate, researchers face a growing burden of analyzing existing work. First, not all publications release code. For instance, only about 20% of arXiv TSF papers have a code link in the surveyed period. Second, released code also requires manual maintenance. Thus, TSF researchers must constantly adapt or implement existing works. However, independent implementations can differ in experimental settings and are difficult to maintain. We therefore propose to use AI agents for benchmarking TSF research and even supporting autoresearch, e.g., developing new TSF models. Yet directly applying existing coding agents, such as Claude Code and Codex, is suboptimal: prohibitive costs, limited scalability to large research projects (e.g., benchmarking), and lack of self-maintenance. To address these challenges, we propose TSFLab, a self-improving agent-agnostic "workbench" that equips compatible coding agents with shared resources and tools for low-cost TSF research and continuous maintenance of research resources. Our experiments cover reproduction, benchmarking, and new method development. In reproduction and benchmarking, TSFLab improves task completion by 20-163% and cuts fresh tokens per completed task by 11-66% across baselines. A pilot study of agent-driven method development also shows improved forecasting accuracy via TSFLab, with 32.8-73.6% fewer fresh tokens and 9.2-68.8% less development time than the other settings.
  </details>

- **[TensorSSM: Memorizing Spatial Position in Dynamic State Space for Tensor Core Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40871)**  `arXiv:2610.40871`  
  _Sheng Zhang, Xinyi Wang, Maolin Wang, Xiangyu Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-dimensional tensors are ubiquitous in real-world applications but lead to massive storage overhead. Functional tensor decomposition addresses this by employing neural networks to map element coordinates to tensor cores, significantly reducing the parameters for data compression. However, existing functional methods typically flatten high-order tensors into 1D sequences. This folding operation causes highly overlapping local coordinate sequences and struggles to capture complex cross-mode spatial dependencies, severely limiting their expressiveness on diverse datasets. To tackle these challenges, we propose TensorSSM, a novel Implicit Neural Representation (INR) for functional decomposition method that memorizes spatial positions within a dynamic State Space Model (SSM). Specifically, TensorSSM first decomposes mode-wise coordinates into sequences spit by K-ary rule, encoding spatial tokens from coarse to fine, which effectively captures spatial variations. Furthermore, we extend the SSM's state space to explicitly map cross-mode spatial dependencies, allowing inter-mode interactions to enhance the updates of each mode. By integrating cross-mode information from previous hidden states into the current state transitions, TensorSSM effectively expands tensor representation along two dimensions: the sequence dimension for coarse-to-fine spatial tokens, and the state dimension for cross-mode memorization. This design effectively integrates spatial information into the state space and the tensor core, substantially advancing the state adaptability and enhancing the expressiveness of the data compression. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TensorSSM achieves significantly high reconstruction accuracy and storage efficiency, exhibiting robust expressiveness across diverse tensor datasets without relying on strict spatial assumptions.
  </details>

- **[FARO: Function-Space Adaptive Return-to-Risk Optimization via Cross-Block Equal-Risk-Contribution Budget Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40858)**  `arXiv:2610.40858`  
  _Xiaojun Wu, Cehao Yang, Xuhui Jiang, Chengjin Xu, Jia Li, Jian Guo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training a large language model is compute-bound, and current recipes divide the work between optimizers by hand: orthogonalized momentum for attention and MLP matrices, Adam-style updates for the rest. This division rests on human priors about which layer type deserves which geometry, and it fixes a single global update budget that every parameter block shares. We introduce FARO, a function-space adaptive optimizer that makes the partition of parameters into blocks the unit of decision. FARO gives each block a geometry by its realized counterfactual return, then freezes the routes and reallocates the frozen per-block budget by cross-block equal risk contribution (ERC), the risk-parity principle from portfolio theory. On a GPT-style model trained on FineWeb, the framework gives two results. First, its FARO-Adaptive arm reaches 3.2780 nats at the full schedule, matching NorMuonAndAdam (3.2794), the best hand-designed recipe: it recovers the human prior without hand-setting per-layer geometry. Second, FARO-ERC improves on that arm: it lowers the clean-data loss at every one of 6 seeds, by 0.0136 nats on average, a modest but robust gain. Its sign test, paired test, and confidence interval all agree, and it exceeds the measured run-to-run noise at no added time or memory. It works by trimming the riskiest blocks rather than expanding the safest, and it holds up under a corrupted stream, still lowering the mean loss and winning the seed majority. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Unsupervised Feature Selection Using Local Intrinsic Dimensionality -- Extended Version](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40736)**  `arXiv:2610.40736`  
  _Oussama Chelly, Michael E. Houle, Ken-ichi Kawarabayashi, Muhammad Rajabinasab, Arthur Zimek_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High dimensionality degrades both the efficiency and the effectiveness of learning algorithms, and in many applications no class labels are available to guide dimensionality reduction. This paper proposes LIDFS, an unsupervised feature selection method based on Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (LID). The method builds on the formal equivalence between LID and the indiscriminability of distance measures. The data matrix is transposed so that each feature is treated as a point, and the LID of each feature is estimated by maximum likelihood from the distances to its $k$ nearest neighboring features. Features with lower LID exhibit greater local discriminability and receive higher importance scores. A refinement step then retains one representative per feature neighborhood and demotes locally redundant features. The result is a complete ranking from which any number of features can be selected without rerunning the procedure. LIDFS does not use any information about labels or the number of classes, and runs in $\mathcal{O}(d^2 n)$ time for $n$ instances and $d$ features. LIDFS is compared with classic and state-of-the-art methods and a random baseline on 23 high-dimensional datasets, the classic benchmark collection. The evaluation uses classification accuracy and AUC with five downstream classifiers, as well as clustering accuracy and normalized mutual information, over both full and aggressive selection ranges. LIDFS ranks first in the supervised evaluations and first or tied second in the clustering-based evaluations. It belongs to the top statistical group together with methods that require the number of classes, and it runs faster than its closest competitor. Several recent methods, by contrast, fail to outperform the random baseline. A sensitivity analysis shows that small neighborhood sizes yield the best results.
  </details>

- **[Don't Just Impute, Detect: Task-Aware Diffusion Pipelines for Edge Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40732)**  `arXiv:2610.40732`  
  _Jeongsu Park, Taehyeon Yun, Geonwoo Kim, Yuji Lim, Moohong Min_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial Internet of Things (IIoT) edge pipelines routinely lose contiguous blocks of multi-sensor data to network dropouts and hardware faults. The standard remedy is a disjointed "impute-then-detect" pipeline whose imputer is trained only for Mean Squared Error (MSE). Such an imputer fills a missing block with the most probable normal pattern: a partially observed anomaly is smoothed back toward normality, and boundary artifacts on normal data raise false alarms. We call this imputation bias and propose the Task-Aware Diffusion Refiner (TADR), a conditional diffusion refiner trained against the loss landscape of a frozen downstream detector. Because back-propagating through the sampling trajectory is intractable, the task-aware terms act on the one-step clean-window estimate that the noise prediction already yields, so training costs no more than plain diffusion training. Under strict Point-to-Point evaluation over five seeds with paired significance tests, TADR raises F1 by $0.338$ over the unrefined detector on SMD ($p = 0.0015$), and the anomaly enhancement objective alone contributes $0.463$ in ablation. We also characterise when the method helps: the gain tracks the continuous, mutually redundant channels available and the anomaly evidence the outage removes, and it is absent on telemetry datasets dominated by one-hot command channels and on PSM. At ten sampling steps TADR runs in $41.4$ ms per window on a single CPU core, sustaining about $24$ concurrent 1 Hz streams from a $4.03$ MB model. Code and per-seed results are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[NECA: Neighbor Event Curve Augmentation for Multi-Horizon ICU Deterioration Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40420)**  `arXiv:2610.40420`  
  _Chenyang Wang, Theresa Jiawei Tang, Xinran Liu, Xinyu Lu, Xulei Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Early prediction of ICU organ dysfunction events supports timely clinical monitoring and intervention, but estimating short-term organ-specific risk from early ICU records remains challenging due to irregular measurements, heterogeneous clinical variables, and the need to predict risk across multiple future horizons.
