This introduces verifiable dense rewards into the reasoning process, facilitating more effective credit assignment in RL optimization. We then optimize the thinking and summary policies in a decoupled manner to maximize the cumulative reward. Additionally, we introduce a novel variance reduction technique, Budget Relative Policy Optimization (BRPO), to enhance the robustness and efficiency of the learning process when reinforcing the thinking policy. Empirical results in mathematical reasoning tasks demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms GRPO across all thinking budgets under various prior distributions, enhancing both training and token efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Slim attention: cut your context memory in half without loss -- K-cache is all you need for MHA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.05840)**  `arXiv:2503.05840`  
  _Nils Graef, Kayvan Zahiri, Andrew Wasielewski_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Slim attention shrinks the context memory size by 2x for transformer models with MHA (multi-head attention), which can speed up inference by up to 2x for large context windows.Slim attention is an exact, mathematically identical implementation of the standard attention mechanism and therefore doesn't compromise model accuracy. In other words, slim attention losslessly compresses the context memory by a factor of 2.For encoder-decoder transformers, the context memory size can be reduced even further: For the Whisper models for example, slim attention reduces the context memory by 8x, which can speed up token generation by 5x for batch size 64 for example.And for the T5-11B model for example, the memory can be reduced by 32x because its MHA projection dimension is larger than the embedding dimension.Seethis https URLfor code and more transformer tricks, andthis https URLfor this paper's YouTube video.
  </details>

- **[Gaussian Process Upper Confidence Bound Achieves Nearly Optimal Regret in Noise-Free Gaussian Process Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19006)**  `arXiv:2502.19006`  
  _Shogo Iwazaki_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study the noise-free Gaussian Process (GP) bandits problem, in which the learner seeks to minimize regret through noise-free observations of the black-box objective function lying on the known reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS). Gaussian process upper confidence bound (GP-UCB) is the well-known GP-bandits algorithm whose query points are adaptively chosen based on the GP-based upper confidence bound score. Although several existing works have reported the practical success of GP-UCB, the current theoretical results indicate its suboptimal performance. However, GP-UCB tends to perform well empirically compared with other nearly optimal noise-free algorithms that rely on a non-adaptive sampling scheme of query points. This paper resolves this gap between theoretical and empirical performance by showing the nearly optimal regret upper bound of noise-free GP-UCB. Specifically, our analysis shows the first constant cumulative regret in the noise-free settings for the squared exponential kernel and Matérn kernel with some degree of smoothness.
  </details>

- **[Bilevel ZOFO: Efficient LLM Fine-Tuning and Meta-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.03604)**  `arXiv:2502.03604`  
  _Reza Shirkavand, Peiran Yu, Qi He, Heng Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning pre-trained Large Language Models (LLMs) for downstream tasks using First-Order (FO) optimizers presents significant computational challenges. Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) methods address these by freezing most model parameters and training only a small subset. However, PEFT often underperforms compared to full fine-tuning when high task-specific accuracy is required. Zeroth-Order (ZO) methods fine-tune the entire pre-trained model without back-propagation, estimating gradients through forward passes only. While memory-efficient, ZO methods suffer from slow convergence and high sensitivity to prompt selection. We bridge these two worlds with Bilevel-ZOFO, a bilevel optimization method that couples fast, local FO-PEFT adaptation at the inner level with stable, memory-efficient ZO updates of the full backbone at the outer level. The FO-PEFT inner loop performs fast, low-memory local adaptation that reduces the variance of ZO estimates and stabilizes the search, guiding the outer ZO updates of the full backbone and reducing prompt sensitivity. In the mean time, the outer ZO provides better generalization ability for PEFT. We provide theoretical convergence guarantees and empirically demonstrate that Bilevel-ZOFO significantly outperforms existing ZO and FO-PEFT methods, achieving 2-4 times faster training while maintaining similar memory efficiency. Additionally, we show by updating the backbone with ZO and adapting only a tiny FO-PEFT block per task, Bilevel-ZOFO combines full-model capacity with few-shot efficiency, making it a very efficient meta-learning algorithm that quickly adapts to new tasks.
  </details>

- **[When Disclosure Is Compulsory: Evaluating Ride-Hailing Rebalancing Policies with Self-Interested LLM Driver Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41132)**  `arXiv:2610.41132`  
  _Xinyu Wang, Andres Fielbaum_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The EU Platform Work Directive, to be transposed by December 2026, requires ride-hailing platforms to state in plain language the main parameters of their automated decisions and how a worker's behaviour affects them. Such rules are exactly what a large language model (LLM) can read, so drivers will be able to let one decide for them. We compare two main families of rebalancing policy once their rules are public: a reactive policy (R) that sends idle drivers to cover requests just lost, and an anticipatory policy (A) that plans moves on a demand forecast. Each driver is an LLM agent maximizing its own net income, given no rules (N) or the rules at the directive's level of detail (L). We test two LLMs, Claude Haiku 5.5 and Jev, which understand the rules equally well, in a three-zone model and in Manhattan. With the rules disclosed, drivers follow the platform more closely: in Manhattan, the share of relocation offers they decline falls from about four in five to one in four under both policies. Yet the effects differ by policy. For the platform, disclosure costs R 3.7% of its profit in Manhattan, as R now pays for moves drivers used to make for free, while A's profit is unchanged; the two policies, which tie on profit under full compliance, end 6.1% apart. For drivers, disclosure raises income in the small model under both policies and LLMs; for users, it raises the service rate there by 10 to 18 percentage points. In Manhattan, self-interested drivers also make A a win-win-win against full compliance, raising driver income, platform profit and service rate by 10 to 12% each. Rebalancing policies that perform equally well under full compliance can thus show opposite results under mandatory disclosure. The study offers a framework to evaluate such policies under the new requirement and is, to our knowledge, the first to do so with LLM agents.
  </details>

- **[Anchor and Perturb: Lazy Agent Remediation by Exploration Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27365)**  `arXiv:2609.27365`  
  _Chengxi Zhong, Yongzhe Chang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anchor and Perturb (AnP) is a lightweight framework that resolves multi-agent coordination failures by decoupling exploratory variance injection from recurrent manifold stability. Existing remediation strategies predominantly alter mixing network architectures or enforce simultaneous exploration across the collective, which inevitably precipitates severe temporal-difference penalties in non-monotonic reward spaces. Specifically, AnP isolates underperforming lazy agents and injects an asymmetric exploratory pulse into targeted coordinates whilst anchoring converged teammates to nominal greedy exploitation. Empirical telemetry benchmarks demonstrate that AnP successfully rescues collapsed joint policies (recovering from a 5% evaluation win rate nadir back to 85%) and facilitates escape from suboptimal coordination plateaus, sustaining peak win rates of 90% without requiring structural network modifications.
  </details>

- **[QueenBee: A Recursively Self-Improving Harness for Communication Topology Design in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27492)**  `arXiv:2606.27492`  
  _Congjia Tian, Yuhang Yao, Jiaming Cui_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based multi-agent systems show great potential for complex tasks through role specialization and collaborative reasoning. However, existing automated workflow design methods primarily focus on one-off search and candidate optimization, lacking sustained accumulation and reuse of structural design experience to meet evolving coordination demands. We propose QueenBee, an experience-guided framework for multi-agent workflow evolution. QueenBee represents workflows as executable programs that generate temporal communication DAGs and maintains Structural Feedback and Transition Bank (SFT-Bank), a persistent structural memory of execution diagnoses, design hypotheses and validation outcomes, and historical programs. This accumulated experience guides subsequent workflow generation, validation, and optimization, enabling the co-evolution of collaboration programs and design experience. Experiments on Silo-Bench show that QueenBee outperforms fixed communication baselines across team sizes and achieves the highest mean task score among recent automated multi-agent workflow optimization methods. Ablation studies further show that SFT-Bank components provide complementary performance gains, while evolution dynamics reveal a consistently improving average trend in the quality of the best collaboration structures found across independent runs as structural experience accumulates.
  </details>

- **[Events as Triggers for Behavioral Diversity in Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12388)**  `arXiv:2605.12388`  
  _Hannes Büchi, Manon Flageat, Eduardo Sebastián, Amanda Prorok_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective multi-agent cooperation requires agents to adopt diverse behaviors as task conditions evolve-and to do so at the right moment. Yet, current Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) frameworks that facilitate this diversity are still limited by the fact that they bind fixed behaviors to fixed agent identities. Consequently, they are ill-equipped for tasks where agents need to take on different roles at very specific moments in time. We argue that, to define these behavioral transitions, the missing ingredient is $\textbf{events}$. Events are changes in the state of the system that induce qualitative changes in the task. Based on this view, we introduce a framework that decouples agent identity from behavior, capturing a continuous manifold from which agents instantiate their behaviors in response to events. This framework is based on two elements. First, to build an expressive behavior manifold, we introduce Neural Manifold Diversity (NMD), a formal distance metric that remains well-defined when behaviors are transient and agent-agnostic. Second, we use an event-based hypernetwork that generates Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) modules over a shared team policy, enabling on-the-fly agent-policy reconfiguration in response to events. We prove that this construction ensures that diversity does not interfere with reward maximization by design. Empirical results demonstrate that our framework outperforms established baselines across benchmarks while exhibiting zero-shot generalization, and being the only method that solves tasks requiring sequential behavior reassignment.
  </details>

- **[A Cross-Layered Multi-Drone Coordination for Medical Supply Delivery during Disaster Response Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09342)**  `arXiv:2605.09342`  
  _Aneesh Calyam, Subrahmanya Chandra Bhamidipati, Zack Murry, Sharan Srinivas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous drone fleets have potential in medical supply delivery during disaster incident response. However, coordinating multiple drones in such settings introduces compounding challenges: dynamic hazards such as wind, obstacles, and intermittent connectivity, constrained energy budgets, and the need to serve patients fairly under strict deadlines and triage-based priority. We present CEDA, a Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution (CTDE) Double Deep Q-Network algorithm augmented with a QMIX-style monotonic mixing network for cooperative multi-drone medical delivery, designed to jointly optimize triage-aware routing, multi-agent coordination, and energy-efficient navigation under dynamic uncertainty. CEDA introduces a Priority-Preserving Fair Scheduling strategy, in which a structured reward function encodes triage priority weights, outcome-level fairness and priority-ordering rewards, and complementary shaping mechanisms that discourage systematic starvation. We evaluate CEDA in a simulated grid environment with five drones serving up to fifty patients, featuring dynamic hazard zones, stochastic action failures, and dynamically spawning patients across three triage levels, and in PX4 software-in-the-loop (SITL) validation using five X500 quadrotors controlled via MAVSDK. Simulation results show that CEDA delivers over 95\% of spawned patients, reduces obstacle collisions by over 95\% across training, and achieves a weighted triage efficiency exceeding 0.90 alongside a Jain's Fairness Index of approximately 1.0 across triage classes. CEDA preserves clinical priority ordering, with Critical patients receiving the fastest response times, while achieving low and comparable mortality across all triage classes. PX4 SITL validation further shows that the learned policy retains its triage-aware behavior under continuous simulated flight and delayed-action conditions.
  </details>

- **[Distributed scalable coupled policy algorithm for networked multi-agent reinforcement learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.05447)**  `arXiv:2512.05447`  
  _Pengcheng Dai, Dongming Wang, Wenwu Yu, Wei Ren_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper studies networked multi-agent reinforcement learning (NMARL) with interdependent rewards and coupled policies. In this setting, each agent's reward depends on its own state-action pair as well as those of its direct neighbors, and each agent's policy is parameterized by its local parameters together with those of its $\kappa_{p}$-hop neighbors, with $\kappa_{p}\geq 1$ denoting the coupled radius. To address the challenge of interdependent policies in collaborative optimization, we introduce a novel concept termed the ``neighbors' averaged $Q$-function'' and derive a new expression for the coupled-policy gradient. Based on these theoretical foundations, we develop a distributed scalable coupled policy (DSCP) algorithm, where each agent relies only on the state-action pairs of its $\kappa_{p}$-hop neighbors and the rewards of its $(\kappa_{p}+1)$-hop neighbors. Specifically, in the DSCP algorithm, we employ a geometric 2-horizon sampling method that does not require storing a full $Q$-table to obtain an unbiased estimate of the surrogate coupled-policy gradient under the executed policy. Moreover, each agent interacts exclusively with its direct neighbors to obtain accurate policy parameters, while maintaining local estimates of other agents' parameters to execute its local policy and collect samples for optimization. These estimates and policy parameters are updated in a distributed manner through local information exchange among direct neighbors, enabling coordinated policy updates across the network. We prove that the gradient norm of the objective function along the joint policy sequence produced by the proposed algorithm converges to zero almost surely. Finally, the effectiveness of the DSCP algorithm is demonstrated through simulations in a robot path planning environment, showing clear improvement over the compared algorithms in the tested setting.
  </details>

### Robotics 🤖

<details open><summary>Click to Collapse</summary>

- **[Hierarchical World Models with Implicit Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42146)**  `arXiv:2610.42146`  
  _Gaoyue Zhou, Yvonne Wu, Zichen Jeff Cui, Nicolas Ballas, Mido Assran, Lerrel Pinto, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Planning with world models enables test-time adaptation for embodied agents, offering superior generalization over direct policy learning. However, long-horizon tasks remain hindered by cumulative errors and search complexity. Existing hierarchical methods often use rigid macro-actions or latent skills, leading to brittle representations and limited fine-grained control. We introduce ID-WM (Implicit Dynamics World Models), a hierarchical world model that decouples state-space reachability from local control. Our framework utilizes a high-level model to sample feasible transitions across the state manifold and a low-level world model as an inverse dynamics solver to ground these transitions into precise actions. This allows the planner to identify reachable subgoals without being constrained by low-level execution details. By prioritizing local physical feasibility over global behavioral patterns, ID-WM synthesizes novel long-horizon plans from short segments, transcending the specific behavioral modes seen during training. We demonstrate its effectiveness across three navigation and manipulation suites, achieving a 38.5% improvement over state-of-the-art policy and diffusion-based planners. Crucially, by leveraging this hierarchical structure to mitigate compounding errors, our approach yields a 135% performance gain over single-level world model MPC, particularly in complex, unseen configurations for long-horizon tasks. Videos can be viewed atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[RoboArchitect: Automating the Design of Robotic Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42141)**  `arXiv:2610.42141`  
  _Shengguang Wu, Suning Huang, Cherie Ho, Ria Doshi, Hao Zhu, Serena Yeung-Levy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work shows that a frozen robot policy performs far better inside an agent harness, where control code decides when to query the policy, consult vision tools, or command the arm directly. Yet today these harnesses are built by hand and redesigned for each new robot or benchmark. We show that harness design can be automated, offering a strong alternative to finetuning on a novel setup. In RoboArchitect, a coding model observes a robot's rollouts, diagnoses failures, and iteratively revises the harness around the frozen policy. The harness evolves on a set of episodes for its optimization and is evaluated on held-out scenes and tasks. Without updating any model weights, RoboArchitect raises average success from 9.7% to 43.3% on unseen scenes (4.5x) and from 12.7% to 40.2% on unseen tasks (3.2x), across multiple benchmarks and policies. The gains are largest where the benchmark is far from the policy's training data: wrapping a general-purpose policy, the evolved harness yields a specialist that often outperforms even the finetuned version. Intuitive control strategies are automatically designed, such as lifting a jammed arm clear or reaching in horizontally when an obstacle blocks the way from above. The harness also evolves its own persistent memory, carrying learned experience, such as effective grasp depths, into later decisions. These results suggest that substantial headroom in robotic manipulation lies in the agent harness, and its design can be effectively automated and optimized without weight updates.
  </details>

- **[ROOF: Learning Humanoid Fall Recovery under Overhead Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42097)**  `arXiv:2610.42097`  
  _Yidan Lu, Jiayuan Yu, Zongrui Cai, Jingwen Sun, Jinrui Han, Jing Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A fallen humanoid beneath an overhead obstacle faces a coupled recovery problem. It must create clearance while organizing support for a stable rise, since attempting to stand before making room can further increase obstruction. We introduce a reinforcement learning method with contact-supported geometric escape guidance, which rewards incremental reduction of robot-obstacle overlap without prescribing an escape path. To achieve this, a learned motion prior, together with posture-dependent support and joint-stall costs, is proposed to shape a single policy that learns when to clear the obstacle, how to establish support, and when to complete the rise, all using proprioception alone. In simulation, it recovers in 88.5% of trials beneath plates that can only be lifted, compared with 18.9% without geometric guidance, and in 98.6% of trials beneath free plates with randomized size, mass and placement. On a Unitree G1, the deployed policy recovered in 95 of 150 attempts across seven real-world obstacle scenes, including furniture and multi-object arrangements absent from training. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[REA: A Routed Embodied Agent Runtime for Asynchronous Brain-Cerebellum Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42053)**  `arXiv:2610.42053`  
  _Pengfei Zhou, Yixing Ma, Zhengfeiyang Zhang, Haoyuan Chen, Zhenglin Wan, Hexin Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation models are becoming remarkably reliable at high-level reasoning, yet they remain too slow and costly to serve as fine-grained controllers. Conversely, fast policies like VLAs and whole-body controllers offer more efficient reflexes, but struggle to generalize. While pairing a deliberative brain (foundation models) with a reactive cerebellum (fast policies) seems natural for embodied systems, two obstacles remain: methods are built and scored on incompatible stacks, and their asynchronous coordination is difficult. For example, an agent that calls the VLA in the agentic loop can stall the fast execution, while a VLA that calls the agent on errors tends to re-trigger fallbacks repeatedly or completely miss silent failures. To address this, we propose REA (Routed Embodied Agent), a unified runtime where a deliberative System 2, an embodied router System 1 and reactive System 0 policies cooperate under a single golden rule - no running policy waits on the brain. Instead of querying the brain at each failure, REA employs a route memory that stores each policy's measured success ahead of time, a boundary-triggered judge gate that consults the brain on policy execution, and a shared-budget escalation ladder for bounded recovery. Evaluated on 3,000 test episodes across 60 LIBERO-Pro tasks, REA reaches 95.9% success and beats the best single-agent baseline in its own policy pool (94.7%) while reducing control steps by 25% and control time by 17%. In a separate 100-episode RoboTwin 2.0 study, REA achieves 84% success with VLA policies and 86% with VLA+code, compared with 80% for the strongest single VLA baseline MEDex. These results demonstrate learned and programmatic policies operating within the same runtime. REA also transfers to BEHAVIOR-1K and a real Unitree G1 humanoid, achieving the highest observed success among the compared methods.
  </details>

- **[Nexus-VLA: Bridging Human and Robot Experiences via a Shared Hierarchical Semantic Latent Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42021)**  `arXiv:2610.42021`  
  _Biaoliang Guan, Tingyu Yuan, Wen Ye, Ziming Ji, Xu Yang, Yan Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale egocentric human videos provide rich and scalable manipulation experience for robot policy learning, yet substantial embodiment gaps in visual appearance, kinematic structure, and action spaces hinder effective knowledge transfer. Existing methods often decouple cross-embodiment knowledge alignment from robot policy learning, making it difficult for human manipulation knowledge to directly and fully contribute to robot action learning. To address this issue, we propose Nexus-VLA, a hybrid policy learning framework that enables cross-embodiment knowledge transfer through end-to-end human-robot joint training, allowing human manipulation experience to directly contribute to robot policy learning. We first introduce a hierarchical cross-embodiment semantic alignment paradigm that progressively establishes shared manipulation semantics from task intent to fine-grained interactions, enabling more complete use of the manipulation knowledge contained in human videos. Building on this hierarchy, we design a shared manipulation latent representation that jointly encodes multi-level shared knowledge into an internal representation directly used for continuous action generation, improving policy learning efficiency while effectively leveraging human knowledge. Across RoboCasa-GR1, OOD settings, and real-world robot experiments, Nexus-VLA achieves an 80.5% task success rate on RoboCasa-GR1 and demonstrates strong advantages in human-data utilization, learning efficiency, and cross-scenario generalization.
  </details>

- **[Suspended-Fabric Robotic Manipulation Surfaces: Modelling, Design Space, and Multimodular Extensio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41982)**  `arXiv:2610.41982`  
  _Pratik Ingle, Jørn Lambertsen, Kasper Støy, Andres Faiña_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic Manipulation Surfaces (RMS) reposition objects by deforming the surface on which they rest. A recent class of RMS suspends a continuous fabric sheet from a sparse grid of vertical linear actuators lowering the actuator density of such surfaces but how to design such surfaces so that objects can be controlled everywhere on it, and how to extend it beyond a single module so that objects can pass between modules and achieve parallel, remain open. This paper presents a separable catenary model of the suspended sheet that is fast to compute and provides both the surface shape for given actuator heights and the actuator heights for a desired local slope. The closed form inverse lets a simple PID controller position objects on the sheet, and the model relates fabric length, module span and actuator stroke to the reachable workspace, yielding a minimum fabric length bound and design charts for these surfaces. The approach is then extended to a multimodular platform in which neighbouring modules share actuators. On a $2 \times 2$ prototype, we demonstrate that centimetre-scale objects are transferred between modules and positioned within 0.03 m over a $1 m^2$ workspace with only nine actuators, enabling multi-object manipulation and opening the way to parallel manipulation.
  </details>

- **[Design and Validation of a Robotic Knee Exoskeleton with Musculoskeletal Simulation-Derived Biological Torque Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41981)**  `arXiv:2610.41981`  
  _Rajiv V. Joshi, Jimin An, Ilseung Park, Dawit Lee, Inseung Kang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data-driven controllers have enabled lower-limb robotic exoskeletons to adapt assistance to the user across many tasks. A leading example is biological torque control, which estimates the user's own joint moment from onboard sensors and assists in proportion to that estimate. However, its estimators are trained on data recorded with the target device, so deploying the controller on new hardware requires collecting new data. Existing open-source efforts release hardware, software, or controllers separately, so this data collection must still be repeated on the assembled device. Here, we present an open-source knee exoskeleton, built from off-the-shelf components and widely available fabrication tools, with a biological torque controller derived entirely from simulation. A muscle-driven imitation policy reproduces squatting motions from an open biomechanics dataset, and the resulting knee moments are distilled into an estimator that uses only onboard sensors, so no training data are recorded on the device. In seven able-bodied participants, assistance reduced peak biological knee extension moment by 21.5% and peak vastus medialis activation by 26.7% during squatting, relative to squatting without the exoskeleton (p<0.05). Our results demonstrate that a biological torque controller derived from simulation can transfer to physical hardware without device-specific data collection. Because the hardware and training pipeline are released together, research groups can train and compare knee exoskeleton controllers on shared hardware without additional data collection overhead.
  </details>

- **[Language-Conditioned Behavioral Cloning for Autonomous Endoscope Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41978)**  `arXiv:2610.41978`  
  _Ariel Rodriguez, Lorenzo Mazza, Ortrun Hellig, Alejandra Toscano, Rayan Younis, Stefanie Speidel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated endoscope guidance reduces the need for continuous manual camera control during minimally invasive surgery. However, the desired camera motion depends on the surgeon's immediate intentions and cannot always be inferred from the visual operative scene alone. We propose to automate the task using language-conditioned behavioral cloning; we define 6 clinically relevant guidance behaviors and collect 235 subtask demonstrations. In preliminary phantom experiments, our policy achieves 100% success on focus, overview, and stop, 80% on search-tip and follow-tip behaviors, and 50% on horizon leveling. These results demonstrate the feasibility of learning heterogeneous surgeon-requested camera behaviors with a single visuomotor policy, while the observed failure modes motivate future work on adding temporal context for more demanding guidance tasks.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Coverage Algorithms for Mobile Ad Hoc Networks in Dynamic Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41936)**  `arXiv:2610.41936`  
  _Hugo Gandhi, Edwin Meriaux, Antonio Loría_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper extends an existing framework for the coordination of mobile agents in unknown worlds, by considering the possibility of dynamic instability. In the original problem, known as the Partially Observable Cooperative Guard Art Gallery Problem (POCGAGP) (arXiv:2512.09495), the multi-agent system must establish a communication network which completely covers an initially unknown constrained space. However, the POCGAGP fails to account for the unpredictability often seen in real-world deployment. To address this, the dynamic case (DPOCGAGP) introduces two settings which can influence the mobile network during deployment: Agent Loss and World Change, which represent dynamic changes to the world. Within this study, we present advancements to two state-of-the-art algorithms, the Centralized Agent-Directed Exploration for Network Coverage (CADENCE), and its decentralized analogue, DADENCE, to provide solutions in these cases. We then compare their performance against another state-of-the-art benchmark algorithm, and show that the adapted algorithms outperform across our benchmark test set, which comprises over 4500 test cases of varied size and structure.
  </details>

- **[Online Learning of Heterogeneous Locomotion Dynamics under Reinforcement Learning Policies for Precise Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41892)**  `arXiv:2610.41892`  
  _Tianlin Zhang, Weipeng Xia, Lingwei Zhang, Yiyang Shao, Zhongyu Li, Yun-Hui Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How to reuse a pretrained locomotion policy for precise loco-manipulation is still an open problem. We propose an online method to learn the closed-loop dynamics under a frozen locomotion policy. The learned model predicts floating-base motion and improves loco-manipulation accuracy. However, unobserved latent conditions of the policy introduce heterogeneous responses, meaning that the same observed state will lead to different motions. Direct learning without accounting for this heterogeneity introduces bias and fluctuations into dynamics estimates, degrading floating-base prediction and end-effector tracking. To address this problem, a contaminated Gaussian model is proposed to approximate the effects of latent conditions. The model uses two Gaussian components to approximate predictable and unpredictable responses under the same observed state. Then, the contaminated Gaussian model is combined with a Gaussian process to construct an online learner using random Fourier features (RFFs). The learner estimates each response's probability of belonging to the predictable component and uses it to weight online updates, capturing predictable responses while reducing bias and fluctuations caused by heterogeneous observations. With consistent and unbiased dynamics estimates, model predictive control (MPC) predicts floating-base motion accurately and enables precise loco-manipulation. A finite-sample error bound is established to ensure uniform convergence in online learning. Simulation and hardware experiments demonstrate consistent end-effector tracking across different manipulator configurations and tasks, with errors of 0.01 m.
  </details>

- **[Movens-Zero: Scaling Humanoid Motion Foundation Models with Large-Scale Human Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41877)**  `arXiv:2610.41877`  
  _Wendong Bu, Tinghong Ye, Qizhou Wang, Jiacheng Li, Zhiqi Ge, Yuze Lin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling humanoid motion foundation models is constrained by the scarcity of diverse, robot-executable data and by the difficulty of jointly learning from heterogeneous multimodal inputs while supporting real-time closed-loop control. We present Movens-5M, constructed from raw Internet videos through clip generation, quality filtering, semantic annotation, motion recovery, and physical refinement with humanoid retargeting and execution verification. It contains 15 million human-motion sequences and 5 million physically executable humanoid-motion sequences spanning over 10,000 hours, aligned with text, RGB video, speech, and music. Building on this data, we introduce Movens-Zero, a scalable understanding-generation-execution architecture that supports joint learning across modalities and real-time closed-loop execution. Task-mixed flow-matching training jointly optimizes a single action expert across seven generation tasks, enabling direct motion generation from text, speech, music, and video. Multimodal Real-Time Chunking connects overlapping chunks under continuously updated instructions and robot states for continuous closed-loop execution. We further introduce Movens-Intent, a four-track benchmark with 8,192 expert-verified condition-motion pairs across text, speech, music, and video. Experiments show consistent gains across all four modalities when scaling execution-verified data from 1% to 100% and action-expert capacity from 64M to 911M parameters, while demonstrating that data quality and cross-modal supervision improve generation and execution.
  </details>

- **[META-Robot: Self-Cognition and Capability Scaling in Agentic Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41868)**  `arXiv:2610.41868`  
  _Xuanzhi Feng, Zeyu Liu, Weiheng Gao, Xinchen Li, Jiankun Tang, Fangqi Zhu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based robotic agent systems can generate programs for robot control, yet their generalization to new tasks often depends on elaborate scaffolding. Expanding their skill repertoires alone offers limited guidance on how to assess new capabilities or identify what remains beyond their reach. We introduce META-Robot, a framework that couples self cognition with capability scaling by expanding the criteria through which an agent evaluates and develops its skills. At its core, action-level predicates make execution outcomes explicit and available for LLM reasoning. The agent uses these assessments to identify its own limitations, extend relevant evaluation criteria, and guide the structured expansion of its skills. Proprioceptive feedback further grounds this process in the robot's physical state, enriching the evidence available for self-cognition. Experiments in simulation and on real robots demonstrate stronger overall task performance, generalization to new tasks, continued expansion of skills and evaluation criteria, and effective use of non-visual proprioceptive signals.
  </details>

- **[When the Simulator Fails, Not the Policy: Auditing Contact Artifacts in Simulated Robot Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41857)**  `arXiv:2610.41857`  
  _Alex Zhou, Junhao Wu, Xueyang Zhou, Yixiao Huo, Hongcheng Gao, Di Zhu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulation benchmarks score robot policies by success rate, and every failed episode counts against the policy. Many of these failures are the simulator's fault, not the policy's; we find the cause and fix it. Testing a vision language action model and a world action model in simulation, we find that the policy often places the object and the simulator then ejects it, and that the benchmark's own scripted demonstrations fail the same way. We present SimuGuard, an auditing plugin for LIBERO, RoboTwin, RoboCasa and RoboDojo. It leaves the evaluation unchanged. At every physics step, it records the state and flags an object that starts moving at a contact faster than the robot's contact or gravity account for. It replays a recorded episode bit for bit, and it reruns the episode with one simulation parameter changed. With it we trace these episodes on RoboTwin to the collision detection of the physics engine: for geometry one to two centimeters apart, the engine reports a penetration of about two centimeters, and the solver throws the object out at several meters per second. The cause is one constant: the distance algorithm discards triangles below a fixed area threshold, which valid triangles fall below at the centimeter scale. With that constant changed and nothing else, episodes with an artifact fall from 12 in 100 to none, and the success rate on five container tasks rises from 89% to 98%. Success rates in simulation thus count failures that the simulator causes. Benchmarks on PhysX 5.3.1 or earlier should rerun in a repaired engine. Where the engine cannot change, they should report the success rate together with the range it spans when every flagged episode fails or passes: 75.8% to 98.5% around a reported 83.1% for LingBot-VA.
  </details>

- **[SketchMate: Agentic Geometric Reasoning for Zero-Shot Precision Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41844)**  `arXiv:2610.41844`  
  _Jiaming Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inserting an unseen part requires sub-millimetre precision and the correct orientation of keyed features. Current zero-shot agentic manipulation methods built on vision-language models (VLMs) produce spatial outputs that are far too coarse for this. We propose to let a VLM build a sketch model of the task, i.e., a few geometric primitives and two mate frames, which a VLM agent measures with geometric tools in multi-view depth and which a standard planner and compliant controller execute. Because a sketch describes the parts independently of their placement, the robot needs to build it only once per task. A sketch memory stores each agent run and transfers the stored sketch to later placements by point-cloud registration, so that the VLM is called only for unfamiliar parts. After grasping the part, the robot inspects both mating faces at close range and corrects the sketch by shape matching. On 69 simulated insertion episodes, our method inserts 88% of the parts at the assembled pose in a strict kinematic test, while ReKep and MOKA with the same VLM insert none. On new placements of eight tasks, reusing one stored run inserts as many parts as calling the agent in each episode, and it produces the sketch in 3.4 s instead of about 90 s.
  </details>

- **[Visual-Inertial RGB-D SLAM with Encoders for a Differential Wheeled Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41768)**  `arXiv:2610.41768`  
  _Zhanghao Zhu, Yutaka Kaizu, Kenichi Furuhashi, Kenji Imou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent years have seen multiple impressive results in visual-inertial odometry (VIO) techniques, by which accurate state estimation can be achieved via the extended Kalman filter (EKF) or nonlinear optimization. However, these approaches are rarely open source, and they tend to fail in real experiments due to the temporary lack of feature points and fast motion. Therefore, in this study, we used encoders to overcome the temporary failure of purely vision-based simultaneous localization and mapping (SLAM). Here, we propose a generative measurement model for encoders and derive an expression for the maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimate and necessary Jacobians for optimization. We use our theory to present a novel tightly coupled visual-inertial encoder RGB-Depth (RGB-D) SLAM system. Tests on our system on an in-house dataset confirmed that our modeling effort led to accurate (with a root mean squared error (RMSE) of approximately 2-7 cm) and robust state estimation in real time. The source code and our dataset containing the encoder information have been published for verification.
  </details>

- **[Higher-Order Kinematic Derivatives for Aspect-Preserving Real-Time Numerical Inverse Kinematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41752)**  `arXiv:2610.41752`  
  _Steffan Lloyd_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper leverages the higher-order kinematic derivatives of serial robots to achieve efficient, aspect-preserving real-time inverse kinematics. First, a family of higher-order solvers is derived, with a necessary orientation-error correction map, yielding the QuIK4 and QuIK5 methods with fourth- and fifth-order convergence. Second, an aspect-preserving step limiter extrapolates the Jacobian determinant along the step to predict and avoid singularities, shortening steps only when they head toward a singularity rather than when the current iterate is ill-conditioned. Third, a Hermite-interpolation certifier verifies the aspect of each answer, and a certified restart algorithm recovers failures from proven same-aspect seeds. The proposed methods are shown to outperform prior methods, reducing failures by up to four orders of magnitude and nearly eliminating aspect switching at tracking-level errors. A C++ library is released with ROS 2 integration.
  </details>

- **[Flexibility-Aware Robotic Manipulation: Deformation Estimation, Kinematics, and Control via Distributed Inertial Measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41702)**  `arXiv:2610.41702`  
  _Amir Hossein Barjini, Sadeq Yaqubi, Pauli Mustalahti, Jouni Mattila_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flexible robot links challenge the rigid-body assumption, making joint measurements alone insufficient for accurate manipulation. This paper introduces a flexibility-aware manipulation framework that connects structural dynamics directly to sensing, kinematics, and control. Instead of prescribing a dense inertial measurement unit (IMU) network, the dominant vibration modes of each flexible link are first identified from response-convergence and excitation-bandwidth criteria. Under a full-rank modal observability condition, $m$ retained modes require only $m$ independent slope measurements and one reference IMU, defining the minimum observable IMU configuration. The resulting measurements are used to reconstruct the continuous link deformation, which is then incorporated into flexibility-aware forward kinematics and a deformation-informed partial differential equation-based controller. Experiments on hydraulic single- and double-link manipulators evaluate the framework. On the long-reach double-link platform, the proposed kinematics achieves end-effector root mean square errors of $0.0107$~m, $0.0194$~m, and $0.0102$~rad while using fewer sensors than the piecewise-rigid method. Closed-loop reaching experiments further demonstrate real-time integration of IMU-estimated joint states and reconstructed deformation into the proposed controller. The results show that dominant modal information can determine sensing requirements and enable real-time flexibility-aware robotic manipulation.
  </details>

- **[When the Past Shapes Action: Understanding Embodied Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41688)**  `arXiv:2610.41688`  
  _Siqi Li, Ziyi Bai, Xinrun Xu, Tinglei Huang, Börje F. Karlsson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in generative foundation models have expanded the capabilities of embodied AI, enabling increasingly general navigation, manipulation, and long-horizon task execution. However, effective interaction with dynamic environments requires more than only strong perception and reasoning: agents must act with partial knowledge and retain, retrieve, and update information from past observations, actions, and experiences. This survey examines embodied memory as a mechanism for preserving decision-relevant information beyond the current observation. We organize existing approaches along two complementary dimensions: six fundamental memory requirements, spanning world-state persistence, task progress, belief maintenance, and experiential learning; and eight architectural categories, including recurrent states, fast weights, visual and symbolic representations, spatial memory, and experience-based systems. We analyze their capabilities and limitations, and examine how existing benchmarks evaluate memory-dependent behavior, distinguishing genuine use of historical information from general task performance. Finally, we identify open challenges in selecting relevant experiences, maintaining consistent beliefs in changing environments, and consolidating knowledge across episodes. Our analysis provides a framework for understanding memory as an essential component of reliable, adaptive, and lifelong-horizon embodied intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Biomechanics do not predict perceptual and cognitive consequences of force augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41679)**  `arXiv:2610.41679`  
  _Maria Molina-Sanchez, Francesco Missiroli, Viktorija Pavalkyte, Mabel Ziman, Julien Russ, Maggie Szymanska, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wearable force-augmentation technologies, including exoskeletons and soft exosuits, are being developed for occupational and rehabilitation settings to enhance performance by reducing the muscular demands of movements. Yet enhanced performance also requires users to perceive the environment accurately, adapt to changing conditions, and decide whether actions are worth their physical cost. We used an elbow-assisting exosuit to investigate the physiological, perceptual and cognitive consequences of force augmentation during lifting. We assessed weight perception, adaptation in a volatile environment, and effort-based decision-making with and without assistance. While assistance reduced muscle activity, its behavioural outcomes did not simply reflect this biomechanical change. Assistance reduced perceived weight, but this effect was strongly shaped by participants' initial experience. Assistance also selectively reduced adaptation to changing environmental contingencies. Finally, reducing the muscular demands of lifting did not translate straightforwardly into how demanding the action felt or whether participants chose to perform it: differences in effort-based decisions were shaped by initial experience rather than simply tracking reductions in muscle activity or perceived fatigue. Together, we show that the perceptual and cognitive consequences of force augmentation cannot be inferred from its biomechanical benefits alone. Effective augmentation therefore requires perceptual and cognitive outcomes to be evaluated alongside physical performance.
  </details>

- **[Interaction-Aware Sampling-Based Motion Planning for Autonomous Surface Vessels in Urban Waterways](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41676)**  `arXiv:2610.41676`  
  _Elia Trevisan, Alejandro Gonzalez-Garcia, Jan Swevers, Javier Alonso-Mora_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous surface vessels operating in urban waterways must coordinate with other vessels in confined spaces while complying with traffic regulations, often without knowing their intentions. We present a decentralized motion planner based on model predictive path integral control, a sampling-based model predictive control scheme. The planner jointly computes a trajectory for the ego vessel and a prediction of the trajectories of the other vessels, and therefore accounts for their interaction. Traffic regulations are encoded as discontinuous costs, which the sampling-based formulation admits without a smooth approximation. We evaluate the method in maps taken from real sections of Amsterdam's canals, in encounters between two vessels and in scenarios with four interacting vessels. Across 780 randomized runs the method reaches every goal without collisions or deadlocks, and outperforms an optimization-based planner designed for the same domain, as well as a version of the proposed planner that does not account for the interaction. We further validate the planner on two scaled vessel prototypes in an outdoor environment, where planning and localization run onboard at 10 Hz, and head-on and crossing encounters are resolved without collisions or rule violations.
  </details>

- **[OpenVIO: A Versatile Open-Source Visual-Inertial Odometry System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41671)**  `arXiv:2610.41671`  
  _Seungwon Choi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual-inertial odometry (VIO) is a fundamental topic in robotics research. Beyond research, VIO has been adopted in a wide range of products. In this manuscript, we present OpenVIO, an open-source VIO system for research and practical applications. It is released under the MIT license. OpenVIO supports pinhole and fisheye cameras and extends this support to stitched 360° images. We describe each part of the system together with its design considerations. We also report benchmark results with other open-source VIO systems on public datasets. OpenVIO is available as open source atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GPU-accelerated Incremental Euclidean Distance Transform for Online Motion Planning of Mobile Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41667)**  `arXiv:2610.41667`  
  _Yizhou Chen, Shupeng Lai, Jinqiang Cui, Biao Wang, Ben M. Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this letter, we present a volumetric mapping system that effectively calculates Occupancy Grid Maps (OGMs) and Euclidean Distance Transforms (EDTs) with parallel computing. Unlike these mappers for high-precision structural reconstruction, our system incrementally constructs global EDT and outputs high-frequency local distance information for online robot motion planning. The proposed system constructs OGM with massive amount of data from multiple types of sensor inputs. Using GPU programming techniques, the system quickly computes EDT in parallel within local volume. The new observation is continuously integrated into the global EDT using the parallel wavefront algorithm while preserving the historical observations. Experiments with datasets have shown that our proposed approach outperforms existing state-of-the-art robot mapping systems and is particularly suitable for mapping unexplored areas. In its actual implementations on aerial and ground vehicles, the proposed system achieves real-time performance with limited onboard computational resources.
  </details>

- **[ResMerge: Residual Policy Learning and Merging for Continual Adaptation of Pre-trained Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41663)**  `arXiv:2610.41663`  
  _Kefei Zhu, Lingyun Xu, Yue Xin, Xiaoqi Li, Caixin Guo, Xincheng Du, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual adaptation to new tasks is essential for robots deployed in the real world, but adapting pre-trained policies remains challenging due to parameter growth and the need for continual demonstration collection. Parameter-Efficient Continual Fine-Tuning (PECFT) isolates task-specific updates, but adapter-per-task designs require demonstrations and grow with the task set. We propose ResMerge, a two-stage framework that learns mergeable residual policies atop a frozen pre-trained policy: residual reinforcement learning (RL) acquires task-specific corrections without additional expert demonstrations during adaptation, and parameter-space merging consolidates compatible residuals to reduce stored parameters. We introduce Residual-Field Consistency (RFC), a simple, bounded, and interpretable metric that assesses compatibility in residual action-field space by comparing correction direction and magnitude. Experiments on LIBERO across two backbones and on a real-world Franka robot show improved continual learning performance over strong PECFT baselines, while selective merging reduces the residual-parameter footprint. Project website:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[$\nabla$Mimic: Ultra-Efficient Humanoid Control with Differentiable Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41654)**  `arXiv:2610.41654`  
  _Weyl Lu, Yubei Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot policy learning is dominated by zeroth-order methods such as PPO, which treat the simulator as a black box and pay for it with enormous sample counts. First-order policy learning differentiates through the dynamics, but on contact-rich whole-body control it has rarely worked: its gradients explode. A recent world-model method showed that derivative information can make humanoid motion tracking far more efficient, using surrogate gradients of a learned model. We show that exact gradients of the simulator itself do better, once two obstacles are removed. Exact gradients contain rare unbounded samples, because contact dynamics is not differentiable everywhere and its derivative diverges near some events; and they are expensive, because the simulator's adjoint has to replay the contact solve.$\nabla$Mimic bounds these samples with median-relative per-sample gradient clipping at every step of the backward pass, and reuses each costly simulated window by multistep regression onto gradient-derived action targets, which bounds every action target independently of the gradient scale. NablaEngine, a differentiable GPU simulation engine for the Unitree G1, removes the cost: it computes MuJoCo's full contact dynamics with fused per-world kernels and the adjoint of a whole control step as a single megakernel, with analytic gradients that match float64 finite differences, $11.5\times$ faster than the available MuJoCo Warp adjoint.On whole-body tracking of all 40 LAFAN1 clips with one policy on one GPU, $\nabla$Mimic reaches the score reported by the state-of-the-art world-model method, 0.82, in 20 minutes instead of 1.2 h, and 0.874 in 1.8 h. SHAC, the standard first-order baseline, stops on exploding gradients within minutes. We release NablaEngine and $\nabla$Mimic as open-source infrastructure for first-order humanoid policy learning.
  </details>

- **[Direct Action-Manifold Modeling for Efficient and Robust Bimanual Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41643)**  `arXiv:2610.41643`  
  _Hongren Wang, Mingyu You, Zihan Li, Zhipeng Wang, Shuo Jiang, Bin He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion and flow matching visuomotor policies require iterative denoising or numerical integration for multimodal action generation, introducing latency and execution mismatch. We instead consider a different formulation: conditioned on vision and proprioception, demonstrated bimanual action chunks concentrate in a structured observation-dependent region of joint-action space, making the action target more direct to model than the generative process. Based on this view, we propose the Direct Manifold Action Transformer (DMAT), which reformulates action generation as the direct modeling of clean, observation-conditioned joint-space action chunks from stochastic interpolants, thereby enabling single-step inference without relying on iterative generative inference or auxiliary trajectory-straightening procedures. Within DMAT, local 3D geometry anchors predictions to spatial structure, while State-Consistent Spectral-Time Refinement (STR) reconciles predicted chunks with proprioception and suppresses high-frequency residuals. Across 567 standard and out-of-distribution (OOD) trials per method, DMAT outperformed Improved 3D Diffusion Policy (IDP3) and DiTFlow3D in aggregate success rate, achieving relative improvements of 129.1% and 95.7%, respectively, while generating action chunks 2.06x and 1.34x faster and remaining robust under repeated 50 N interventions. These results pave the way for a novel generative paradigm for efficient and robust bimanual manipulation.
  </details>

- **[RTPolicy: Real-Time (32.9 Hz) Multi-View Visuomotor Policy for Robotic Manipulation on Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41625)**  `arXiv:2610.41625`  
  _Xianbao Hou, Zehao Ni, Yonghao He, Lingfeng Qian, Xin Shi, Hu Su, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low inference latency is critical for closed-loop robotic manipulation, especially under asynchronous inference, where action chunks must be updated promptly to correct accumulated deviations. However, existing visuomotor policies rely on computationally expensive visual backbones, resulting in high inference latency and deployment overhead. In this paper, we propose RTPolicy, a multi-view visuomotor policy that attains accurate manipulation with high inference efficiency. Its core design combines view-aware feature fusion with visual compression to aggregate complementary information across camera views into a compact token representation. The representation conditions a flow-matching action generator with the robot state and flow time, reducing the cost of action generation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that this design reduces the computational cost of visual representation and action generation while preserving visual information required for manipulation. On the RoboTwin~2.0 single-task clean evaluation across 50 tasks, RTPolicy attains a mean success rate of 52.26\%, outperforming the strongest leaderboard baseline $\pi_0$ (46.4\%). We further evaluate RTPolicy under asynchronous streaming inference on a real bimanual platform, where RTPolicy attains a mean success rate of 72.0\% and sustains real-time inference at 30.35 ms (32.9 Hz) on the RDK S600 and 149.72 ms (6.7 Hz) on the RDK S100, both in INT16. Project Page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[VLA Policies Do Not Know When Not to Act: Unintended Task Execution and Start Suppression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41609)**  `arXiv:2610.41609`  
  _Minsu Jang, Jae-Woo Choi, Youngwoo Yoon, Dohyung Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A robot should act only on an instruction from a person or on an intent that the robot explicitly represents and generates. Unintended actions taken without such an instruction can cause risk or inconvenience to people and the environment. We examine how recent vision-language-action (VLA) policies respond when the instruction is missing or meaningless. We apply a paired protocol with empty, neutral, nonsense, infeasible, and wrong-task instructions to six model checkpoints in LIBERO-Goal simulation. Holding the initial state fixed and varying only the instruction, we ask three questions: whether the robot starts to move, which task it completes, and what the instruction determines. Even when the instruction was empty, the robot started moving in every episode with all six checkpoints, and with five of them it often completed a task that the instruction had not specified. On two GR00T checkpoints, the instruction determined which task was executed but not whether the robot started moving; the robot moved even on infeasible instructions. We then tested three methods for suppressing the start: prepending "if no task is given do not move" to the instruction; a gate that holds the robot when the first action chunk for the given instruction nearly equals that for an empty instruction; and fine-tuning the policy to hold still on non-task inputs. The prefix stopped the robot in none of the six checkpoints, and the gate held it on non-task inputs in five of the six checkpoints. The fine-tuned policy never completed a task on non-task inputs, but it did not reliably keep the robot still, and it failed to suppress the start on infeasible instructions.
  </details>

- **[AC-SMPC: Differentiable Actor--Critic MPC with State Constraints for Safe and Fast Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41477)**  `arXiv:2610.41477`  
  _Chaoran Wu, Jiayu Fan, Qichen Tan, Yuyi Liu, Jin Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Achieving safe navigation in confined dynamic environments remains a challenging problem, as robots must balance navigation efficiency with safety. Existing actor--critic MPC architectures typically employ differentiable MPC layers, but this way cannot readily accommodate explicit state constraints, making it intractable to involve collision avoidance within learning-based planning. To address this issue, we propose an actor--critic model predictive control framework with state constraints. We first use vertices to represent the robot and obstacles, and half-spaces to represent the workspace. Based on these, the nonlinear robot dynamics is locally linearized and the hyperplane-based collision avoidance constraints are convexified into the differentiable MPC layer, and then the actor exploits closed-loop feedback to adjust the MPC objective parameters that shape local motion behavior, while the critic evaluates long-term task performance. We further relax the convexified constraints with bounded and penalized slack variables to form an elastic quadratic program, accommodating cases where the local constraint approximations cannot be satisfied simultaneously. Simulation tests and experiments, involving dynamic collision avoidance for multi-robot in narrow cases, illustrate the advantages of the proposed framework in adaptively adjusting the learned policy and improving navigation efficiency over existing approaches while maintaining the motion safety.
  </details>

- **[SAFARI: Semantic- And Frontier-driven Autonomous Risk Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41456)**  `arXiv:2610.41456`  
  _Ugur Kafkasyali, Mihnea Toader, Julia Richter, Turcan Tuna, Cesar Cadena, Marco Hutter_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic exploration can reduce human exposure to hazardous environments by autonomously searching unknown areas for objects of interest while constructing a complete, risk-aware map.However, existing autonomous exploration approaches typically prioritize geometric coverage or information gain, without explicitly coupling semantic object detection and the risk associated with operating near potentially hazardous objects.Moreover, many systems have been demonstrated primarily in simulation or controlled environments, leaving limited validation of risk-aware semantic exploration in complex, unstructured field settings.To address this gap, we propose SAFARI, a framework that treats object detection as a native channel of a unified probabilistic map, thereby jointly informing exploration and planning with both geometric and semantic evidence.We evaluated SAFARI in 11 missions across three field sites on a quadruped platform. The framework achieved an average coverage of 98.7% of the accessible mission area, without operator intervention for 97.6% of mission time, and detected objects with characteristic dimensions as small as 15cm.Lastly, through ablation studies and a detailed analysis of system behavior, we show that object-size awareness and safety-aware path planning are crucial for effective hazardous-object search.
  </details>

- **[BioAgent: Self-Directed Skill Acquisition and Capability Growth for Long-Horizon Laboratory Protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41383)**  `arXiv:2610.41383`  
  _Gaojing Zhang, Changwen Chen, Zichen Luo, Jialiang Li, Yuhan Wang, Yifei Xie, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Executing an unfamiliar laboratory protocol requires a robot to identify missing capabilities and determine whether each operation remains valid as the experiment progresses. This letter presents BioAgent, an embodied agent that addresses these coupled challenges of capability sufficiency and experimental validity. It matches human-reviewed requirements to validated skills and task packages, selects tool-based implementations for missing behaviors, and requests human teaching when needed during learning. Learned skills undergo sequential task-package validation to check outcomes and compatibility between successive operations. During execution, precondition, outcome, and experimental-state checks govern progression, autonomous recovery, or termination. Execution evidence guides tool selection, while accepted experience supports offline policy updates and task packaging. On the second task, BioAgent reuses 17 skill types unchanged, revises two existing implementations, and acquires five new types. On two dual-arm laboratory tasks with 20 and 31 ordered skill invocations, BioAgent achieves complete-task success rates of 66\% and 58\% with autonomous recovery over 50 runs per task. Across 25 atomic skills evaluated over 50 autonomous trials each with recovery disabled, success rates range from 84\% to 100\%, with 17 skills achieving 50/50.
  </details>

- **[MolmoSpaces-CommonSense: Evaluating Commonsense in Robotic Manipulation Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41377)**  `arXiv:2610.41377`  
  _Arjun Guru, Max Argus, Jae Sung Park, Jiafei Duan, Ali Farhadi, Ranjay Krishna_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world robotic manipulation requires reasoning about cluttered scenes, partially observable or hidden objects, and semantically appropriate actions, in addition to low-level execution within those scenes. However, existing simulation benchmarks primarily test the latter and remain dominated by comparatively clean environments with limited clutter and narrowly scoped task structure, leaving many forms of commonsense reasoning untested. We address this by introducing a taxonomy of reasoning processes for robotic manipulation grounded in developmental cognitive science. We operationalize this taxonomy through a new benchmark of robot tasks, MolmoSpaces-CommonSense, that requires reasoning about occlusion, containment, support relations, semantically correct grasp points, spatial affordances, and hidden objects. These evaluations require policies not only to recognize relevant scene structure but also to execute correct actions in them. We evaluate several zero-shot generalist manipulation policies. Across model types and architectures, we observe consistent failures in commonsense reasoning: success rates in cluttered scenes drop by over 50% as distractors increase, policies act on incorrect objects in cluttered settings 30% of the time, and affordance-sensitive grasping remains unreliable even with highly specific language prompts, with correct object part selection happening only about 50% of the time. Further, VLM-based success estimation struggles to reliably evaluate rollouts in these settings, suggesting that the challenge extends beyond control to the broader perception-reasoning-action stack. Our results suggest that existing benchmarks may substantially overestimate zero-shot real-world readiness. Code and policy leaderboard link atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DiffWBC: Unlocking Steerable General Humanoid Motion Tracking via One-Step Diffusion Whole-Body Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41354)**  `arXiv:2610.41354`  
  _Yifei Huang, Yazhao Yang, Weijia Li, Zhiheng Ma, Dian Shen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General humanoid motion trackers typically rely on deterministic policies that cannot adapt online without brittle reference editing or costly retraining. We present DiffWBC, shifting whole-body control to a generative paradigm via a one-step diffusion model. By modeling distributions over future trajectories, DiffWBC unifies general tracking, test-time steering, and simulation-free failure discovery within a single controller: (1) Generative general tracking: DiffWBC requires a single forward pass per 50 Hz control step and can be distilled from an RL teacher or trained from scratch via model-based RL, achieving the lowest local tracking error among six general trackers on held-out benchmarks (20.1 mm on BONES) and deploying on a physical Unitree G1. (2) Zero-shot test-time steering: differentiable cost gradients and body-group masks modulate the frozen controller online, substantially outperforming kinematic reference editing (0.84 clean pass at 1.25 m ceiling vs. 0.10) and zero-shot generalizing across hurdles, spatial reaching, actuator faults, and tray balancing. (3) Simulation-free failure discovery: plan dispersion across generation noise effectively identifies physical tracking failures (91% precision in top 2%) without physics simulations; targeted fine-tuning on flagged clips resolves 50% of failure cases. Videos and interactive demonstrations are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CalibSwitch: Observation-Driven Camera-LiDAR Extrinsic Recovery and Selective Maintenance for Multimodal Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41352)**  `arXiv:2610.41352`  
  _Haifa Zhang, Yijing Wang, Zhiqiang Zuo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Camera--LiDAR perception on mobile robots and intelligent vehicles depends on reliable sensor geometry throughout deployment. Installation variations create uncertainty in the initial extrinsics, while changing observation quality makes frame-wise estimates fluctuate during operation. This paper presents CalibSwitch, an observation-driven framework that separates absolute extrinsic recovery from selective state maintenance. A cross-modal absolute extrinsic generator (CAEG) combines projection-free bidirectional feature interaction with geometry-regularized learning to recover complete SE(3) candidates from image--point observations and coarse view context, without a supplied numerical initial transform. A selective recurrent calibration policy (SRCP) combines observation quality and temporal history to determine when a candidate should replace the working geometry consumed by a frozen multimodal detector. On nuScenes, CAEG achieves mean translation and rotation errors of 0.248 m and 5.884$^\circ$. Under sustained geometric transitions, SRCP reaches a mean composite error of 1.46 with 9.70\% candidate commitments, outperforming quality-based and temporal-consistency gating at comparable commitment rates. Relative to unconditional per-frame updating, it improves frozen-detector mAP and NDS by 1.43 and 0.92 points. Paired geometry--observation uncertainty tests, two real-vehicle platforms, and 13.5-FPS single-camera Jetson execution demonstrate the system value of selective geometric-state maintenance for continuous perception.
  </details>

- **[Geometry-Aware Humanoid Retargeting via Manifold ODEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41349)**  `arXiv:2610.41349`  
  _Pengyu Zhu, Heye Wang, Xi Wang, Xuyang Wu, He Kong, Guodong Shi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we focus on interaction-preserving motion retargeting, which transfers human demonstrations to humanoids while maintaining spatial relationships with objects and terrain. Previous work, such as OmniRetarget, addresses this task through temporally coupled constrained optimization, requiring repeated sequential quadratic programming (SQP) iterations at each frame. We study this problem from an ordinary differential equation (ODE) perspective by modeling the continuous-time limit of its framewise Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) conditions as an ODE on the robot configuration manifold. This viewpoint offers two benefits: (i) it connects OmniRetarget's SQP iterations to a backward discretization of the ODE, enabling us to establish their local convergence under suitable regularity assumptions; and (ii) it leads to TaSP-KKT (tangent-space parametrization for KKT flow), a new retargeting method based on a forward discretization of the KKT flow. The proposed method achieves the same first-order convergence to the ODE solution as the corresponding backward scheme while requiring only one weighted tangent-cone projection and a closed-form manifold update per step, thereby avoiding repeated inner SQP iterations. Numerical experiments show that TaSP-KKT achieves retargeting quality comparable to, though slightly lower than, that of OmniRetarget while running approximately twice as fast.
  </details>

- **[Joint-Health-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Robust Robot Control Under Actuator Degradation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41348)**  `arXiv:2610.41348`  
  _Haifa Zhang, Yijing Wang, Zhiqiang Zuo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots deployed in unstructured environments are susceptible to actuator-authority degradation, which reduces the effective control authority of individual actuators and can cause reinforcement learning policies trained under nominal dynamics to fail. Existing robustness approaches rely on discrete fault labels, broad domain randomization, or global latent variables, often without explicitly modeling continuous per-joint residual actuation capability. We propose a joint-health-aware reinforcement learning framework for robust robot control under simulated actuator degradation. Our framework encodes actuator condition in a continuous joint-health vector and models degradation as multiplicative attenuation of executed actions. A dedicated HealthNet estimator infers per-joint health from recent observations and actions, allowing the policy to redistribute control effort across healthy and degraded joints. We evaluate the method on Walker, Go2, Cassie, and Panda-based ManiSkill manipulation tasks under diverse degradation masks and severity levels. The method outperforms nominal and degradation-trained no-health baselines on the main evaluated metrics. Relative to the nominal baseline, task-level locomotion returns improve by up to 250.1\% and manipulation success rates by up to 15.2 percentage points; in the high-degradation Cassie group, return increases from 3.94 to 40.50 (+928.0\%). Ablations support the contribution of per-joint health estimation, action-degradation modeling, and health-conditioned control.
  </details>

- **[HandMimicX: Learning Human-Like Dexterous Manipulation via an Object-Centric Policy from Egocentric Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41336)**  `arXiv:2610.41336`  
  _Zihan Gao, Yufei Liu, Yuhong Zhang, Zhen Yu, Xinhao Huang, Zhinan You, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning dexterous manipulation from human videos remains an open challenge, particularly in the context of transferring a single demonstration into a spatially generalizable robotic skill and maintaining physically plausible human-like interactions. Unlike prior works that emphasize hand-motion imitation, we introduce an object-centric framework for acquiring dexterous skills from egocentric human RGB-D videos. Within our framework, we formulate the reinforcement learning (RL) objective in terms of object motion to decouple the underlying skill from the specific hand trajectory and impose constraints on hand-object interaction states to promote more anatomically natural hand configurations. Extensive experiments in simulation and on real robots demonstrate that our method enables efficient learning from human videos and generalizable skill transfer across novel spatial configurations.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Embodiment Motion Planning by Coupling Body-Agnostic World Model with Differentiable Ego-Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41330)**  `arXiv:2610.41330`  
  _Zhidong Wang, Zirui Li, Haoran Xu, Yujie Yan, Zitong Shan, Jingsong Liang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Motion planning in clutter is embodiment-specific: the feasible free space and reachable commands depend on each robot's geometry and dynamics. Existing cross-embodiment policies output normalized commands that disregard these differences, or compensate through per-robot training and distillation. World models can in principle pool cross-embodiment data, yet command-conditioned models bind their latent dynamics to a single action space, while pose-conditioned models omit the mapping from commands to body-specific motion. On flat ground, however, embodiment affects the planner's observations only through the camera ego-motion. This letter proposes Hourglass, an hourglass-shaped architecture that factorizes cross-embodiment motion planning at this interface: a body-agnostic world model and each robot's differentiable ego-dynamics, which map commands to that ego-motion, are coupled only through it. The world model is trained on data pooled across embodiments and camera mounts; each robot's ego-dynamics is a reduced-order template with identified physical parameters. A generalist parameter-conditioned policy is learned once at this junction: each imagined step rolls out the template and then the world model, and imagination training through each robot's template makes the policy body-aware. On seven simulated robots of five locomotion types, the generalist policy outperforms imitation-learning and diffusion-policy baselines on the same world model by 3.8--10.6 percentage points in success rate and matches per-robot specialists without dedicated training, while approaching its parameter-conditioned MPPI teacher within 1.4--2.8 points without an online map. After three minutes of teleoperated driving for identification, the synthetic-data-trained model transfers zero-shot to a real skid-steer robot unseen in training, navigating unseen indoor scenes.
  </details>

- **[Learning from Physical Experience:Context-Space Adaptation for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41320)**  `arXiv:2610.41320`  
  _Yihan Zeng, Yisu Li, Kaixin Huang, Marc WuDunn, Amelia Kovacs, Mikael Harseno, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent multimodal foundation models exhibit strong perception, reasoning, and in-context learning capabilities, but translating these capabilities into physical action remains challenging. Through real-world experiments across diverse manipulation tasks, we find that ChatGPT-6-Astra, a state-of-the-art general-purpose reasoner, can serve as a physically actionable, context-responsive manipulation policy, demonstrating effective fine manipulation and spatial reasoning. Building on these findings, we introduce LEXP, a harness for learning from physical experience through context-space adaptation. LEXP combines task-level planning with an experience module that interprets execution evidence, extracts and retains lessons with applicability conditions, and reuses relevant lessons to generate contextual feedback for subsequent actions. Model weights and the action interface remain fixed throughout. Across 12 real-world manipulation tasks, LEXP achieves $23/36$ successful trials (63.9\%), compared with $19/36$ (52.8\%) with memory alone and $2/36$ (5.6\%) for vanilla Astra. On three tasks, bread transfer, sort trash and toiletries, and retrieve drawer, removing planning reduces success from $7/9$ to $4/9$, while removing retained experience reduces it to $3/9$. Execution traces illustrate feedback-guided corrections and cross-episode reuse of an inspection strategy. Execution analyses illustrate how task structure and experience-derived feedback guide task progression, action correction, and experience reuse.
