These results support context-space adaptation as a practical mechanism for turning physical interaction into reusable guidance for robotic manipulation.
  </details>

- **[TRACE: Target-State Risk-Aligned Conditional Execution for Embodied Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41275)**  `arXiv:2610.41275`  
  _Mingyang Lyu, Gongce Cao, Yiyang Jia, Lu Jia, Erliang Lin, Yuhao Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied visual tracking requires a robot to follow a language-specified person under occlusion, look-alike distractors, and ambiguous instructions. We show that improving tracking persistence can simultaneously increase safety violations. Using a compact 0.6B vision-language-action (VLA) tracker with a frozen backbone, we update only the planner head with proximal policy optimization (PPO). PPO improves success and following rates across all three EVT-Bench settings, but increases the collision rate on distracted tracking because episodes that previously ended in target loss can instead end in contact. We introduce TRACE, an execution-time target-state risk gate that preserves the learned policy and intervenes selectively. A lightweight risk head processes 84 cues, including candidate boxes, identity margins, a learned range-and-bearing probe, and the policy's commands. When the calibrated risk exceeds a threshold, TRACE rescales the translational component of the next waypoint while leaving yaw unchanged. The executed action is identical to PPO's on more than 98% of steps. In paired evaluations on identical episodes, TRACE reduces the distracted-tracking collision rate by 3.96 percentage points, while further improving success and following rates. Matched slowdown controls without target-state timing do not reproduce this improvement. Concurrent evaluation reproduces a collision reduction of 3.77 points, compared with 0.29 points for identical-arm null pairs, indicating that the effect exceeds evaluation variability. These results show that target-state-aware execution can recover the safety cost of policy post-training with minimal intervention.
  </details>

- **[CONSORT: Uncertainty-Constrained Multi-Robot Planning for Cooperative Localization in GPS-Denied Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41235)**  `arXiv:2610.41235`  
  _Tony Zhang, Ryan Bartholomew, Kalliyan Velasco, Joshua Mangelson, Brady Moon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Centralized multi-agent path planning with cooperative localization in GPS-denied environments is generally computationally intractable when formulated in full belief space due to tightly coupled uncertainty dynamics. Prior work typically approximates belief dynamics or restricts inter-agent coupling. This work examines a multi-agent planning problem in which a primary agent must reach a goal in minimum time subject to a chance-constrained collision-avoidance requirement and a terminal bound on localization uncertainty. We obtain a tractable surrogate of the underlying belief-space problem by optimizing over the mean-state trajectories of all agents and propagating covariance for terminal constraint evaluation only. We propose CONSORT (Cooperative Navigation with Support-Optimized Routing and Trajectories), a discrete-to-continuous pipeline combining hexagonal-grid joint search with spline-based trajectory refinement. Across 50 randomized scenarios, the planners separate as the terminal uncertainty bound tightens, with CONSORT solving 80% of scenarios where the strongest baseline solves 54%, at comparable path length. In marine simulations, with the plans executed under feedback control on an independently developed factor-graph estimate, the trajectories remain collision-free and their terminal uncertainty falls within 6% of the planner's prediction.
  </details>

- **[TinyAstral: Transferring Self-Evolving Harnesses to Tiny Generalist Manipulation Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41216)**  `arXiv:2610.41216`  
  _Kehong Gong, Xiaoxu Zheng, Guanli Hou, Seunghun Lee, Mengqi Guo, Runjia Tan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot agents built on vision-language models depend on both the planner and its harness: the tools, feedback, memory, and recovery logic around it. Can improvements to a large teacher's harness be carried into a compact agent with lower inference compute cost? We present TinyAstral, a two-stage approach. We first freeze an open-weight teacher and improve its harness through recursive self-improvement (RSI): frontier LLM agents read the teacher's trajectories and propose harness edits, and each edit is accepted only if it passes a paired statistical test. We then freeze the final harness, collect teacher trajectories with reasoning and failure recovery, and distill a small student that replaces the teacher in the same harness. On RoboTwin, harness evolution raises the teacher's success from 33.4% to 63.6%; the final 4B student reaches 62.8%. Across four harness versions, distilled 4B students score close to their corresponding teachers, and later versions yield stronger students, showing that harness evolution gains carry into compact models. The harness gains also transfer to other frozen planners without retraining. On identical episodes, the final 4B student outperforms open 4B agents, the open 27B harness-RSI model SenseNova-RoboRSI, and our own teacher model placed in three competitors' harnesses. It stays close to its teacher on all three training benchmarks, and on three benchmarks never used for RSI or training it scores close to a 552B planner running the same harness. On the measured deployment episodes, it uses about one fifth of its teacher's accelerator time.
  </details>

- **[Capability-Conditioned Graph Control Barrier Functions for Zero-Shot Safe Navigation of Heterogeneous Mobile Robot Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41214)**  `arXiv:2610.41214`  
  _Joonkyung Kim, Janghyun Cho, Jeehyun Lee, Changjoo Nam, Yiwei Lyu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-robot systems are increasingly deployed as heterogeneous fleets with diverse kinematic and actuation capabilities. While graph control barrier function (GCBF) methods provide a scalable framework for decentralized safe control, existing approaches typically assume homogeneous robot dynamics. Methods that directly encode robot classes can distinguish known types, but do not represent state-dependent motion capabilities or naturally transfer to unseen robot models. To address this challenge, we propose the Capability-Conditioned Heterogeneous Graph Control Barrier Function (CC-HGCBF), defined in a shared second-order reference-point operational space. This formulation provides a common geometric representation of safety across heterogeneous robots while retaining their model-specific dynamics and admissible control constraints. To enable zero-shot transfer, we introduce the Radial Capability Field (RCF), a compact, fixed-dimensional representation of each robot's state-dependent finite-horizon reachable velocity set. Conditioning the shared graph barrier and decentralized policy on the RCF enables them to adapt to changes in robot state, kinematic constraints, and control limits without relying on predefined robot identities. We jointly train the barrier and policy using proactive safety labeling and neural CBF-QP supervision, and evaluate them on mixed teams containing both seen and unseen robot models. Experiments demonstrate scalable safe coordination and zero-shot transfer to robot models and capability variations withheld during training. Physical-robot experiments further demonstrate zero-shot deployment to unseen kinematic models without retraining.
  </details>

- **[A Capability-Aware Backup-Recoverability Filter for Heterogeneous Multi-Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41188)**  `arXiv:2610.41188`  
  _Joonkyung Kim, Yanze Zhang, Wenhao Luo, Yiwei Lyu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe navigation in heterogeneous multi-robot teams faces two coupled challenges: differences in robots' braking and turning capabilities limit how each robot can respond to an approaching neighbor, while simultaneous interactions require a single control action to satisfy multiple collision-avoidance constraints. Together, these challenges can make local safety constraints incompatible with a robot's admissible controls, leaving a decentralized safety controller without a feasible action. We propose a Capability-Aware Backup-Recoverability Filter (CA-BRF), based on the insight that navigation actions should preserve access to a certified recovery maneuver. First, we construct a backup-recoverable set in a shared Cartesian operational space by evaluating whether prescribed backup maneuvers maintain pairwise safety until the robots come to rest. Each maneuver respects the corresponding robot's kinematic and actuation constraints, making the recovery certificate capability-dependent. Second, CA-BRF filters candidate controls by selecting admissible interpolations between candidate and backup actions while coordinating the selection across simultaneous interactions. This preserves backup recoverability while retaining as much of the candidate action as possible and ensures that all pairwise recovery conditions are satisfied by a single executable control for each robot. Together, we establish recursive feasibility and safety for the discrete-time closed-loop system even when the nominal safety-constrained optimization becomes infeasible. The validity and effectiveness of the proposed CA-BRF are validated in simulations with up to 30 heterogeneous robots and experiments of robots with five different kinematic classes.
  </details>

- **[Mechanistic Interpretability for End-to-End Self-Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41185)**  `arXiv:2610.41185`  
  _Manav Gagvani, Benjamin Namikas, Sivamurugan Velmurugan, Nysa Kumar, Shrey Sharma, Thomas Peterson, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving models achieve strong planning performance but remain largely opaque, limiting observability and trust. We apply mechanistic interpretability to E2E driving by training Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) on the internal activations of a simple, single-camera end-to-end path planning model. Unsupervised clustering of SAE latents reveals interpretable structure aligned with route intent, vehicle speed, and braking behavior, recovering route intent with 98.3% accuracy without supervision. Through paired counterfactual image edits, we further show that specific SAE features are visually grounded and systematically respond to scene changes such as traffic light state and stop sign presence. Steering individual SAE latents produces predictable, monotonic changes in planner outputs including lateral displacement, curvature, and braking. Finally, we demonstrate a practical application of these representations: we build a lightweight adapter trained on SAE latents that reduces trajectory selection error by 3.44%, outperforming an equivalent adapter that omits SAE latents, confirming that the sparse representation captures useful information for interpretability. Code and visualizations are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Veloxity: Towards Memory-Safe UAV Embedded Firmware using the Rust Programming Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41053)**  `arXiv:2610.41053`  
  _Derek Ward, Brian Bascom, Tyler Larsen, James Usevitch, Phil Tokumaru, Tim McLain_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent years have witnessed increased momentum towards memory-safe programming languages for safety-critical systems. Memory-safe languages can eliminate entire classes of common software errors that have historically required extensive analysis and expenditure to avoid. Nevertheless, memory-safe language adoption in aerospace remains fragmented and incomplete, particularly for unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs). Recent Rust UAV projects either run only in simulation, are closed source, or have not been integrated with an established autonomy ecosystem and tested against existing C/C++ firmware. To address this gap, we present Veloxity: an open-source embedded UAV autopilot written in the memory-safe Rust programming language. Veloxity lowers the barrier to using memory-safe languages within modern ecosystems by providing a minimal, modular foundation for autonomous UAVs. We compare Veloxity to equivalent autopilots written in C++ in simulation, with hardware testbenches, and via outdoor waypoint-following flight tests, demonstrating functional parity with improved performance. These results indicate that modern memory-safe languages are a viable option for reliable and performant firmware for autonomous aerospace systems.
  </details>

- **[Contact Imitation Interface: Transferring Dexterous Skills by Showing How Contact Unfolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41028)**  `arXiv:2610.41028`  
  _Zhixuan Xu, Yichen Li, Bingchen Wu, Rui Wang, Yiwen Hou, Zixuan Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot-free human demonstrations offer a promising route to scaling dexterous manipulation data. The key challenge is to preserve the demonstrator's natural dexterity while producing contact-grounded observations and actions that transfer to robot hands. We present Contact Imitation Interface (CIMI), a framework with contact-grounded demonstration and learning interfaces. The demonstration interface combines shared contact geometry and fingertip RGB views with contact-alignment-first retargeting and reprojection of wider scene views. The learning interface introduces a hierarchical policy in which a contact-surface planner predicts relative contact-surface trajectories and a controller coordinates wrist and finger commands to improve contact alignment. CIMI combines contact-grounded observations, retargeting, and action representations, improving mean final-stage success by at least 50 percentage points over baseline policies across six hand-task pairs on LEAP and Wuji. Hardware designs and software are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[STAR: Steering Action Representations during Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41009)**  `arXiv:2610.41009`  
  _Guangyu Zhao, Yongchang Zhang, Haoxuan Ru, Kaichen He, Haoran Wen, Kun Zhan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action models (VLAs) map language instructions and visual observations to robot actions, showing strong capabilities across robotic manipulation tasks. However, in our probing experiment, we find that action representations near the action output do not fully preserve task-relevant visual grounding. Motivated by this late-layer grounding gap, we introduce STAR, for STeering Action Representations, a training-free inference method for pre-trained VLAs. STAR applies an exponential moving average over late-layer residual updates, allowing later action representations to inherit object-aware update directions from earlier layers without model updates or architectural changes. It further uses recent proprioceptive states to adapt the mixing strength, using conservative mixing when execution dynamics change rapidly. Experiments on robot manipulation benchmarks show that STAR improves late-layer object awareness and increases average task success rates by 4.0 percentage points. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FlapSim: A Composable MuJoCo Simulation Platform and Reduced-Order Aerodynamic Model Hierarchy for Flapping-Wing Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40902)**  `arXiv:2610.40902`  
  _Mingshan He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flapping-wing simulation must reconcile high-frequency wingbeat aerodynamics with the slower dynamics relevant to flight control and reinforcement learning. We present FlapSim, a modular open-source MuJoCo simulation platform that combines robot descriptions, wing geometry, and composable aerodynamic models in a deterministic simulation pipeline. Across $48$ held-out scenarios spanning frequency, gimbal, wind, frequency-step, gust, and cross-robot flat-plate regimes, we compare five reduced-order configurations operating at $1\,\text{ms}$ with a $50\,\mu\text{s}$ quasi-steady blade-element reference. The built-in hover cycle-averaged baseline achieves a $67.51\times$ CPU speedup but only $0.6593$ mean wrench fidelity. Combining multirate $21$-substep clock synchronization, three-dimensional linear/quadratic inflow and gust coupling, analytical blade-element geometry scaling, and phase-locked $1f\text{--}6f$ harmonics raises mean fidelity to $0.9646$ at a $65.19\times$ speedup in a five-block model. A streamlined four-block model without explicit step-transient lag states achieves $0.9662$ fidelity at a $67.98\times$ speedup. Controlled ablations show that stroke harmonics are particularly important during frequency transitions, improving held-out frequency-step fidelity by $+0.3176$. Transfer-function analysis explains this effect: fixed-window cycle averaging retains harmonic contributions when the wingbeat frequency changes, so within-cycle reconstruction also improves the filtered wrench. Once phase alignment and harmonic synthesis are included, explicit transient lag states provide no additional benefit in this benchmark. These results characterize the accuracy--computation tradeoff of the aerodynamic hierarchy and identify timing, inflow, geometry, and harmonic representation as central considerations for reduced-order flapping-wing simulation.
  </details>

- **[CaPtain: A Pipeline for Post-Training Code-as-Policy Robot Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40799)**  `arXiv:2610.40799`  
  _Yijia Fan, Xilin He, Yilei Jiang, Dongming Wu, Jiaxu Wang, Xiangyu Yue_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Across coding, web, and tool-use tasks, language-model agents are now routinely trained with the same recipe: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to learn the interface, then multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL) to master it. Code-as-policy (CaP) casts robot manipulation in the same form, as the policy writes code against robot APIs and reads back the results, so the same recipe should apply. Yet existing CaP agents sidestep training: they either prompt closed frontier models or delegate manipulation to pre-trained vision-language-action models (VLAs) and high-level skills, while small open vision-language models (VLMs) fail outright. We present CaPtain, an open post-training pipeline that turns a small open VLM into a capable CaP agent, with no VLA, frontier model, or privileged state at test time. CaPtain trains and deploys the policy inside CaPtain-Harness, which exposes low-level perception, geometry, and guarded-motion primitives across simulation and real hardware. We first align the base model via SFT on synthetic trajectories generated with GPT-6 Astra, then apply multi-turn RL. Multi-turn RL must credit tens of agent requests, whose physical effects often surface turns later, from a single end-of-episode outcome. We therefore introduce a physical critic that reads privileged simulator state, never seen by the policy, and credits each request by how much it changes the predicted chance of success. Starting from Qwen3.5-4B (0% zero-shot), CaPtain-4B reaches 55.4% held-out LIBERO success after SFT and 73.8% after RL. On LIBERO-PRO, it reaches 78.5%, surpassing several frontier-model agents, and its RL gains persist across three perturbation suites (+17.6 to +21.2 points). Without target-domain training, it attains 83.3% on SimplerEnv WidowX, exceeds frontier models on CaP-Bench, and runs on a real robot after simulation-only adaptation. We release all code, data, and model weights.
  </details>

- **[WorldBridge: Bridging Video and Action for Task Generalization in World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40748)**  `arXiv:2610.40748`  
  _Jun Cen, Yubo Guo, Hao Li, Liangkai Li, Yaoxu Lyu, Meng Wei, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Achieving the broad task generalization of embodied models in physical environments remains a central challenge in robot learning. We study this challenge in World Action Models (WAMs) through two complementary capabilities: video generalization, which predicts the visual evolution of new tasks, and action generalization, which translates these predictions into executable behavior through a video-to-action bridge. By varying video and action supervision for tasks outside an initial training set, we examine their respective contributions to task transfer. On RoboTwin 2.0, using only in-distribution video-action supervision yields a 25.07% success rate on out-of-distribution tasks. Introducing videos of these tasks without their action labels raises success to 55.90%, while adding just one action-labeled demonstration per task further improves it to 70.70%. These findings suggest that broader video coverage enables task transfer by leveraging existing inverse-dynamics capabilities, while even sparse action supervision further strengthens those capabilities. Guided by this perspective, we introduce WorldBridge, a WAM with separate video and action experts connected through structured attention and trained with joint flow matching. WorldBridge incorporates visual memory at two temporal scales and action forcing, alongside a unified data processing pipeline that standardizes and filters heterogeneous robot trajectories and egocentric hand-derived supervision. Across multiple manipulation benchmarks, WorldBridge achieves state-of-the-art performance among the compared methods, including 96.23% on RoboTwin 2.0 full data setting and 80.9% on clean to random setting, 82.8% on RoboCasa GR1 TableTop, and 57.6% on DOMINO Level-1. Our study highlights the complementary roles of video prediction and action grounding in developing robots that generalize across tasks.
  </details>

- **[MUSBRIC: LEGO-Compatible Pneumatic Muscle Bricks for Reconfigurable Musculoskeletal Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40285)**  `arXiv:2610.40285`  
  _Qiyao Wang, Yinglei Zhu, Liang Zhou, Wenzhe Gong, Xin-Jun Liu, Huichan Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Musculoskeletal robots combine compliant actuators with articulated skeletons, but exploring alternative configurations often requires repeated fabrication. We present MUSBRIC, a modular hardware platform and accompanying MuJoCo-based simulator for reconfigurable musculoskeletal robots. LEGO-compatible endcaps turn fiber-reinforced extensile pneumatic artificial muscles into reusable, plug-and-play muscle bricks that connect directly to standard LEGO components. Experimentally identified actuator behavior supports corresponding musculoskeletal simulations. An elbow-joint prototype validates load-dependent flexion and extension, showing that pulley-guided routing enables reversible motion and a mechanical stop stabilizes the straight posture. A robotic hand demonstrates compliant grasping and multi-joint construction. Matched hardware and simulation sweeps across 15 leg configurations demonstrate systematic exploration of attachment geometry, while a quadruped assembled from four legs demonstrates walking. Together, these demonstrations span mechanism analysis, configuration exploration, and functional robot construction within a single reconfigurable system. The hardware designs and simulation code will be released as open source soon to facilitate reproduction and further development.
  </details>

- **[LOGIV: Graph-Verified Execution with Causal Local Repair for Long-Horizon Robot Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40016)**  `arXiv:2610.40016`  
  _Enqiao Lu, Yuxiang Xie, Xingrui Yu, Yuyang Wang, Yipeng Gu, Zhirui Fang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic robots must turn a high-level goal into actions while keeping the physical conditions needed for future steps valid. A completed action does not ensure that its effects will persist: a later disturbance can invalidate support that the remaining plan still assumes. We present LOGIV, a graph engineering framework for agentic robot execution that uses an explicit task graph to connect symbolic planning, observation-based verification, and embodied action. LOGIV compiles a validated plan into condition-support and resource-ordering edges, checks their physical validity during execution, and uses dependency tracing to determine what must be restored after a failure. Repair is constrained by retained unfinished actions, and the combined continuation is symbolically validated before execution resumes. The framework operates above existing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) and World Action Model (WAM) policies without adding trainable components. Across 20 dual-arm tasks from RoboTwin V2 and BiCoord with three execution backbones, LOGIV improves mean task success without injected disturbances by 10.67 and 15.33 percentage points, respectively. Under predefined perturbations, it achieves higher task completion and condition restoration than three adapted recovery baselines for every backbone on both benchmarks. On three real-robot tasks, successful trials increase from 12/30 to 17/30. These results support explicit, continuously checked task dependencies as a practical interface for reliable agentic robot execution.
  </details>

- **[OpenFlyV2: An onboard vision-language-action model for aerial perception and navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39287)**  `arXiv:2610.39287`  
  _Junli Liu, Zhongrui You, Chenhui Li, Zhen Li, Yuyang Tian, Qizhen Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human cognition flexibly draws on a shared perceptual foundation to understand environments, attend to referenced entities, reason about spatial relationships, and act. Endowing aerial robots with an analogous functional organization remains challenging, as existing systems are often specialized for individual capabilities, dependent on offboard computation, or unable to sustain closed-loop operation on resource-constrained platforms. We present OpenFlyV2, a unified onboard vision-language-action architecture that uses a shared vision-language representation to support semantic understanding, language-grounded perception, spatial reasoning, and continuous aerial control within a single model. A compact action expert converts the shared multimodal context into short-horizon flight waypoints, while motion-aware temporal memory and action priors reduce the computational cost of closed-loop execution. We construct \dataset{}, a simulation-based dataset comprising approximately 70,000 navigation trajectories and 60,000 vision-language instruction--response pairs. OpenFlyV2 generates action chunks, each comprising 12 waypoints, entirely onboard at 10 Hz using an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX. It transfers zero-shot from simulation to physical quadrotor flight, achieving a 48\% navigation success rate across four real-world environments without real-world aerial training data. Extensive results demonstrate that broad vision-language competence and continuous closed-loop flight can coexist within a single model on a resource-constrained aerial platform. Code will be made publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Actuator-Aware Observer Inputs for Cascade ADRC of Quadrotors Under Rotor Lag, Measurement Delay and Sensor Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39126)**  `arXiv:2610.39126`  
  _Xuan-Quy Dao, Ngoc-Bich Le, Le-Minh Nguyen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In sampled cascade linear active disturbance rejection control (LADRC) of quadrotors, the extended-state observer typically receives the instantaneous command, although the delayed measurement reflects an earlier, rotor-filtered acceleration. This paper proposes actuator-aware (AA) observer inputs: altitude and attitude observers use limited virtual inputs filtered by an assumed rotor model and aligned with the measurement delay, and horizontal observers use acceleration reconstructed from measured attitude. Fixed gains are selected on the discrete-time model under sensitivity and step-response requirements and stationary covariance ceilings on each channel's control variance and increment and on the noise-induced inner-loop increment of the full cascade. In paired set-point trials, PID-Budget, AA-Budget and AA-HalfGain reach mean RMSEs of 65.5, 19.2 and 16.9~mm; under a stricter common cascade ceiling, the AA designs reach 29.9 and 26.4~mm versus 72.3~mm for a PID that uses roughly double their rotor-command increment. PX4 software-in-the-loop runs reproduce these results. Behind EKF2, a noise model fitted to the estimation errors predicts the roll/pitch and yaw increments within 5\% on held-out and out-of-sample runs and the total rotor-command increment within 14\%. The controllers were then redesigned for this stack under the same sensitivity and response specification and estimator-aware ceilings. AA-Budget-E reduces the set-point RMSE from 59.1~mm (PID-E) to 44.0~mm with a lower rotor-command increment in every paired set-point and lemniscate run (mean S6 reduction: 45\% before and 15\% after output quantization); AA-HalfGain-E beats PID-E only on the lemniscate. These results indicate that the observer input and the outer-to-inner noise path are design variables of sampled cascade LADRC. The evidence is simulation-based, and the ceilings are nominal tuning constraints.
  </details>

- **[SimRetract: Zero-Shot Sim-to-Real Robotic Assistance for Retractor Take-Over in Open Surgery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38234)**  `arXiv:2610.38234`  
  _Xucheng Wang, Zhizhou Yang, Sung Eun Kim, Romain Hardy, Michael P. Sampson, Aakash Reddy, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We demonstrate the first zero-shot sim-to-real transfer for a surgeon-robot collaboration task in open surgery: retractor take-over. A robot arm takes a retractor from the surgeon's hand and preserves retraction at handoff, using a policy trained only on simulated demonstrations, without human demonstrations or real-world fine-tuning. Imitation learning from real demonstrations is limited by collection and labeling costs, and by performance degradation when the environment changes. Our framework, SimRetract, generates simulated demonstrations designed for transfer: the policy observes masked instrument images and grasp geometry, learns to recover from off-nominal states, and acts with margin for execution drift. Deployed zero-shot, it grasps the retractor in 90% of 50 surgeon-defined phantom presentations and preserves retraction at handoff in 78%. Results on unseen retractors suggest potential for generalization to instruments with similar grasp geometry. Across ten policy configurations and 1,200 simulated and real trials, we localize the sim-to-real gap to components: in simulation, grasp rates across ablations span 80-96%, while on the real robot removing the instrument mask alone drops performance to 22% grasped and 12% held. Transfer-oriented design thus makes zero-shot sim-to-real transfer feasible for a surgical assistance task without human demonstrations. Code is available here:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[PDRVLA: A Vision-Language-Action Model with Physical Dynamics Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37381)**  `arXiv:2610.37381`  
  _Hanqing Wang, Zhaotian Cai, Ruichuan An, Bohan Zeng, Tao Zhang, Yilin Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision--Language--Action (VLA) models generate robot actions from visual observations and language instructions, but their understanding of physical dynamics remains limited. Video world models can capture scene dynamics, yet generating future videos introduces high latency into the control loop. We introduce PDRVLA, whose lightweight Physical Dynamics Module extracts physical tokens from vision--language features to condition action generation without online video generation. The module learns through direct prediction of future RGB, depth, and optical-flow features, with auxiliary supervision through a frozen pretrained video world model. Training relies entirely on publicly available data, expanded through human-to-robot synthesis, subtask annotation, and embodied chain-of-thought (ECoT) annotation. Across four simulation benchmarks, PDRVLA achieves success rates of 99.3% on LIBERO, 89.9% on LIBERO-Plus, 93.6% on RoboTwin~2.0, and 81.5% on SimplerEnv. Ablations show improved future prediction with world-model supervision and evaluate how subtask conditioning, future object-pose supervision, and ECoT training affect action success.
  </details>

- **[MOON: Learning When to Forget and How to Move On in Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33546)**  `arXiv:2610.33546`  
  _Jingmeng Zhou, Seson Sun, Binyao Xu, Lingming Zhang, Hanshi Wang, Fudong Ge, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent vision-and-language navigation (VLN) policies act on their visual history, and an incorrect action can take them off the instructed route. Paired interventions on four VLN policies show that clearing the history doubles pooled success at the onset of a deviation but lowers it on route, while later in a deviation, removing only the off-route frames helps much less. After a deviation, the retained history keeps the policy from turning where a turn is needed, which we call behavioral inertia. We propose MOON, a framework that equips a frozen VLN policy with two lightweight modules, adding less than 1% of its parameters. A deviation judge decides when to forget, raising an alarm that clears the history when it predicts that the agent is off route. A recovery policy, trained with GRPO from alarm states, learns how to move on, turning more of the successes found only by sampling into a single greedy rollout. Experiments on R2R-CE and RxR-CE show that MOON reaches the highest R2R-CE success rate among the compared methods and raises the success rate of its frozen base by 5.2 and 6.0 points, and an ablation with a random trigger indicates that this gain depends on when the history is cleared. Without retraining, MOON also improves zero-shot object-goal navigation on HM3D-OVON. Together, these results suggest that deviation recovery depends on knowing when to forget, not only how to act. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Do Earth-Trained Humanoid Controllers Walk on the Moon? Gravity, Terrain, and Soil Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30080)**  `arXiv:2610.30080`  
  _Guillem Casadesus Vila, Sri Aditya Deevi, Grace X. Gao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoid robots could aid lunar operations, but how to train their locomotion controllers for lunar gravity, terrain, and soil is unclear. We compare 12 strategies for a simulated Unitree G1 across gravity, terrain, and rigid or granular 3D resistive force theory (3D RFT) contact. Earth-trained policies keep 0.64 to 0.75 survival at the Moon on flat rigid ground in 10 s episodes, but falls continue, so longer episodes would see lower survival. Moon-trained and fine-tuned policies, in contrast, fall in every Earth episode, nearly all within 2 s. Rough-terrain training helps on uneven rigid ground only at training gravity. 3D RFT soil training helps on Earth sand but little on lunar soil. On three held-out soil models, Moon-trained policies lose no detectable progress at lunar gravity. Among tested strategies, only gravity-randomized rough 3D RFT training keeps mean survival above 0.83 at Earth and 0.95 at the Moon on all key tests up to 20 deg. Its ablation without soil training differs consistently only on soil at Earth gravity, falling in most sand episodes. These predictions depend on the contact model and soil stiffness, so lunar soil claims require agreement across soil models.
  </details>

- **[Learning from Fleet Experience: Collective Experience Learning Across 96 Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25736)**  `arXiv:2610.25736`  
  _Chao Yu, Shu'ang Yu, Hao Lin, Hongzhi Zang, Yuxiang Yan, Chenxi Jiang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot deployment generates experience that can support policy improvement, but continued reliance on expert corrective actions makes learning difficult to scale across growing fleets. We propose learning from fleet experience: using deployed robots' own executions as a source of supervision for further policy improvement. We call this paradigm Collective Experience Learning (CEL), in which robots pool execution experience to update a shared policy that returns to the fleet. Following expert-supervised initialization, we realize CEL through failure-guided experience retrieval fine-tuning (FERT). FERT uses failure contexts to retrieve relevant segments from successful trajectories and prioritizes their recorded actions for supervised updates, without collecting additional expert demonstrations or corrective actions. Operators provide binary episode-outcome labels and environment resets. A fault-tolerant asynchronous training system (FATS) coordinates robot execution, cloud inference and policy optimization despite local interruptions. Across five real-world manipulation tasks, the resulting policies achieve success rates of 93.3-99.2%, evaluated over 120 trials per task on robots excluded from training data collection. With collection time per robot held fixed, larger fleets provide more experience and generally improve performance across the evaluated sizes. On multi-stage phone packaging, learning with 96 robots raises success from 79.2% to 98.3%. These results support CEL as a scalable approach to improving robot policy reliability through experience generated during deployment.
  </details>

- **[TacClaw-Infinity: A Large-Scale Vision-Tactile-Language-Action Dataset for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20320)**  `arXiv:2610.20320`  
  _Fucai Zhu, Xidong Zhang, Yichi Zhang, Yipan Wang, Fubo Wang, Zibin Wu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale robot learning increasingly relies on diverse human demonstrations, yet tactile manipulation data remain difficult to scale, and existing multimodal datasets often lack consistent quality control and structured supervision. We present TacClaw-Infinity, a large-scale structured Vision-Tactile-Language-Action (VTLA) dataset for contact-rich manipulation, collected with TacClaw, a tactile-enabled UMI-style handheld interface that records synchronized visual observations, fingertip tactile signals, and end-effector trajectories without requiring a target robot during data collection. TacClaw-Infinity is built through a distributed worldwide collection across homes, laboratories, industrial workplaces, retail stores, and outdoor environments including tasks contributed by academic and industrial collaborators. Together, these efforts yield over 10,000 hours, 1 million episodes, and 1 billion frames spanning more than 1,000 task types. We develop a scalable pipeline for trajectory reconstruction, multimodal quality control, embodiment-aware filtering, and structured annotation, covering contact-centric segmentation, subtask description, and spatial grounding. We validate TacClaw-Infinity at both the data and model levels: At the data level, we assess the collection interface, the action-space compatibility of the curated demonstrations, and the structured annotations, while for the model level, we demonstrate cross-embodiment transfer across three heterogeneous robots without robot-specific demonstrations.
  </details>

- **[I3L: Iterative Interactive Imitation Learning for Whole-Body Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17748)**  `arXiv:2610.17748`  
  _Wensi Ai, Christopher Luey, Karthik Dharmarajan, Evans Han, Yunfan Jiang, Stef Ren, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Policies trained offline from teleoperated demonstrations can degrade outside their training distribution. This gap is particularly acute in long-horizon whole-body mobile manipulation, where long horizons amplify compounding errors and coordinated arm, torso, and base motion makes exhaustive demonstration coverage impractical. We introduce I3L, an integrated hardware and learning system that combines whole-body correction with iterative policy refinement to address this last-mile reliability gap. Its low-cost, open-source JoyLo+ interface uses policy-tracking leader arms and touch-triggered takeover to enable coordinated corrections across the body parts, while iterative deployment and retraining allow operators to target failures of the updated policy. We evaluate I3L across three robot embodiments and eight simulated and real-world tasks, including four long-horizon whole-body mobile manipulation tasks. Controlled simulation experiments show that four-round HG-DAgger achieves the highest final success on both tasks. Under this protocol, I3L improves autonomous success over the base policies by an average of 68.3 percentage points and achieves at least 90% success on every task, outperforming demonstration-only training by 35.6 percentage points on average with the same number of training trajectories. Averaged across tasks, takeover duration per episode decreases by 72% over four rounds. Together, these results show that I3L helps close the last-mile gap between offline training and reliable deployment and provides practical insights into collecting and using corrections for long-horizon whole-body mobile manipulation. Videos, visualizations, and appendix are atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Detect and Suppress: A Mechanistic Defense against Adversarial Patches in VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03498)**  `arXiv:2610.03498`  
  _Yukiya Horiba, Koshiro Aoki, Shunsuke Yasuki, Bum Jun Kim, Taiki Miyanishi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adversarial patches can disrupt Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models by manipulating visual observations, leading to failures in robot control. However, it remains poorly understood which internal mechanisms underlie these failures and how targeted interventions can mitigate them. In this work, we mechanistically analyze VLA representations using a sparse autoencoder (SAE) and identify a feature whose activation strongly correlates with the presence of an adversarial patch. Based on this analysis, we suppress the identified feature at inference time only when a linear probe detects an attack. This intervention improves robustness without the cost of fine-tuning the VLA. We evaluate our method against VLA adversarial patch attacks on LIBERO-10. Conditional intervention improves success rate under intermittent attacks, whereas continuously applying the same intervention substantially degrades policy performance. These results show that attack-related internal representations can provide useful targets for VLA adversarial defense and that controlling when to intervene is important for limiting disruption to nominal policy behavior.
  </details>

- **[Denoising Multirobot Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35651)**  `arXiv:2609.35651`  
  _Yuhao Zhang, Keisuke Okumura, Ajay Shankar, Amanda Prorok_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-robot trajectory planning is a fundamental problem in multi-robot coordination but remains computationally challenging due to its nonconvex, multimodal, and high-dimensional nature. This work builds upon D4orm, a dynamics-aware diffusion-denoising framework, and develops a family of planning architectures for diverse operational requirements. Unlike conventional numerical optimization methods, D4orm employs sampling-based optimization to generate solution trajectories through massively parallel sampling, leveraging modern computing architectures such as GPUs. Its diffusion-denoising structure iteratively optimizes deformations to candidate control trajectories, providing an efficient and versatile paradigm for generating kinodynamically feasible and conflict-free trajectories. Using D4orm as the building block for advanced planners, we present a decoupled planner for improved scalability, an online receding-horizon planner with feedback control, and a distributed planner for resource-constrained settings. Evaluations with differential-drive and holonomic robots in 2D and 3D environments demonstrate that D4orm-based approaches find high-quality solutions faster and more reliably than other sampling-based optimization methods, such as MPPI, as well as a learned diffusion-model-based method. We further demonstrate zero-shot deployment on ten real quadrotors with obstacles, large-scale deconfliction with 100 simulated robots, and fully onboard distributed `lifelong' operation with six ground robots. Overall, these results establish diffusion denoising as a scalable and reliable framework for multi-robot coordination. Code and video:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Compressed delayed-information projection for underwater navigation under acoustic measurement latency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27439)**  `arXiv:2609.27439`  
  _Shuyue Li, Miguel López-Benítez, Eng Gee Lim, Fei Ma, Qian Dong, Mengze Cao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Delayed acoustic position measurements describe past states but may reach an underwater vehicle after its navigation estimate has advanced. This paper presents compressed delayed-information projection (CDIP), a causal delayed-update method implemented in a 15-dimensional error-state Kalman filter (ESKF). CDIP retains source-associated inertial measurement unit (IMU) snapshots and maintains current-to-snapshot cross-covariances, projecting delayed corrections without repeated full-from-start filtering. In 154 paired simulation recordings with synthetic horizontal-position fixes delayed by 1.5~s, CDIP reduced mean trajectory-position root-mean-square error (RMSE) from 1.062~m for a reception-time Baseline to 0.456~m (57.1\%). Its mean RMSE approached that of full-from-start Replay (0.451~m), with 99.2\% lower mean core-loop time per processed IMU step for the tested implementations. A six-delay sweep, scheduled permanent-loss outages of 50--300~s, and packet-erasure tests further characterized sensitivity to measurement timing and availability. A separate 256-trial nonlinear synthetic assessment identified departures from the prespecified calibration criterion for CDIP and two fully augmented ESKF references. Together, these results demonstrate an accuracy--computation trade-off for causal underwater navigation under the evaluated delayed-measurement simulation conditions.
  </details>

- **[Learning and Transferring Closed-Loop Robot Software](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19906)**  `arXiv:2609.19906`  
  _So Kuroki, Kento Kawaharazuka, Yujin Tang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Closed-loop robot policies require observation processing, state management, and situation-dependent branching, making them costly to design and tune manually. Although coding agents increasingly support control-code generation and optimization, it remains unclear whether implementations improved on source tasks also support policy acquisition for new tasks. We study this question by treating complete closed-loop implementations as reusable execution experience. To learn each source policy, a coding agent generates policy code from a few successful demonstrations and iteratively improves it using simulation feedback. The validation-selected implementations are retained in a software archive. To transfer the acquired software to new tasks, the agent generates and improves policies using archived implementations, target demonstrations, and execution feedback. We evaluate policy learning and its relationship to cross-task transfer. For learning, across four source tasks in RoboCasa, iterative optimization increases the success rate from 28.3% with initially generated policy code to 64.2% with validation-selected policy code. We further validate this learning approach on a physical Unitree G1. For transfer, across nine additional target tasks in RoboCasa, success rates are 45.2% without references, 41.5% using initially generated policy code from source tasks, and 57.0% using their validation-selected policy code. These results show that improving source-task code also improves learning on new tasks.
  </details>

- **[Keeping the Franka Emika Panda alive: a ROS 2 stack with a reliable position interface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19740)**  `arXiv:2608.19740`  
  _Antonio Langella, Davide Risi, Vincenzo Petrone, Enrico Ferrentino, Pasquale Chiacchio_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents an open-source software stack that restores ROS 2 support for the Franka Emika Panda robot while resolving the long-standing unreliability of its external position control interface. We first analyze the root causes of unstable position control and show that the observed vibrations and protective stops arise from the timing of the external control loop and sampling jitter, rather than from limitations of the robot itself. Building on this analysis, we introduce an asynchronous hardware interface that decouples real-time communication from the ROS 2 control loop, a rate-matching mechanism for slower command sources, and a position-domain reference generation strategy that produces reliable, smooth position commands. Experimental validation shows that the proposed architecture reliably tracks velocity references by reducing motion artifacts introduced by the official implementation, and the stack is validated across motion planning, compliance control, position-controlled manipulation, and haptic teleoperation on two independent Panda platforms. By restoring a modern, reliable, and open ROS 2 ecosystem for the Panda, this work lowers the barrier to developing safe, responsive, and reproducible human-robot collaboration applications that integrate planning, perception, interaction, and shared autonomy. Code and videos are available on our website atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive-WAM: Quality-Guided Early-Exit Planning from Intermediate Video-Diffusion Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06008)**  `arXiv:2608.06008`  
  _Sining Ang, Yuguang Yang, Yan Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large video diffusion models provide rich spatiotemporal priors for autonomous driving, but existing world-action models often inherit the cost of iterative future-video generation even though deployment only requires an ego trajectory. We ask a more basic question: how much of a video diffusion model must be executed to make a reliable driving decision? Through a controlled study of video denoising timesteps and Diffusion Transformer (DiT) depth, we find that planning performance is largely insensitive to the tested video-noise levels, whereas strong trajectories can already be decoded from intermediate layers. Based on this observation, we introduce Adaptive-WAM, a quality-aware multi-exit planner built on a Wan2.2-5B backbone. Trajectory diffusion heads are attached to selected DiT blocks, and a lightweight trajectory-quality scorer terminates inference once the best trajectory decoded so far satisfies a quality threshold; otherwise, computation continues from the cached hidden state to a deeper exit. The deployed planner therefore avoids the iterative classifier-free denoising loop and VAE decoding required for future-video synthesis, while dynamically allocating backbone depth according to trajectory quality. On NAVSIM, the adaptive single-trajectory planner achieves 90.8 PDMS; a separate fixed-exit variant reaches 92.6 PDMS with 64 proposals. It further obtains 89.9 EPDMS on NAVSIM v2, yielding the best reported results among the compared front-view video world-model planners. Without target-domain fine-tuning, Adaptive-WAM transfers to nuScenes with 0.88 m average L2 error and a 0.08\% collision rate. On an A100, adaptive routing improves PDMS from 90.62 to 90.79 while averaging 170 ms end-to-end planning latency, approximately 10\% below the 190 ms fixed block-15 planner and 47\% below the 320 ms fixed full-depth planner. Code will be released.
  </details>

- **[UniCross: Unified Cross-Skill Dexterous Manipulation Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28198)**  `arXiv:2607.28198`  
  _Hui Zhang, Julian Ferchow, Jie Song, Mirko Meboldt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many dexterous manipulation tasks require the object to remain securely held throughout the interaction. From the perspective of hand-object relational motion, such manipulation comprises four canonical skills: grasping, relocation, in-hand rotation, and in-hand translation. Human hands flexibly compose these skills to accomplish complex tasks. Existing approaches, however, model these skills separately with skill-specific action constraints, objectives, or even dedicated hand morphologies, which breaks the compatibility and continuity required for long-horizon composition. In this work, we present a unified framework that models all four skills in a single formulation instantiated with desired relational targets. The formulation preserves cross-skill compatibility and enables distillation of a single policy without skill identifiers, which achieves strong per-skill performance and generalizes to unseen objects. The compatibility further supports the policy to perform long-horizon skill chaining and execute relational targets unseen during training. The framework also applies across different hand morphologies. Together, these results suggest that distinct dexterous manipulation skills can be modeled as instantiations of a shared relational task formulation. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Decompose and Reorganize: Planning with Primitives and Visuomotor Policies Learned from Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25397)**  `arXiv:2607.25397`  
  _Yizhou Chen, Hang Xu, Dongjie Yu, Yupu Lu, Tengye Xu, Zeqing Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Successfully automating dexterous, long-horizon bimanual manipulation requires frameworks capable of both high-level reasoning and fine-grained execution. Traditional task and motion planning (TAMP), while excellent at symbolic planning, is often brittle in contact-rich operations. Simultaneously, imitation learning (IL), while effective in manipulation tasks with visual feedback, is limited by its low capability in spatial generalization and multi-stage operation. To reconcile their complementary strengths and limitations, we propose DR-LfD (Decomposed and Reorganized Skills Learned from Demonstrations), a framework that integrates visuomotor policies into a TAMP-gated decision-making system. Based on contact relationships, DR-LfD decomposes human demonstrations into atomic skills, which are reproduced as visuomotor policies or object-centric primitives. The initiation, termination, and constraints of the visuomotor policies are carefully modeled and implemented in a TAMP-compatible form, enabling reorganization of skills learned from different sources. DR-LfD transforms the learning problem from one requiring exponential demonstration data over possible skill sequences to one whose demonstration burden scales with the number of distinct skill types, with limited data for each skill. Real-world and simulation benchmarks demonstrate data-efficient skill composition under unseen setups and physical constraints. On two DexMimicGen tasks, DR-LfD with 100 demonstrations outperforms diffusion-based imitation policies with 1000.
  </details>

- **[More than a Manipulator: Planning Propellant-Free Attitude Maneuvers for Free-Floating Spacecraft](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12130)**  `arXiv:2607.12130`  
  _Harsh G. Bhundiya, Avi Soval, Keenan Albee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spacecraft attitude control is traditionally achieved using momentum exchange devices or propellant-consuming thrusters. Meanwhile, a growing number of missions require robotic manipulators, which are typically treated as disturbance sources to be rejected rather than as actuators for spacecraft reorientation. This work investigates the use of manipulator motions for propellant-free attitude control by formulating a trajectory optimization problem with critical joint and collision avoidance constraints. Using an interior-point solver for the resulting nonlinear program, complex slew and detumble trajectories are computed for a range of spacecraft-manipulator systems, demonstrating rest-to-rest slew maneuvers of up to 90$^\circ$ and detumble maneuvers with a 40% reduction in angular velocity. The achievable control authority is also compared directly with that of reaction wheel arrays via momentum and torque envelopes, demonstrating the potential for manipulators to serve as redundant or even primary attitude control systems. This work provides a framework for using manipulators as multipurpose attitude control actuators, with particularly promising applications in in-space assembly and manufacturing when grasping payloads with high relative mass fractions.
  </details>

- **[Sequential Planning via Anchored Robotic Keypoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30613)**  `arXiv:2606.30613`  
  _Bryce Grant, Aryeh Rothenberg, Logan Senning, Zonghe Chua, Zach Patterson, Peng Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Changes to what a robot can see do not necessarily mean that there is a change in the problem it is trying to solve, and many language-conditioned manipulation systems struggle with this distinction. When something in a scene moves, the robot might need to execute its actions in different places, but its manipulation plan can stay the same. We propose Sequential Planning via Anchored Robotic Keypoints (\spark), which describes a task as a symbolic behavior tree that we only anchor to the current 3D observations when each action is called. By verifying the plan at runtime we can recover from failures locally and anchor the objects to the scene again. We ask for a new plan only if this fails. We isolate this concept by taking a fixed plan and moving objects in the scene while executing. When displacing objects by $10$\,cm we were still able to complete $66.7\%$ of trials with scene-diff verification and layered recovery, and we did not have to change the plan. We test our full system on \libpro with these conditions. There is no practice on the tasks being tested and no access to simulator states during rollouts, and as in \rats and \capx a success check after each attempt decides whether to retry. We use one open-weight 27B planner with 4-bit weights. Under this protocol \spark achieves a mean success of $66.8\%$ across the 6 position and task settings and $66.8\%$ on the held-out language and object settings. We also achieve a $68\%$ success rate across 11 physical task-embodiment settings on 3 different robots. Our work demonstrates that for an important class of failures the robot does not need to ask for a new plan for its task. It can be enough to update the system's view of the world. Code and videos can be found atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[VEGA: Learning Navigation VLAs from In-the-Wild Egocentric Video with Geometric Trajectory Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18426)**  `arXiv:2606.18426`  
  _Gershom Seneviratne, Yohan Abeysinghe, Jianyu An, Vaibhav Shende, Rahul Kumar, Dinesh Manocha_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce VEGA, an approach for training navigation VisionLanguage-Action (VLA) models from unlabeled egocentric navigation videos. Internet-scale egocentric videos provide a scalable source of navigation-relevant visual observations, capturing cluttered scenes, close-range obstacles, and natural human motion through real-world spaces. However, these videos are not directly usable for policy learning because they do not provide obstacle-aware trajectories conditioned on explicit navigation goals in the robot's coordinate frame. VEGA addresses this gap by reconstructing local scene geometry from monocular video, sampling navigation goals (represented as text, image, or spatial waypoints) and generating obstacle-aware trajectories using the constructed geometry. The resulting trajectory distribution is then used to train a flow-matching VLA navigation policy. By using geometry exclusively during training, VEGA distills obstacle-aware planning directly into a vision-based policy. Furthermore, we introduce VEGA-Bench, a benchmark containing 250k scenes and approximately 5 million navigation goals paired with scene geometry, designed to evaluate goal progress, collision avoidance, and obstacle clearance of VLAs. Our evaluation shows that VEGA achieves competitive goal progress while reducing collisions by 33.0% and improving obstacle clearance by 17.9% over the strongest baseline on VEGABench, while improving success by at least 150.0%, reducing collisions by at least 66.7%, and improving obstacle clearance by at least 60.0% in real-world trials. Ultimately, we demonstrate that video-derived geometric supervision provides a scalable and effective signal for training obstacle-aware navigation VLAs. The code and benchmark will be released at the time of publication.
  </details>

- **[Rapid co-design of Buoyancy-assisted robots for Challenging Locomotion using Gaussian Evolutionary Specialists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07424)**  `arXiv:2606.07424`  
  _Ankit Sinha, Nitish Sontakke, Dennis Hong, Yusuke Tanaka, Sehoon Ha_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Designing high-performance legged robots requires jointly optimizing morphology and control. Model-free reinforcement learning (RL) enables learning complex controllers during co-design, but repeatedly training policies across candidate morphologies makes design optimization computationally expensive. Existing approaches can amortize policy learning across morphologies, but a single shared policy tends to converge toward one control strategy regardless of morphology, which we refer to as behavioral diversity collapse. We introduce Gaussian Evolutionary Specialists (GES), an RL-based co-design framework that maintains a population of specialist policies whose design-space territories evolve according to their relative control performance. We evaluate GES on the Buoyancy-Assisted Light Legged Unit (BALLU) robot for obstacle traversal and ramp walking over design spaces of up to four dimensions. GES achieves higher best design performance than Monolithic and Mixture-of-Experts baselines, with gains of 5.0-9.0 cm in obstacle height and 10.0-12.7 points in ramp grade. For the same 200 design-optimization evaluations, GES reduces wall-clock co-design time by 67.4% compared with retraining a policy for every candidate. Finally, teleoperated hardware experiments evaluate the optimized BALLU morphology, which traverses obstacles of up to 24 cm, compared with 8 cm on the original design.
  </details>

- **[NDPP-Grasp: Non-Differentiable Physical Plausibility Constraint-Guided Task-Oriented Dexterous Grasp Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02432)**  `arXiv:2606.02432`  
  _Qiuchi Xiang, Haoxuan Qu, Hossein Rahmani, Jun Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Task-oriented dexterous grasp generation aims to produce dexterous grasp poses that are both physically plausible and functionally suitable for specified manipulation tasks. Existing diffusion-based methods often address these two requirements in a decoupled manner: they first train a grasp diffusion model for task alignment and then rely on post-generation refinement to improve physical plausibility. However, this after-the-fact correction strategy applies physical plausibility guidance only once the grasp has already been generated, leaving the generation trajectory itself unguided by physical constraints and potentially leading to suboptimal grasps. To address this problem, we propose a novel framework that directly injects physical plausibility guidance into the denoising process of a task-aligned grasp diffusion model in a practical and effective manner, even when physical plausibility constraints are non-differentiable. This allows physical plausibility to shape grasp generation throughout denoising while preserving task alignment. Extensive experiments demonstrate the efficacy of our framework.
  </details>

- **[TacO: Benchmarking Tactile Sensors for Object Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21976)**  `arXiv:2605.21976`  
  _Anya Zorin, Zilin Si, Sachin Bhadang, Myungsun Park, Junsung Park, Alexiy Buynitsky, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-based learning from demonstrations has achieved remarkable success in enabling robots to perform manipulation tasks and high-level semantic reasoning, yet it remains insufficient for complex, contact-rich manipulation. While there is broad agreement that tactile sensing improves manipulation, there is no empirical guidance on which tactile sensors are best suited for which manipulation tasks. In this paper, we provide a systematic, task-driven evaluation of tactile sensors for robot manipulation and propose a framework for selecting and evaluating sensors based on manipulation policy performance. Separate manipulation policies are trained for tactile sensors of four distinct modalities: visual, acoustic, magnetic, and resistive, across three tasks: pick-and-place with unknown mass, object reorientation, and plug insertion. For each task, we analyze how sensor properties, including spatial resolution, shear sensing, tactile representation, and inherent material friction, affect task performance. Rather than tactile sensing being universally beneficial in the same way, our results show that the usefulness of tactile information depends strongly on sensor modality, material properties, and the specific manipulation tasks. The tactile sensors, code, data, and hardware setup will be publicly available on the project website.
  </details>

- **[Semantically Adaptive Control Barrier Functions with Vision-Language Models for Real-Time Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21142)**  `arXiv:2603.21142`  
  _Jeffrey Chen, Rohan Chandra_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots operating in dynamic, unstructured environments must maintain safety without becoming unnecessarily conservative. While control barrier functions (CBFs) provide a principled mechanism for collision avoidance, their behavior is typically determined from geometric state alone and therefore cannot distinguish between semantically different situations with similar physical configurations. We present AlphaAdj, a vision-to-control navigation framework that uses egocentric RGB observations and a vision-language model (VLM) to adapt CBF conservativeness according to visual semantic context. The VLM produces a scalar contextual caution signal that dynamically modulates the CBF parameter alpha, while a geometry- and speed-dependent cap bounds admissible aggressiveness. To support real-time operation despite VLM latency, inference runs asynchronously with the control loop, and the semantic signal is extracted directly from token-level output probabilities rather than full autoregressive text generation. We evaluate AlphaAdj across matched-geometry, dynamic-human, and cluttered navigation scenarios, as well as on a physical Clearpath Jackal. When obstacle geometry is held fixed, AlphaAdj allocates 0.405 m more clearance to a human than to a benign object, while geometry-only and non-visual adaptive CBF baselines produce no semantic differentiation. This additional behavioral reserve improves robustness to unexpected human motion, including cluttered conditions in which all evaluated geometric baselines collide while AlphaAdj remains collision-free. The system maintains a 0.0167 s control period with 0.158 s mean VLM round-trip latency, demonstrating that semantic visual reasoning can complement geometric safety control without entering the critical control path.
  </details>

- **[Specification-Aware Distribution Shaping for Robotics Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17969)**  `arXiv:2603.17969`  
  _Sadık Bera Yüksel, Derya Aksaray_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotics foundation models have demonstrated strong capabilities in executing natural language instructions across diverse tasks and environments. However, they remain largely data-driven and lack formal guarantees on constraint satisfaction during deployment. In practice, robots often need to comply with operational constraints involving rich spatio-temporal requirements such as time-bounded goal visits, sequential objectives, and persistent safety conditions. In this work, we propose a specification-aware action distribution optimization framework that enforces a broad class of Signal Temporal Logic (STL) constraints during execution of a pretrained robotics foundation model without modifying its parameters. At each decision step, the method computes a minimally modified action distribution that satisfies a hard STL feasibility constraint by reasoning over the remaining horizon using forward dynamics propagation. We validate the proposed framework in simulation using state-of-the-art robotics foundation models across multiple environments and complex specifications.
  </details>

- **[FlowTouch: View-Invariant Visuo-Tactile Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08255)**  `arXiv:2603.08255`  
  _Seongjin Bien, Carlo Kneissl, Tobias Jülg, Frank Fundel, Thomas Ressler-Antal, Florian Walter, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tactile sensation is essential for contact-rich manipulation tasks. It provides direct feedback on object geometry, surface properties, and interaction forces, enhancing perception and enabling fine-grained control. An inherent limitation of tactile sensors is that readings are available only when an object is touched. This precludes their use during planning and the initial execution phase of a task. Predicting tactile information from visual information can bridge this gap. A common approach is to learn a direct mapping from camera images to the output of vision-based tactile sensors. However, the resulting model will depend strongly on the specific setup and on how well the camera can capture the area where an object is touched. In this work, we introduce FlowTouch, a novel model for view-invariant visuo-tactile prediction. Our key idea is to use an object's local 3D mesh to encode rich information for predicting tactile patterns while abstracting away from scene-dependent details. FlowTouch integrates scene reconstruction and Flow Matching-based models for image generation. Our results show that FlowTouch is able to bridge the sim-to-real gap and generalize to new sensor instances. We further show that the resulting tactile images can be used for downstream grasp stability prediction. Our code, datasets and videos are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[$π$-StepNFT: Wider Space Needs Finer Steps in Online RL for Flow-based VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02083)**  `arXiv:2603.02083`  
  _Siting Wang, Xiaofeng Wang, Zheng Zhu, Minnan Pei, Xinyu Cui, Cheng Deng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-based vision-language-action (VLA) models have emerged as a powerful paradigm for embodied control, but multi-step denoising makes policy-gradient online reinforcement learning (RL) difficult because the final-action likelihood induced by the sampler is not directly tractable. We identify an exploration--supervision mismatch within the denoising trajectory, where stochastic differential equation (SDE) sampling explores a wider local stochastic transition space while supervision derived from the final denoised output does not directly use the adjacent transitions observed during sampling. We propose $\pi$-StepNFT (Step-wise Negative-aware Fine-Tuning), a flow-native online RL framework that couples wider stochastic exploration with finer step-wise supervision without computing the final-action likelihood. $\pi$-StepNFT treats observed SDE transitions as supervision units, using variance-normalized transition errors to rank mirrored positive and negative velocity branches and map rollout feedback to local velocity updates. On LIBERO, starting from shared few-shot SFT initializations, $\pi$-StepNFT raises average success from 57.6\% to 92.0\% for $\pi_0$ and from 77.1\% to 94.0\% for $\pi_{0.5}$. On ManiSkill, it achieves the highest reported mean success among the evaluated RL baselines on five test-only visual perturbations. Our project homepage is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CHOP: Counterfactual Human Preference Labels Improve Obstacle Avoidance in Visuomotor Navigation Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02004)**  `arXiv:2603.02004`  
  _Gershom Seneviratne, Jianyu An, Vaibhav Shende, Sahire Ellahy, Yaxita Amin, Kondapi Manasanjani, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visuomotor navigation policies have shown strong perception-action coupling for embodied agents, yet they often struggle with safe navigation and dynamic obstacle avoidance in complex real-world environments. We introduce CHOP, a novel approach that leverages Counterfactual Human Preference Labels to align visuomotor navigation policies towards human intuition of safety and obstacle avoidance in navigation. In CHOP, for each visual observation, the robot's executed trajectory is included among a set of counterfactual navigation trajectories: alternative trajectories the robot could have followed under identical conditions. Human annotators provide pairwise preference labels over these trajectories based on anticipated outcomes such as collision risk and path efficiency. These aggregated preferences are then used to fine-tune visuomotor navigation policies, aligning their behavior with human preferences in navigation. Experiments on the SCAND dataset show that visuomotor navigation policies fine-tuned with CHOP reduce near-collision events by 49.7%, decrease deviation from human-preferred trajectories by 45.0%, and increase average obstacle clearance by 19.8% on average across multiple state-of-the-art models, compared to their pretrained baselines. These improvements transfer to real-world deployments on a Ghost Robotics Vision60 quadruped, where CHOP-aligned policies improve average goal success rates by 24.4%, increase minimum obstacle clearance by 6.8%, reduce collision and intervention events by 45.7%, and improve normalized path completion by 38.6% on average across navigation scenarios, compared to their pretrained baselines. Our results highlight the value of counterfactual preference supervision in bridging the gap between large-scale visuomotor policies and human-aligned, safety-aware embodied navigation.
  </details>

- **[WildOS: Open-Vocabulary Object Search in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.19308)**  `arXiv:2602.19308`  
  _Hardik Shah, Manthan Patel, Erica Tevere, Deegan Atha, Marcel Kaufmann, Shehryar Khattak, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous navigation in complex, unstructured outdoor environments requires robots to operate over long ranges without prior maps and limited depth sensing. In such settings, relying solely on geometric frontiers for exploration is often insufficient. In such settings, the ability to reason semantically about where to go and what is safe to traverse is crucial for robust, efficient exploration. This work presents WildOS, a unified system for long-range, open-vocabulary object search that combines safe geometric exploration with semantic visual reasoning. WildOS builds a sparse navigation graph to maintain spatial memory, while utilizing a foundation-model-based vision module, ExploRFM, to score frontier nodes of the graph. ExploRFM simultaneously predicts traversability, visual frontiers, and object similarity in image space, enabling real-time, onboard semantic navigation tasks. The resulting vision-scored graph enables the robot to explore semantically meaningful directions while ensuring geometric safety. Furthermore, we introduce a particle-filter-based method for coarse localization of the open-vocabulary target query, that estimates candidate goal positions beyond the robot's immediate depth horizon, enabling effective planning toward distant goals. Extensive closed-loop field experiments across diverse off-road and urban terrains demonstrate that WildOS enables robust navigation, significantly outperforming purely geometric and purely vision-based baselines in both efficiency and autonomy. Our results highlight the potential of vision foundation models to drive open-world robotic behaviors that are both semantically informed and geometrically grounded. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Reflection-Based Relative Localization for Cooperative UAV Teams Using Active Markers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17166)**  `arXiv:2511.17166`  
  _Tim Lakemann, Daniel Bonilla Licea, Viktor Walter, Martin Saska_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reflections of active markers in the environment are a common source of ambiguity in onboard visual relative localization. This work presents a novel approach that exploits these typically unwanted reflections for onboard relative localization in heterogeneous multi-UAV teams operating over specular, near-planar surfaces such as calm water and flat indoor floors. Using only a camera, active markers, and a single-beam rangefinder, the method requires no prior knowledge of robot size, marker configuration, or surface reflectance properties, and propagates the uncertainty introduced by surface irregularities, including those of dynamic water surfaces. We further characterize, analytically and experimentally, how surface tilt biases the estimate, and identify the slope range over which the level-surface assumption holds. Indoor multi-UAV and outdoor experiments validate the localization accuracy, demonstrating reliable operation across varying lighting conditions and greater effective range than state-of-the-art vision-based active-marker methods, with higher accuracy beyond 30 m. The project website with the video is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[EquiContact: A Hierarchical SE(3) Vision-to-Force Equivariant Framework for Spatially Generalizable Contact-rich Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.10961)**  `arXiv:2507.10961`  
  _Joohwan Seo, Arvind Kruthiventy, Soomi Lee, Megan Teng, Seoyeon Choi, Xiang Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents a framework for learning vision-based robotic policies for contact-rich manipulation tasks, which generalize spatially across task configurations.We propose EquiContact, a novel hierarchical framework composed of a high-level vision planner, such as a Diffusion Equivariant Descriptor Field (Diff-EDF) and a low-level compliant visuomotor policy, such as a Geometric Compliant Action Chunking Transformer (G-CompACT). G-CompACT, operating using localized observations from wrist-mounted RGB images, end-effector pose feedback in the form of a geometrically consistent error vector (GCEV) and force-torque readings, produces actions defined on the end-effector frame.We show that the entire EquiContact pipeline is $\SE$-equivariant under equivariant reference-frame estimation and left-invariant visual features, from perception to force control. Once the robot learns to execute a contact-rich task, such as inserting a peg-in-hole (PiH), it can execute similar tasks at different locations and orientations without retraining of the low-level policy. We also describe three key components for attaining spatially generalizable contact-rich policies: geometrically consistent compliance, the use of localized policies, and induced equivariance.Real-world peg-in-hole (PiH) experiments demonstrate near-perfect success rates under spatially out-of-distribution configurations, validating the proposed framework and principles, while additional screwing and surface-wiping experiments evaluate the transfer of the localized policy design beyond insertion. The experimental videos and more details can be found on the project website:this https URL
  </details>
